### Accurate and Fast Channel Estimation for Fluid Antenna Systems with Diffusion Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.04930v1)
- **Authors**: Erqiang Tang, Wei Guo, Hengtao He, Shenghui Song, Jun Zhang, Khaled B. Letaief
- **Abstract**: Fluid antenna systems (FAS) offer enhanced spatial diversity for next-generation wireless systems. However, acquiring accurate channel state information (CSI) remains challenging due to the large number of reconfigurable ports and the limited availability of radio-frequency (RF) chains -- particularly in high-dimensional FAS scenarios. To address this challenge, we propose an efficient posterior sampling-based channel estimator that leverages a diffusion model (DM) with a simplified U-Net architecture to capture the spatial correlation structure of two-dimensional FAS channels. The DM is initially trained offline in an unsupervised way and then applied online as a learned implicit prior to reconstruct CSI from partial observations via posterior sampling through a denoising diffusion restoration model (DDRM). To accelerate the online inference, we introduce a skipped sampling strategy that updates only a subset of latent variables during the sampling process, thereby reducing the computational cost with minimal accuracy degradation. Simulation results demonstrate that the proposed approach achieves significantly higher estimation accuracy and over 20x speedup compared to state-of-the-art compressed sensing-based methods, highlighting its potential for practical deployment in high-dimensional FAS.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

The paper proposes a novel channel estimation method for fluid antenna systems (FAS) using diffusion models (DMs). Recognizing the challenges of acquiring accurate CSI due to the high dimensionality and limited RF chains in FAS, the authors leverage DMs to capture the spatial correlation structure of 2D FAS channels.  A simplified U-Net architecture is used to build the DM, trained offline in an unsupervised manner. Online, this trained DM acts as a learned implicit prior within a denoising diffusion restoration model (DDRM) framework to reconstruct CSI from partial observations. To further accelerate online inference, a skipped sampling strategy is introduced, updating only a subset of latent variables.  Simulation results demonstrate significant improvements in estimation accuracy and speed compared to compressed sensing (CS)-based methods.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:** The application of diffusion models to the channel estimation problem in fluid antenna systems is a novel contribution. While DMs have seen use in other wireless communication tasks (e.g., MIMO channel estimation), the unique characteristics of FAS (especially the dense, reconfigurable antenna placement and resulting channel structure) justify a specialized approach. The design of a lightweight U-Net architecture tailored for FAS channels and the introduction of a skipped sampling strategy to accelerate online inference are also novel aspects of the work.  The idea of leveraging the structural similarity between 2D FAS channels and natural images (albeit simpler local patterns) to justify using a U-Net style network is a compelling argument.

* **Significance:** The problem of accurate and fast channel estimation in FAS is critical to realizing the potential benefits of this technology. The paper offers a promising solution to this challenge, demonstrating significant performance gains over existing CS-based approaches in terms of both accuracy and speed. The over 20x speedup is particularly significant, making real-time implementation more feasible, which is a major issue with current approaches. The results suggest that DMs can be effectively used as learned priors to represent complex FAS channel structures without needing strong assumptions on sparsity.

* **Strengths:**
    * **Strong performance:** The simulation results clearly demonstrate the superiority of the proposed method over the baselines.
    * **Computational efficiency:** The skipped sampling strategy significantly reduces computational cost without substantial loss in accuracy.
    * **Practical relevance:** The paper addresses a key challenge in FAS, making the work practically relevant.
    * **Sound methodology:** The combination of a DM-based prior with a DDRM-based posterior sampling approach is well-reasoned and effectively implemented.
    * **Clear presentation:**  The paper is well-written and the methodology is clearly explained.

* **Weaknesses:**
    * **Limited evaluation scope:** The evaluation is based only on simulations. The performance in a real-world environment with more complex channel characteristics and noise conditions needs to be assessed.
    * **Hyperparameter sensitivity:** The performance of DMs is often sensitive to hyperparameter tuning, including diffusion time, noise schedule, skipped sampling rate etc. The paper doesn't provide a detailed sensitivity analysis to these parameters. The authors merely say they set  {na = 0, b = 1, and nc = 1 to maximize the contribution from the observation y}. More details on how the authors chose these hyperparameters should be included.
    * **Complexity of DM training:** While the inference is sped up, the initial training of the diffusion model is computationally intensive and requires a large dataset. The cost of generating the training dataset in a real world environment (collecting the channel data) is not addressed.
    * **The lightweightedness of the U-Net architecture can be examined further:** The paper can evaluate the number of parameters compared to other methods.

* **Potential Impact:** The paper has the potential to influence the development of more practical and efficient channel estimation techniques for FAS. The demonstrated accuracy and speed improvements could lead to more widespread adoption of FAS in future wireless systems. Furthermore, the application of diffusion models for channel estimation could inspire further research in this area.

**Overall Score:** 7.5/10

**Justification:**

The paper presents a novel and well-executed approach to channel estimation in FAS using diffusion models. The performance gains, particularly the speedup, are significant.  However, the evaluation is limited to simulations, and the complexity of DM training as well as the hyperparameter sensitivity needs to be addressed. These issues need to be resolved before the work can be considered a true breakthrough. Therefore, the paper merits a score of 7.5, reflecting its important contribution while acknowledging the need for further validation and refinement.

- **Classification**: cs.IT
- **Score**: 7/10

### Prompt-Based LLMs for Position Bias-Aware Reranking in Personalized Recommendations
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.04948v1)
- **Authors**: Md Aminul Islam, Ahmed Sayeed Faruk
- **Abstract**: Recommender systems are essential for delivering personalized content across digital platforms by modeling user preferences and behaviors. Recently, large language models (LLMs) have been adopted for prompt-based recommendation due to their ability to generate personalized outputs without task-specific training. However, LLM-based methods face limitations such as limited context window size, inefficient pointwise and pairwise prompting, and difficulty handling listwise ranking due to token constraints. LLMs can also be sensitive to position bias, as they may overemphasize earlier items in the prompt regardless of their true relevance. To address and investigate these issues, we propose a hybrid framework that combines a traditional recommendation model with an LLM for reranking top-k items using structured prompts. We evaluate the effects of user history reordering and instructional prompts for mitigating position bias. Experiments on MovieLens-100K show that randomizing user history improves ranking quality, but LLM-based reranking does not outperform the base model. Explicit instructions to reduce position bias are also ineffective. Our evaluations reveal limitations in LLMs' ability to model ranking context and mitigate bias. Our code is publicly available at https://github.com/aminul7506/LLMForReRanking.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided research paper:

**Summary:**

The paper explores the potential of using Large Language Models (LLMs) for reranking recommendations generated by a traditional recommender system. The authors propose a hybrid framework combining a base recommender (LightGCN) with an LLM (GPT-40). They investigate strategies to mitigate position bias, a known issue where items appearing earlier in a list are more likely to be interacted with regardless of relevance. These strategies include randomizing the order of user history within the prompt and providing explicit instructions to the LLM to reduce position bias. The experiments, conducted on the MovieLens-100K dataset, show that LLM-based reranking doesn't improve upon the base model's performance. Randomizing user history improves ranking slightly, but explicit instructions to mitigate position bias are ineffective. The paper concludes that current LLMs have limitations in understanding ranking context and mitigating bias in recommendations, highlighting the need for more structured prompting or fine-tuning approaches.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The idea of using LLMs for recommendation is not entirely new, but the specific focus on reranking a pre-filtered list from a traditional model, coupled with the investigation of position bias mitigation strategies within the LLM context, adds a degree of novelty. The exploration of different prompting strategies targeted specifically towards mitigating position bias, such as randomizing history order and instruction-based mitigation, is a key contribution.

*   **Significance:** The significance lies in its empirical evaluation of LLMs for recommendation, specifically addressing a crucial problem: position bias. The findings are important because they demonstrate the limitations of naive applications of LLMs in this domain.  The negative results, though not always desirable, are valuable in guiding future research by showing what *doesn't* work. Highlighting the difficulty of simply instructing an LLM to overcome a well-established bias is significant.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Clear problem definition:** The paper clearly defines the problem of position bias in LLM-based recommendation.
    *   **Well-defined methodology:** The hybrid framework and prompting strategies are well-defined and easy to understand.
    *   **Empirical evaluation:** The experiments are conducted on a standard dataset (MovieLens-100K), allowing for comparison with other methods.
    *   **Analysis of results:** The authors provide a good analysis of the results, discussing the possible reasons for the limitations of LLMs in this context.
    *   **Reproducibility:** The availability of the code promotes reproducibility, facilitating the validation and extension of their findings.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited scope:** The experiments are limited to a single dataset (MovieLens-100K) and a specific LLM (GPT-40). While this provides a starting point, the generalizability of the findings to other datasets and LLMs is uncertain.  It's worth noting that GPT-40 is not a common designation, it's possible that GPT-4 was intended.
    *   **Shallow prompting exploration:** While they tested different prompting strategies, the prompt engineering seems relatively basic. Deeper dives into prompt optimization, chain-of-thought reasoning, or fine-tuning might have yielded better results.
    *   **Lack of comparison to other debiasing techniques:** While they attempt to address bias with prompting, the paper would have been stronger if it compared their approach to existing debiasing techniques used in traditional recommendation systems.
    *   **Incremental contribution:** The hybrid approach, while sensible, is not a fundamentally new architectural concept. The experiments around prompt engineering reveal the current limitations, but aren't groundbreaking themselves.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper's findings can influence the field by:
    *   Highlighting the challenges of using LLMs for recommendation and the need for more sophisticated approaches.
    *   Motivating research into better prompting strategies and fine-tuning methods for LLMs in recommendation.
    *   Encouraging the development of hybrid models that combine the strengths of traditional recommender systems and LLMs.

*   **Overall:** The paper makes a worthwhile contribution by empirically investigating the use of LLMs for recommendation and demonstrating the limitations of naive approaches in addressing position bias. However, the incremental nature of the contribution, limited scope of experimentation, and relatively shallow exploration of prompting strategies temper its overall impact.

**Score: 6**

**Rationale:** The paper is a solid piece of research with a clear methodology and analysis. Its merit lies in demonstrating the difficulties of applying LLMs directly to recommendation, particularly concerning position bias, rather than introducing a revolutionary approach. The code availability is a big plus. However, the somewhat limited experimentation (single dataset/LLM, relatively basic prompting) and the lack of comparison to existing debiasing methods hold it back from a higher score.  It provides valuable empirical insights, but isn't a groundbreaking advance in the field.

- **Classification**: cs.IR
- **Score**: 6/10

### Position: Epistemic Artificial Intelligence is Essential for Machine Learning Models to Know When They Do Not Know
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.04950v1)
- **Authors**: Shireen Kudukkil Manchingal, Fabio Cuzzolin
- **Abstract**: Despite the impressive achievements of AI, including advancements in generative models and large language models, there remains a significant gap in the ability of AI to handle uncertainty and generalize beyond the training data. We argue that AI models, especially in autonomous systems, fail to make robust predictions when faced with unfamiliar or adversarial data, as evidenced by incidents with autonomous vehicles. Traditional machine learning approaches struggle to address these issues due to an overemphasis on data fitting and domain adaptation. This position paper posits a paradigm shift towards epistemic artificial intelligence, emphasizing the need for models to learn not only from what they know but also from their ignorance. This approach, which focuses on recognizing and managing uncertainty, offers a potential solution to improve the resilience and robustness of AI systems, ensuring that they can better handle unpredictable real-world environments.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper advocates for a paradigm shift in AI, termed "Epistemic Artificial Intelligence" (EAI), which emphasizes learning from ignorance rather than solely from data. It argues that current AI systems, particularly in autonomous systems, lack robustness due to their inability to handle uncertainty and generalize beyond training data. EAI focuses on recognizing and managing epistemic (reducible) uncertainty, suggesting that AI models should explicitly represent what they *don't* know. The paper explores the theoretical foundations, benefits, and potential applications of EAI in various domains, including supervised learning, unsupervised learning, reinforcement learning, generative AI, and large language models. It highlights the limitations of existing methods like Bayesian approaches and deep ensembles in capturing epistemic uncertainty and proposes the use of second-order uncertainty measures like credal sets and random sets. The authors discuss challenges in scaling up EAI and integrating it with symbolic reasoning and continuous learning. Finally, the paper outlines opportunities for EAI in science and engineering, specifically in climate change prediction and pandemic modeling.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core idea of emphasizing epistemic awareness in AI is not entirely new. The notion that AI systems need to be able to express 'I don't know' has been a topic of discussion. However, the paper provides a focused and comprehensive articulation of the EAI concept, connecting it explicitly to the philosophical principle of "knowing that you do not know," and it emphasizes second-order uncertainty measures. The explicit framing of a distinct field dedicated to Epistemic AI and the breadth of coverage in the paper represents a step forward. The use of imprecise probabilities (credal sets, etc.) to represent this ignorance has been suggested before, but the paper makes a strong case for its renewed emphasis.

*   **Significance:** The potential significance of EAI is considerable. Improving AI robustness and handling uncertainty are critical for deploying AI systems in safety-critical domains and for addressing complex real-world problems. The focus on epistemic uncertainty is crucial for making AI systems more reliable and trustworthy. If EAI can lead to models that are better calibrated, more robust to adversarial attacks, and more capable of generalizing to novel situations, it would be a significant advancement. The discussion of potential applications in climate change prediction and pandemic modeling is particularly compelling, given the immense societal impact of these challenges.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Clear Articulation:** The paper provides a clear and concise definition of EAI and its key principles.
    *   **Comprehensive Overview:** It offers a broad overview of existing uncertainty quantification methods and their limitations.
    *   **Practical Examples:** The paper provides practical examples of how EAI can be applied in various domains.
    *   **Future Directions:** It outlines key challenges and opportunities for future research in EAI.
    *   **Unified Framework:** The paper attempts to bring together a range of techniques under a common umbrella term.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Lack of Concrete Results:** While the paper discusses the theoretical benefits of EAI, it lacks substantial empirical results demonstrating the superiority of EAI-based models in real-world applications. The paper makes some claims that more recent work (Manchingal et al., Caprio et al., etc) have "demonstrated superior performance," but the paper would be stronger by including more details about these specific results and analysis.
    *   **Scaling Challenges:** The paper acknowledges the challenges in scaling up EAI methods, particularly those based on imprecise probabilities. However, it does not provide concrete solutions to address these challenges.
    *   **Limited Scope:** The paper primarily focuses on a specific view of epistemic uncertainty, largely based on imprecise probabilities. While credal sets and belief functions are effective, other approaches that might represent other facets of ignorance (e.g., related to causality or model mis-specification) are given less attention.
    *   **Specificity of Solutions:** Some of the proposed solutions, such as hierarchical embeddings or new loss functions, seem specific and might be more suitable to the application than the high-level concept of epistemic AI.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence the field by providing a new perspective on AI development and highlighting the importance of uncertainty awareness. It could stimulate research in areas such as second-order uncertainty measures, robust AI, and neurosymbolic reasoning. It encourages a shift from a pure data-fitting mindset towards more robust and uncertainty-aware models.

**Justification for the Score:**

While the paper presents a compelling vision for the future of AI, its lack of substantial empirical validation limits its impact. The ideas discussed offer a valuable direction for future research, but the practical challenges need to be addressed before EAI can become a widely adopted paradigm. While recent work (Manchingal et al., Caprio et al) is cited as demonstrating superior performance, more detail and a critical assessment of the specific results and their limitations would improve the paper. This is also a fairly high-level position paper that outlines research directions, so while it is potentially influential, it's hard to see its specific short-term effects.

Score: 6.5

- **Classification**: cs.AI
- **Score**: 6/10

### Chain-of-Thought Tokens are Computer Program Variables
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.04955v1)
- **Authors**: Fangwei Zhu, Peiyi Wang, Zhifang Sui
- **Abstract**: Chain-of-thoughts (CoT) requires large language models (LLMs) to generate intermediate steps before reaching the final answer, and has been proven effective to help LLMs solve complex reasoning tasks. However, the inner mechanism of CoT still remains largely unclear. In this paper, we empirically study the role of CoT tokens in LLMs on two compositional tasks: multi-digit multiplication and dynamic programming. While CoT is essential for solving these problems, we find that preserving only tokens that store intermediate results would achieve comparable performance. Furthermore, we observe that storing intermediate results in an alternative latent form will not affect model performance. We also randomly intervene some values in CoT, and notice that subsequent CoT tokens and the final answer would change correspondingly. These findings suggest that CoT tokens may function like variables in computer programs but with potential drawbacks like unintended shortcuts and computational complexity limits between tokens. The code and data are available at https://github.com/solitaryzero/CoTs_are_Variables.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper investigates the inner workings of Chain-of-Thought (CoT) prompting in Large Language Models (LLMs).  The central hypothesis is that CoT tokens function similarly to variables in computer programs, storing intermediate values used in subsequent computations.  The authors empirically tested this on multi-digit multiplication and dynamic programming tasks.  They find that only tokens storing intermediate results are crucial, that these results can be represented in different (latent) forms without significant performance degradation, and that intervening on these tokens causally affects the final answer. They also highlight the existence of a computational complexity limit to how compressed the intermediate values can be.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper presents a compelling analogy between CoT tokens and program variables. While the observation that CoT enhances reasoning is well-established, the specific investigation into the *role* of individual tokens as stores of intermediate values is a useful perspective. The experiments involving removing non-result tokens and representing results in latent spaces are insightful and provide empirical support for the central hypothesis. However, the intervention experiments, while confirming causality, are not particularly surprising. Similar causal interventions have been explored in other LLM research.

*   **Significance:**  Understanding how CoT works is crucial for designing more efficient and effective prompting strategies.  If CoT tokens are indeed acting like variables, this could lead to improved methods for generating, compressing, or even manipulating the reasoning process in LLMs. The insights into computational complexity limitations are also valuable, as they suggest boundaries to how much reasoning can be packed into a given sequence length.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   The paper clearly articulates a hypothesis and designs experiments to test it.
    *   The empirical results, particularly regarding token removal and latent representations, are compelling.
    *   The paper connects its findings to relevant theoretical frameworks (e.g., circuit complexity).
    *   The study utilizes a systematic approach across two compositional tasks, strengthening the generalizability of the findings.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The causality argument, while strengthened by the intervention experiments, isn't entirely groundbreaking. Previous work has explored causality in LLMs.
    *   The limitations are clearly stated (synthetic tasks, single model), but these limitations constrain the scope and impact of the findings. Applying these techniques to larger, more complex models and real-world datasets would significantly increase the paper's significance.
    *   The discussion of "shortcuts" learned by the model is interesting, but not fully fleshed out. A more detailed analysis of these shortcuts could provide further insights into the model's reasoning process.
    *   The paper uses Qwen-2.5-1.5B model for analysis. While this can be a good starting point, the impact of their research could be more compelling if they showcased the work on larger, state-of-the-art LLMs.

*   **Potential Influence:** The "CoT as Variables" analogy offers a fresh perspective on CoT prompting and could inspire future work on:
    *   Developing more efficient CoT strategies by focusing on explicitly storing and manipulating intermediate results.
    *   Designing methods for intervening on CoT reasoning to correct errors or guide the model toward better solutions.
    *   Investigating the role of individual layers in the reasoning process (as suggested by the probing experiments).
    *   Better understanding the computational limits of compressed reasoning.

*   **Rigorous Rationale for Score:**

The paper provides a useful contribution to the understanding of CoT prompting. However, the empirical evaluation is on relatively simple synthetic tasks and the theoretical novelty is limited. The causal intervention experiments, while confirming the hypothesis, are not particularly groundbreaking. This limits the potential impact of the paper. While the findings are interesting and offer a valuable perspective, their scope and generalizability are constrained by the current limitations.

Score: 6

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 6/10

### Graffe: Graph Representation Learning via Diffusion Probabilistic Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.04956v1)
- **Authors**: Dingshuo Chen, Shuchen Xue, Liuji Chen, Yingheng Wang, Qiang Liu, Shu Wu, Zhi-Ming Ma, Liang Wang
- **Abstract**: Diffusion probabilistic models (DPMs), widely recognized for their potential to generate high-quality samples, tend to go unnoticed in representation learning. While recent progress has highlighted their potential for capturing visual semantics, adapting DPMs to graph representation learning remains in its infancy. In this paper, we introduce Graffe, a self-supervised diffusion model proposed for graph representation learning. It features a graph encoder that distills a source graph into a compact representation, which, in turn, serves as the condition to guide the denoising process of the diffusion decoder. To evaluate the effectiveness of our model, we first explore the theoretical foundations of applying diffusion models to representation learning, proving that the denoising objective implicitly maximizes the conditional mutual information between data and its representation. Specifically, we prove that the negative logarithm of the denoising score matching loss is a tractable lower bound for the conditional mutual information. Empirically, we conduct a series of case studies to validate our theoretical insights. In addition, Graffe delivers competitive results under the linear probing setting on node and graph classification tasks, achieving state-of-the-art performance on 9 of the 11 real-world datasets. These findings indicate that powerful generative models, especially diffusion models, serve as an effective tool for graph representation learning.
- **Summary**: This paper introduces Graffe, a self-supervised graph representation learning framework based on diffusion probabilistic models (DPMs). Graffe employs a graph encoder to distill a source graph into a compact representation, which then conditions a diffusion decoder during its denoising process. The paper theoretically demonstrates that the denoising objective implicitly maximizes the conditional mutual information between the data and its representation. Empirically, Graffe achieves competitive results in node and graph classification tasks, attaining state-of-the-art performance on several real-world datasets.

**Rigorous and Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies in its application of diffusion models to graph representation learning and its theoretical analysis of the denoising objective within this context. While DPMs have been explored for visual semantics, their application to graph data, particularly with a strong theoretical grounding linking it to InfoMax principles, is a significant contribution. The Diff-InfoMax principle, extending the standard InfoMax, and its application in graph representation learning appears to be a key original idea.

*   **Significance:** The paper demonstrates that DPMs, typically associated with generative tasks, can be effectively adapted for discriminative representation learning in graphs. The theoretical analysis provides a strong justification for why this approach works and offers insights into the role of information content in the denoising process. The empirical results validate the effectiveness of Graffe, showcasing its competitive or state-of-the-art performance on a range of graph learning tasks. This suggests that leveraging DPMs opens promising new avenues for research in graph representation learning. The rigorous proofs provided give solid foundations to the findings.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Strong Theoretical Foundation:** The paper offers a solid theoretical justification for its approach, linking the denoising objective to conditional mutual information and introducing the Diff-InfoMax principle. The theorem proves the negative log likelihood of the denoising score as a lower bound on the conditional mutual information, which is crucial to give a solid theoretical backing to their methodology.
    *   **Extensive Empirical Validation:** The paper presents comprehensive experimental results on a diverse set of datasets, demonstrating the effectiveness of Graffe in both node and graph classification tasks. This wide range of test settings greatly strengthens confidence in the findings.
    *   **Clear and Well-Structured Presentation:** The paper is well-written and organized, making it easy to understand the proposed approach and its theoretical underpinnings.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Computational Cost:** Although not explicitly mentioned, DPMs can be computationally expensive to train, potentially limiting the scalability of Graffe to very large graphs. This should have been mentioned and explored.
    *   **Encoder Choice:** While the paper justifies the use of GAT and GIN as encoders, there is limited exploration of alternative encoder architectures and their impact on performance. It could have mentioned and explored a different encoder.
    *   **Limited Ablation Analysis:** While the ablation studies assess the impact of masking and decoder choices, a more comprehensive ablation analysis could further elucidate the contribution of different components of Graffe.

*   **Potential Influence:** Graffe's novel combination of DPMs and graph representation learning is likely to influence future research in this area. The Diff-InfoMax principle could provide a theoretical foundation for other self-supervised learning approaches. The code release will also facilitate adoption and further exploration of this method.

Despite some minor limitations, the paper presents a significant contribution to the field of graph representation learning. The novel application of diffusion models, the rigorous theoretical analysis, and the compelling empirical results make this a highly valuable contribution.

Score: 8

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 8/10

### Learning Item Representations Directly from Multimodal Features for Effective Recommendation
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.04960v1)
- **Authors**: Xin Zhou, Xiaoxiong Zhang, Dusit Niyato, Zhiqi Shen
- **Abstract**: Conventional multimodal recommender systems predominantly leverage Bayesian Personalized Ranking (BPR) optimization to learn item representations by amalgamating item identity (ID) embeddings with multimodal features. Nevertheless, our empirical and theoretical findings unequivocally demonstrate a pronounced optimization gradient bias in favor of acquiring representations from multimodal features over item ID embeddings. As a consequence, item ID embeddings frequently exhibit suboptimal characteristics despite the convergence of multimodal feature parameters. Given the rich informational content inherent in multimodal features, in this paper, we propose a novel model (i.e., LIRDRec) that learns item representations directly from these features to augment recommendation performance. Recognizing that features derived from each modality may capture disparate yet correlated aspects of items, we propose a multimodal transformation mechanism, integrated with modality-specific encoders, to effectively fuse features from all modalities. Moreover, to differentiate the influence of diverse modality types, we devise a progressive weight copying fusion module within LIRDRec. This module incrementally learns the weight assigned to each modality in synthesizing the final user or item representations. Finally, we utilize the powerful visual understanding of Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) to convert the item images into texts and extract semantics embeddings upon the texts via LLMs. Empirical evaluations conducted on five real-world datasets validate the superiority of our approach relative to competing baselines. It is worth noting the proposed model, equipped with embeddings extracted from MLLMs and LLMs, can further improve the recommendation accuracy of NDCG@20 by an average of 4.21% compared to the original embeddings.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper addresses a critical limitation in conventional multimodal recommender systems (MMRSs): the optimization bias towards multimodal features at the expense of item ID embeddings. The authors argue that this bias hinders the learning of optimal item representations. To tackle this, they introduce LIRDRec (Learns Item Representations Directly from Multimodal Features for Recommendation), a novel model that directly learns item representations from multimodal features, without relying on item ID embeddings. LIRDRec incorporates a multimodal transformation mechanism to fuse features from different modalities effectively and a progressive weight copying module to differentiate the influence of various modalities. Additionally, the model leverages multimodal large language models (MLLMs) to extract semantics from item images. Extensive experiments on real-world datasets demonstrate that LIRDRec surpasses existing baselines and shows improved performance in cold-start scenarios. A theoretical analysis supports the empirical findings.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core idea of directly learning item representations from multimodal features, bypassing item ID embeddings, constitutes the paper's primary novelty. This addresses a well-identified limitation in existing MMRSs and opens avenues for improving recommendation accuracy. The progressive weight copying mechanism and the multimodal transformation mechanism further enhance the model's ability to capture intricate relationships between different modalities.
*   **Significance:** The paper's significance stems from its ability to demonstrably improve recommendation accuracy, especially in cold-start situations. The theoretical analysis provides a strong foundation for the empirical observations, adding to the credibility of the proposed approach. The approach of using powerful MLLMs, if generalizable, adds to potential influence.
*   **Strengths:**
    *   Clear Problem Statement: The paper clearly articulates the problem of optimization gradient bias in MMRSs.
    *   Strong Theoretical Analysis: The theoretical analysis strengthens the validity of the proposed approach.
    *   Comprehensive Experiments: The extensive experiments on diverse datasets provide substantial evidence of LIRDRec's effectiveness and superiority.
    *   Code availability: The public code ensures that other researchers can easily reproduce and build upon the work.
    *   Insightful Ablation Studies: The ablation studies provide detailed insights into the relative importance of individual components of the proposed model.
*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   Complexity: While the design choices seem well motivated, the combination of DCT, DNN layers for uni-modal projection, the DNN within progressive weighting, the GCN layers, the specific residual connections and summation with the initial feature X results in a fair number of layers and components. The model has many moving parts whose sensitivity can be difficult to disentangle.
    *   The model could be more concise and conceptually simpler.
    *   The paper doesn't deeply explore the computational cost compared to other approaches (the appendix only presents asymptotic analysis, not running times). If some approaches are much faster to run and obtain reasonably good results, that should be mentioned as a point against LIRDRec.
    *   Limited discussion on MLLM-based features. While MLLMs and LLMs further enhance performance, there could have been a deeper exploration of different prompting strategies or model choices for the MLLM/LLM components. There should be an evaluation of how robust is this part of the pipeline given the potential of the generative model to hallucinate attributes unrelated to the item.
    *   The improvements over existing approaches while significant are still in single-digit percentages. While significant on certain datasets, the gains seem less pronounced on others.
*   **Potential Influence:** LIRDRec has the potential to influence future research in MMRSs by promoting the exploration of alternative architectures that directly learn representations from multimodal features, rather than relying on item ID embeddings. The progressive weight copying technique could also be adopted in other multimodal learning tasks.

Overall, the paper presents a well-motivated, theoretically grounded, and experimentally validated approach to learning item representations in multimodal recommender systems. While there are areas for improvement, such as addressing the model's complexity and computational cost in more detail, the paper makes a valuable contribution to the field.

Score: 7.5

- **Classification**: cs.IR
- **Score**: 7/10

### DenseGrounding: Improving Dense Language-Vision Semantics for Ego-Centric 3D Visual Grounding
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.04965v1)
- **Authors**: Henry Zheng, Hao Shi, Qihang Peng, Yong Xien Chng, Rui Huang, Yepeng Weng, Zhongchao Shi, Gao Huang
- **Abstract**: Enabling intelligent agents to comprehend and interact with 3D environments through natural language is crucial for advancing robotics and human-computer interaction. A fundamental task in this field is ego-centric 3D visual grounding, where agents locate target objects in real-world 3D spaces based on verbal descriptions. However, this task faces two significant challenges: (1) loss of fine-grained visual semantics due to sparse fusion of point clouds with ego-centric multi-view images, (2) limited textual semantic context due to arbitrary language descriptions. We propose DenseGrounding, a novel approach designed to address these issues by enhancing both visual and textual semantics. For visual features, we introduce the Hierarchical Scene Semantic Enhancer, which retains dense semantics by capturing fine-grained global scene features and facilitating cross-modal alignment. For text descriptions, we propose a Language Semantic Enhancer that leverages large language models to provide rich context and diverse language descriptions with additional context during model training. Extensive experiments show that DenseGrounding significantly outperforms existing methods in overall accuracy, with improvements of 5.81% and 7.56% when trained on the comprehensive full dataset and smaller mini subset, respectively, further advancing the SOTA in egocentric 3D visual grounding. Our method also achieves 1st place and receives the Innovation Award in the CVPR 2024 Autonomous Grand Challenge Multi-view 3D Visual Grounding Track, validating its effectiveness and robustness.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper "DenseGrounding: Improving Dense Language-Vision Semantics for Ego-Centric 3D Visual Grounding" addresses challenges in ego-centric 3D visual grounding, specifically the loss of fine-grained visual semantics and limited textual context. It proposes DenseGrounding, a novel approach featuring a Hierarchical Scene Semantic Enhancer (HSSE) to retain dense visual semantics and a Language Semantic Enhancer (LSE) that leverages large language models to provide rich contextual information for text descriptions. Experiments demonstrate significant improvements over existing methods on the EmbodiedScan dataset. The method secured first place in the CVPR 2024 Autonomous Grand Challenge Multi-view 3D Visual Grounding Track.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper presents a reasonable amount of novelty, particularly in the integration of the HSSE and LSE modules. The HSSE effectively addresses the visual semantic loss issue by hierarchically aggregating features and broadcasting enriched semantics to the point cloud. The LSE, utilizing LLMs to augment text descriptions with contextual information from a constructed scene database, is also novel. The improvement over standard techniques like simply concatenating related descriptions is non-trivial.

*   **Significance:** The significance of the work lies in its ability to improve the accuracy of 3D visual grounding, which has direct applications in robotics and human-computer interaction. The improvement over the previous SOTA on the EmbodiedScan dataset is notable (5.81% on the full dataset). Furthermore, the first-place achievement in the CVPR 2024 challenge adds credibility. The ablation studies provide useful insights into the contribution of each component.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   Addresses a well-defined problem with clear challenges.
    *   Proposes a novel architecture (DenseGrounding) with two key modules (HSSE and LSE).
    *   Demonstrates significant performance improvements on a standard benchmark.
    *   Includes comprehensive experiments and ablation studies.
    *   Won a prestigious challenge, showing real-world applicability.
    *   The use of a strong baseline, EmbodiedScan, makes the comparison more meaningful.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The paper focuses on a specific dataset (EmbodiedScan) and it might be important to know how well it generalizes to other datasets. A cross-dataset experiment is provided in Table 6, and the increase over the baseline is impressive; however, the absolute performance is relatively low.
    *   The improvement is mainly demonstrated on the validation set. More results on the held-out test set would further strengthen the claims.
    *   While the LSE uses LLMs, the approach might still be limited by the quality and availability of data in the Scene Information Database (SIDB). The dependence on the SIDB could be a limitation, especially for new environments.
    *   Qualitative results, while showing an improvement, are limited and do not provide a thorough understanding of failure cases.
    *   The paper doesn't address the computational cost of the proposed method in detail. How the addition of the HSSE and LSE modules affects inference time/resources is unclear.
    * The description of the enhanced baseline is quite minimal (Appendix A.1.1) given that it is a strong baseline in comparison. More detail would add to the completeness of the work.

*   **Potential Impact:**
    The paper has the potential to influence future research in 3D visual grounding and embodied AI. The HSSE and LSE modules provide useful building blocks for improving the accuracy and robustness of perception systems. The work also highlights the importance of leveraging external knowledge and improving cross-modal interactions.

**Justification of Score:**

Considering the novelty of the approach, the significant performance gains on the EmbodiedScan dataset, and the success in the CVPR challenge, the paper demonstrates a valuable contribution to the field. However, the limited qualitative results and the need for greater insight into the generalization capabilities and computational overhead prevents it from achieving a higher score.

### ReAlign: Bilingual Text-to-Motion Generation via Step-Aware Reward-Guided Alignment
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.04974v1)
- **Authors**: Wanjiang Weng, Xiaofeng Tan, Hongsong Wang, Pan Zhou
- **Abstract**: Bilingual text-to-motion generation, which synthesizes 3D human motions from bilingual text inputs, holds immense potential for cross-linguistic applications in gaming, film, and robotics. However, this task faces critical challenges: the absence of bilingual motion-language datasets and the misalignment between text and motion distributions in diffusion models, leading to semantically inconsistent or low-quality motions. To address these challenges, we propose BiHumanML3D, a novel bilingual human motion dataset, which establishes a crucial benchmark for bilingual text-to-motion generation models. Furthermore, we propose a Bilingual Motion Diffusion model (BiMD), which leverages cross-lingual aligned representations to capture semantics, thereby achieving a unified bilingual model. Building upon this, we propose Reward-guided sampling Alignment (ReAlign) method, comprising a step-aware reward model to assess alignment quality during sampling and a reward-guided strategy that directs the diffusion process toward an optimally aligned distribution. This reward model integrates step-aware tokens and combines a text-aligned module for semantic consistency and a motion-aligned module for realism, refining noisy motions at each timestep to balance probability density and alignment. Experiments demonstrate that our approach significantly improves text-motion alignment and motion quality compared to existing state-of-the-art methods. Project page: https://wengwanjiang.github.io/ReAlign-page/.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper "ReAlign: Bilingual Text-to-Motion Generation via Step-Aware Reward-Guided Alignment" introduces a new approach to generating 3D human motions from bilingual text descriptions (English and Chinese). The paper addresses two key challenges: the lack of bilingual text-motion datasets and the misalignment between text and motion distributions in diffusion models.  To tackle these issues, the authors: 1) introduce BiHumanML3D, a new bilingual human motion dataset; 2) propose BiMD, a Bilingual Motion Diffusion model that leverages cross-lingual alignment to capture semantics; and 3) develop ReAlign, a Reward-guided sampling Alignment method that comprises a step-aware reward model to assess alignment quality during sampling, guiding the diffusion process toward an optimally aligned distribution. The step-aware reward combines text-aligned and motion-aligned modules, refining noisy motions at each timestep. Experiments demonstrate improvements in text-motion alignment and motion quality compared to existing methods.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper has several novel contributions:
    *   **BiHumanML3D Dataset:** Creating a bilingual text-to-motion dataset is itself a significant contribution given the scarcity of such resources. This opens up new avenues for research in cross-lingual motion generation.
    *   **BiMD Model:** Developing a bilingual motion diffusion model that leverages cross-lingual alignment is a logical and effective approach. Using cross-lingual embeddings is not novel in itself, but its specific application in text-to-motion generation with a bilingual context is new.
    *   **ReAlign:** The reward-guided alignment strategy, particularly the step-aware reward model, is a key innovation. Decomposing the reward into text-aligned and motion-aligned components and integrating timestep information is an important improvement over simply fine-tuning the diffusion model using reinforcement learning. The "plug-and-play" nature of ReAlign is valuable.

*   **Significance:**
    *   **Addressing a Gap:** The paper addresses a significant gap in the field of text-to-motion generation, which has primarily focused on monolingual English datasets.
    *   **Improved Alignment:** The ReAlign method demonstrates a clear improvement in text-motion alignment, which is a persistent challenge in diffusion-based models. This translates to more coherent and semantically accurate motions.
    *   **Potential Impact:** The paper has the potential to impact cross-linguistic applications in areas such as gaming, filmmaking, and robotics, making motion synthesis more accessible and inclusive.
    *   **Strong Empirical Results:** The experiments are comprehensive, demonstrating the effectiveness of the proposed method on both the new BiHumanML3D dataset and existing datasets. Ablation studies provide insights into the contribution of each component.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   Clear problem definition and motivation.
    *   Well-designed bilingual dataset construction pipeline.
    *   Innovative and effective ReAlign method.
    *   Comprehensive experiments and analysis.
    *   Plug-and-play approach, allowing for easy integration with existing methods.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   Dependence on CLIP and Pre-trained Models: Like many current approaches, it relies heavily on pre-trained language models like CLIP, potentially inheriting biases and limitations. While mitigating some effects via finetuning, inherent constraints may apply.
    *   Computational Cost: While the paper addresses it well, applying diffusion models can be slow. Though addressed via timestep-aware adjustments, practical applications may require further refinement, like MotionLCM.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper is likely to stimulate further research in bilingual and multilingual text-to-motion generation. The ReAlign method could be adapted for other modalities and generative tasks. The BiHumanML3D dataset will be a valuable resource for the community.

*   **Justification of Score:** While the individual components are built upon existing research, the paper's combination of a new bilingual dataset, cross-lingual aligned diffusion model, and step-aware reward-guided alignment makes a noteworthy contribution to the field. It addresses an under-explored problem with a well-designed solution and strong empirical validation.

### ChainMarks: Securing DNN Watermark with Cryptographic Chain
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.04977v1)
- **Authors**: Brian Choi, Shu Wang, Isabelle Choi, Kun Sun
- **Abstract**: With the widespread deployment of deep neural network (DNN) models, dynamic watermarking techniques are being used to protect the intellectual property of model owners. However, recent studies have shown that existing watermarking schemes are vulnerable to watermark removal and ambiguity attacks. Besides, the vague criteria for determining watermark presence further increase the likelihood of such attacks. In this paper, we propose a secure DNN watermarking scheme named ChainMarks, which generates secure and robust watermarks by introducing a cryptographic chain into the trigger inputs and utilizes a two-phase Monte Carlo method for determining watermark presence. First, ChainMarks generates trigger inputs as a watermark dataset by repeatedly applying a hash function over a secret key, where the target labels associated with trigger inputs are generated from the digital signature of model owner. Then, the watermarked model is produced by training a DNN over both the original and watermark datasets. To verify watermarks, we compare the predicted labels of trigger inputs with the target labels and determine ownership with a more accurate decision threshold that considers the classification probability of specific models. Experimental results show that ChainMarks exhibits higher levels of robustness and security compared to state-of-the-art watermarking schemes. With a better marginal utility, ChainMarks provides a higher probability guarantee of watermark presence in DNN models with the same level of watermark accuracy.
- **Summary**: Okay, I'll provide a summary and critical evaluation of the "ChainMarks: Securing DNN Watermark with Cryptographic Chain" paper.

**Summary:**

The paper introduces ChainMarks, a new dynamic watermarking scheme for deep neural networks (DNNs) designed to be more secure and robust against watermark removal and ambiguity attacks. ChainMarks embeds watermarks by creating a cryptographic chain of trigger inputs, where each input is derived from the previous one using a hash function and the target labels are derived from the model owner's digital signature.  This chain establishes a cryptographic relationship between trigger inputs and labels, making it harder for attackers to forge alternative watermarks or remove existing ones.  The watermark's presence is verified using a two-phase Monte Carlo method to determine a more accurate decision threshold, considering both model classification probabilities and watermark generation properties. The authors evaluate ChainMarks against state-of-the-art watermarking schemes and various attack types, demonstrating its superior robustness and security.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's primary novelty lies in the integration of a cryptographic chain into the trigger inputs of a DNN watermarking scheme. Existing watermarking methods often rely on statistical properties or simple correlations between triggers and labels, making them vulnerable to adversarial attacks that exploit these weaknesses. The introduction of a cryptographic chain establishes a more secure and verifiable link, making it more difficult for attackers to forge or remove watermarks without knowledge of the secret key and digital signature. The Two-Phase Monte Carlo estimation method for a better watermark detection is a valuable contribution as well.

*   **Significance:** DNN watermarking is an important area to protect intellectual property and secure DNN models. The paper addresses a crucial limitation of existing watermarking schemes: their vulnerability to ambiguity attacks where adversaries create counterfeit watermarks. By making watermark forgery harder, ChainMarks offers a potentially more practical watermarking solution for real-world deployment.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Cryptographic Security:** The cryptographic chain provides a strong security guarantee that traditional watermarking methods lack.
    *   **Robustness:** The experiments show that ChainMarks is robust against various watermark removal attacks, including input preprocessing, model modification, and model extraction.
    *   **Effective Watermark Verification:** The two-phase Monte Carlo method offers a more accurate decision threshold for watermark presence, mitigating the risk of false positives or negatives.
    *   **Comprehensive Evaluation:** The paper includes a comprehensive evaluation against state-of-the-art watermarking schemes and various attack types.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Computational Overhead:** The two-phase Monte Carlo method for watermark verification could introduce computational overhead, especially for large models and datasets. The paper provides insufficient details on the computational cost of the method.
    *   **Scalability:** While the paper discusses how to extend ChainMarks to larger datasets like ImageNet, the experimental evaluation is limited to CIFAR-10 and CIFAR-100. The effectiveness and scalability of ChainMarks in more complex scenarios require further investigation.
    *   **Practical Considerations:** The paper assumes that the model owner has access to the original dataset, which may not always be feasible in practice.

*   **Impact:** ChainMarks has the potential to influence the design of future DNN watermarking schemes by highlighting the importance of cryptographic techniques in securing watermarks against adversarial attacks. The proposed techniques can offer greater confidence in model ownership.

*   **Justification of Score:**

    While the paper presents a significant improvement over existing watermarking schemes in terms of security, some limitations need to be addressed.  Specifically, the computational overhead and scalability of ChainMarks should be further investigated. Also, while robust in the experiments, further real-world evaluations would be important.

### Latent Preference Coding: Aligning Large Language Models via Discrete Latent Codes
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.04993v1)
- **Authors**: Zhuocheng Gong, Jian Guan, Wei Wu, Huishuai Zhang, Dongyan Zhao
- **Abstract**: Large language models (LLMs) have achieved remarkable success, yet aligning their generations with human preferences remains a critical challenge. Existing approaches to preference modeling often rely on an explicit or implicit reward function, overlooking the intricate and multifaceted nature of human preferences that may encompass conflicting factors across diverse tasks and populations. To address this limitation, we introduce Latent Preference Coding (LPC), a novel framework that models the implicit factors as well as their combinations behind holistic preferences using discrete latent codes. LPC seamlessly integrates with various offline alignment algorithms, automatically inferring the underlying factors and their importance from data without relying on pre-defined reward functions and hand-crafted combination weights. Extensive experiments on multiple benchmarks demonstrate that LPC consistently improves upon three alignment algorithms (DPO, SimPO, and IPO) using three base models (Mistral-7B, Llama3-8B, and Llama3-8B-Instruct). Furthermore, deeper analysis reveals that the learned latent codes effectively capture the differences in the distribution of human preferences and significantly enhance the robustness of alignment against noise in data. By providing a unified representation for the multifarious preference factors, LPC paves the way towards developing more robust and versatile alignment techniques for the responsible deployment of powerful LLMs.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper, along with a novelty/significance score:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces Latent Preference Coding (LPC), a novel framework for aligning large language models (LLMs) with human preferences. LPC uses discrete latent codes to represent the multifaceted factors influencing human preferences, thereby moving beyond the single reward function approach commonly used in alignment methods.  LPC integrates with existing offline alignment algorithms like DPO, SimPO, and IPO. The method is evaluated on various benchmarks using Mistral-7B and Llama3-8B models, demonstrating improved performance compared to baselines. Further analysis shows that the learned latent codes capture the distribution of human preferences and improve robustness against noisy data.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Addresses a significant problem:** The paper directly addresses the limitation of current preference modeling techniques that struggle to capture the complexity and multi-faceted nature of human preferences. The observation that human preferences depend on multiple, sometimes conflicting, factors is well-articulated and motivating.
    *   **Novel Approach:** The use of discrete latent codes to represent underlying preference factors is a significant contribution. This allows the model to learn implicit factors without pre-defined reward functions or handcrafted weights. The variational inference framework enables automatic learning of these representations.
    *   **Broad Applicability:** The framework's seamless integration with multiple existing offline RLHF algorithms (DPO, SimPO, IPO) demonstrates its versatility and practical value. The experimental section showcases this integration clearly.
    *   **Strong Empirical Results:**  The consistent improvement over baseline algorithms across different models and benchmarks provides convincing evidence for the effectiveness of LPC.  The analysis of the learned latent codes, particularly regarding their ability to capture differences in preference distributions and robustness to label noise, adds further weight to the findings. The visualization of the T-SNE features is also compelling.
    *   **Detailed evaluation:** A wide variety of tasks and evaluation techniques are used to validate the effectiveness of LPC.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Complexity and Implementation:**  While the paper argues that LPC introduces negligible additional computational cost, the architecture does have added complexity, and real-world application may prove challenging depending on the use case.
    *   **Limited Scope of Experimentation:** While the paper conducts extensive experiments, the tasks are still limited to those included in the UltraFeedback dataset and standard downstream benchmarks. The impact of LPC on more complex or subjective tasks could be further explored. The dependence of LPC performance to label noise can be further explored.

*   **Novelty and Significance:**

The use of *discrete* latent variables to model human preference *factors* within LLMs alignment is the main contribution of this paper. Additionally, the seamless integration with common RLHF models and the comprehensive analysis of the latent code representation are noteworthy.
However, it can be argued the latent variable approach to preference modeling is not entirely novel, as other recent works attempt similar methods (though typically using continuous latents) *concurrently* -- though this paper introduces interpretable latent code representations and showcases superior robustness.

**Justification:**

The paper presents a genuinely novel approach to addressing a significant bottleneck in LLM alignment. It builds upon existing methods but significantly improves them through the incorporation of latent preference coding.  The comprehensive experiments and analyses demonstrate the effectiveness of the approach across multiple settings, while addressing concerns in a more robust approach compared to prior arts.

### Rethinking Invariance in In-context Learning
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.04994v1)
- **Authors**: Lizhe Fang, Yifei Wang, Khashayar Gatmiry, Lei Fang, Yisen Wang
- **Abstract**: In-Context Learning (ICL) has emerged as a pivotal capability of auto-regressive large language models, yet it is hindered by a notable sensitivity to the ordering of context examples regardless of their mutual independence. To address this issue, recent studies have introduced several variant algorithms of ICL that achieve permutation invariance. However, many of these do not exhibit comparable performance with the standard auto-regressive ICL algorithm. In this work, we identify two crucial elements in the design of an invariant ICL algorithm: information non-leakage and context interdependence, which are not simultaneously achieved by any of the existing methods. These investigations lead us to the proposed Invariant ICL (InvICL), a methodology designed to achieve invariance in ICL while ensuring the two properties. Empirically, our findings reveal that InvICL surpasses previous models, both invariant and non-invariant, in most benchmark datasets, showcasing superior generalization capabilities across varying input lengths. Code is available at https://github.com/PKU-ML/InvICL.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the order sensitivity issue in In-Context Learning (ICL) for large language models (LLMs). It identifies two key elements for designing an invariant ICL algorithm: information non-leakage and context interdependence.  The paper proposes Invariant ICL (InvICL), a method that achieves invariance while maintaining these two properties through a leave-one-out embedding scheme. InvICL outperforms both invariant and non-invariant baselines on benchmark datasets, demonstrating improved generalization. The paper also provides theoretical analysis of InvICL from an optimization perspective, linking it to gradient descent.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies primarily in the *combination* of known concepts and their specific instantiation in InvICL. While invariant ICL, information non-leakage and context interdependence have been explored individually, the paper highlights their simultaneous importance and devises a new method to achieve them concurrently. The leave-one-out approach, while not entirely new, is cleverly adapted to this specific context.

*   **Significance:** The paper is significant because it offers a practical solution to a known problem in ICL, order sensitivity, while not sacrificing performance.  The improved generalization capabilities demonstrated by InvICL are also important, suggesting that this approach can lead to more robust and reliable ICL systems. The theoretical connection to gradient descent provides a potentially fruitful avenue for further research.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   Clear problem statement and well-defined desiderata for invariant ICL.
    *   Thorough empirical evaluation across synthetic and real-world datasets.
    *   Theoretical analysis connecting InvICL to gradient descent.
    *   Ablation studies to demonstrate the importance of both invariant mask and symmetric PE.
    *   Addresses a significant issue regarding practical application of ICL for broader deployment.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The novelty is incremental. While the combination of concepts is clever, the individual components are not entirely novel.
    *   The parallel implementation increases the memory costs by 14%. The practical implication of memory consumption is brushed over and there could be some deployments where it is infeasible to increase memory by that much.
    *   While the optimization connection is intriguing, the theoretical analysis section is limited and could be more rigorous and insightful. There needs to be further clarification of the constraints on the attention weight matrices and show that actual pre-trained models align to these constraints.
    *   The code has not yet been thoroughly checked to ensure that there were no implementation errors in the compared baselines.

*   **Potential Impact:** InvICL has the potential to influence future research on invariant ICL algorithms.  It provides a new benchmark and a set of design principles for developing better invariant ICL systems. The connection to gradient descent also opens up avenues for exploring theoretical properties of ICL.

*   **Overall Assessment:**
    The paper offers a valuable contribution by identifying key desiderata for invariant ICL and introducing a practical algorithm that achieves them, alongside valuable analysis. The combination of known ideas contributes to a performance boost and could influence ICL deployment for broader use.

### Inter-Diffusion Generation Model of Speakers and Listeners for Effective Communication
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.04996v1)
- **Authors**: Jinhe Huang, Yongkang Cheng, Yuming Hang, Gaoge Han, Jinewei Li, Jing Zhang, Xingjian Gu
- **Abstract**: Full-body gestures play a pivotal role in natural interactions and are crucial for achieving effective communication. Nevertheless, most existing studies primarily focus on the gesture generation of speakers, overlooking the vital role of listeners in the interaction process and failing to fully explore the dynamic interaction between them. This paper innovatively proposes an Inter-Diffusion Generation Model of Speakers and Listeners for Effective Communication. For the first time, we integrate the full-body gestures of listeners into the generation framework. By devising a novel inter-diffusion mechanism, this model can accurately capture the complex interaction patterns between speakers and listeners during communication. In the model construction process, based on the advanced diffusion model architecture, we innovatively introduce interaction conditions and the GAN model to increase the denoising step size. As a result, when generating gesture sequences, the model can not only dynamically generate based on the speaker's speech information but also respond in realtime to the listener's feedback, enabling synergistic interaction between the two. Abundant experimental results demonstrate that compared with the current state-of-the-art gesture generation methods, the model we proposed has achieved remarkable improvements in the naturalness, coherence, and speech-gesture synchronization of the generated gestures. In the subjective evaluation experiments, users highly praised the generated interaction scenarios, believing that they are closer to real life human communication situations. Objective index evaluations also show that our model outperforms the baseline methods in multiple key indicators, providing more powerful support for effective communication.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces an "Inter-Diffusion Generation Model of Speakers and Listeners for Effective Communication."  This model aims to improve the realism of virtual interactions by generating not only speaker gestures but also listener gestures. It uses an inter-diffusion mechanism to capture the dynamic interplay between speaker and listener gestures based on speech content and listener feedback. The model uses a diffusion architecture and incorporates interaction conditions alongside a GAN to enhance the denoising process. Experimental results show improved naturalness, coherence, and speech-gesture synchronization compared to existing speaker-focused methods. The authors incorporate data from the TWH and ZeroEGG datasets, modify their architecture, and introduce a new loss function to enhance communication and training efficiency between speaker and listener. They also carry out ablation experiments and conduct an extensive user study to validate their approach.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:** The primary novelty lies in explicitly modeling the listener's gestures within a co-speech gesture generation framework.  Existing works largely focus on generating gestures for speakers. Adding listener behaviors creates a more comprehensive, realistic simulation of human conversation. The inter-diffusion mechanism, along with the adjusted conditions based on roles, contributes to the novelty. However, this is incremental over existing diffusion model architectures. They are using a common diffusion model, but modify input conditions.

* **Significance:**  Generating realistic and interactive virtual humans is important for VR/AR, gaming, and film applications.  The paper addresses a significant limitation in current gesture generation research – the lack of attention to listener responses. By modeling these responses, the generated interactions become more believable and immersive. However, the model's dependency on data quantity and type for both the speaker and listener is a concern.

* **Strengths:**
    *   **Addresses a Gap:** Directly tackles the under-explored aspect of listener gestures in co-speech gesture generation.
    *   **Inter-Diffusion Mechanism:** The method is well-explained. The proposed inter-diffusion mechanism aims at a natural flow of gesture generation between both speaker and listener.
    *   **Comprehensive Evaluation:**  The paper presents thorough objective and subjective evaluations. The inclusion of a user study strengthens the claims of improved realism.
    *   **Ablation Studies:**  The ablation study helps to show the contributions of the different model components.
    *   **Dataset Combination:** Leveraging existing datasets, TWH and ZeroEGG, to build a more suitable training set for the task, which may push the training further.
*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Incremental Over Diffusion Models:** While novel in application, the core underlying architecture is still rooted in known diffusion model techniques.
    *   **Data Dependency:** Relies heavily on the availability of high-quality, paired speaker-listener data. The need for such data may limit the generalizability of this approach.
    *   **Limited Generalizability** The current models depend on limited contexts and do not generalize well.
    *   **Computational Cost:** Diffusion models are known to be computationally expensive, and the paper could benefit from explicitly discussing the model's efficiency and scalability for real-time applications. Also, since it relies on both TWH and ZeroEGG, this may become a problem in future applications.

*   **Potential Impact:** The paper has the potential to influence research in gesture generation, particularly in creating more realistic and engaging virtual human interactions. It might encourage the development of new datasets and models that explicitly consider the role of listeners.

**Rigorous Rationale for Score:**

The paper presents a novel approach by incorporating listener gestures into a gesture generation pipeline, but leverages established methods with diffusion modeling. The evaluation is robust and shows improvements, but the impact is moderate and the limitations are significant. The incremental modification of a prevalent model is not highly impactful. It is thus a modest contribution but not a trivial one.
