### The Pitfalls of Growing Group Complexity: LLMs and Social Choice-Based Aggregation for Group Recommendations
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.05016v1)
- **Authors**: Cedric Waterschoot, Nava Tintarev, Francesco Barile
- **Abstract**: Large Language Models (LLMs) are increasingly applied in recommender systems aimed at both individuals and groups. Previously, Group Recommender Systems (GRS) often used social choice-based aggregation strategies to derive a single recommendation based on the preferences of multiple people. In this paper, we investigate under which conditions language models can perform these strategies correctly based on zero-shot learning and analyse whether the formatting of the group scenario in the prompt affects accuracy. We specifically focused on the impact of group complexity (number of users and items), different LLMs, different prompting conditions, including In-Context learning or generating explanations, and the formatting of group preferences. Our results show that performance starts to deteriorate when considering more than 100 ratings. However, not all language models were equally sensitive to growing group complexity. Additionally, we showed that In-Context Learning (ICL) can significantly increase the performance at higher degrees of group complexity, while adding other prompt modifications, specifying domain cues or prompting for explanations, did not impact accuracy. We conclude that future research should include group complexity as a factor in GRS evaluation due to its effect on LLM performance. Furthermore, we showed that formatting the group scenarios differently, such as rating lists per user or per item, affected accuracy. All in all, our study implies that smaller LLMs are capable of generating group recommendations under the right conditions, making the case for using smaller models that require less computing power and costs.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper investigates the performance of Large Language Models (LLMs) in group recommendation systems when using social choice-based aggregation strategies. It focuses on how group complexity (number of users and items) impacts the accuracy of LLMs in zero-shot learning scenarios. The study explores different LLMs, prompt conditions (including in-context learning and explanation generation), and the formatting of group preferences. The key findings are: performance deteriorates with increasing group complexity (especially beyond 100 ratings); LLMs show varying sensitivity to complexity; in-context learning improves performance significantly at higher complexity; prompt modifications (domain cues, explanation prompts) do not greatly improve accuracy; and the format in which group scenarios are presented influences accuracy. The paper argues for considering group complexity as a factor in GRS evaluation and advocates for smaller LLMs with optimized conditions for cost and efficiency.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper contributes to the understanding of LLM capabilities within the context of group recommender systems, specifically regarding the impact of group complexity.  While LLMs have been used in recommendation before, a focused analysis of *complexity* as a key factor influencing accuracy, especially in conjunction with social choice-based aggregation, is a valuable contribution. The investigation of prompt engineering variations (in-context learning, explanations) provides incremental, but useful, insights. However, many of the prompt techniques and aggregation strategies explored have been examined in other LLM contexts, limiting the novelty to their specific application within GRS under complexity constraints.

*   **Significance:** Identifying the limitations of LLMs in handling growing group complexity is significant for the development of practical GRS using LLMs. The paper addresses a real-world consideration that could impede the adoption of LLMs in recommendation systems dealing with large groups or extensive item sets.  The finding that smaller LLMs can perform well under optimized conditions is also relevant for resource-constrained scenarios. The observations concerning the influence of data formatting are practically useful. The paper's weakness lies in the fairly standard methodological choices, and limited evaluation depth of the model and its prompt choices.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   Systematic investigation of group complexity as a variable.
    *   Comparison of multiple LLMs.
    *   Analysis of different prompting strategies.
    *   Practical recommendations for using smaller LLMs and optimizing data format.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   Limited novelty in prompt engineering techniques, adapting them to the specific domain is worthwhile.
    *   The experimental setup is relatively straightforward. There's a lack of sophistication in prompt design or exploration of more advanced aggregation strategies.
    *   The reliance on synthetic data limits the generalizability of the results to real-world scenarios.
    *   The definition of accuracy could be more rigorous;  a soft definition is understandable but should have been rigorously justified.
    *   The paper does not include an ablative analysis to justify the prompt choices; for example, why *those* three examples for in-context learning.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper can influence the field by:

    *   Highlighting the need to incorporate group complexity in GRS evaluation when using LLMs.
    *   Encouraging researchers to explore smaller, locally runnable LLMs with tailored prompting strategies for GRS.
    *   Guiding the development of prompt engineering techniques specifically for GRS tasks.

*   **Score Justification:** The paper makes a valuable contribution by highlighting the influence of complexity on LLM performance within the context of Group Recommender Systems. It is a solid empirical study that extends the existing knowledge, but the study lacks the groundbreaking impact to earn a higher score.  The methodology is sound but not particularly innovative, and the reliance on synthetic data and the soft definition of accuracy limits the scope of the results. Nevertheless, the study is well-motivated and the results provides practical insights to guide future efforts on GRS, justifying the assigned score.

**Score: 6.5**

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 6/10

### Scalable Multi-Stage Influence Function for Large Language Models via Eigenvalue-Corrected Kronecker-Factored Parameterization
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.05017v1)
- **Authors**: Yuntai Bao, Xuhong Zhang, Tianyu Du, Xinkui Zhao, Jiang Zong, Hao Peng, Jianwei Yin
- **Abstract**: Pre-trained large language models (LLMs) are commonly fine-tuned to adapt to downstream tasks. Since the majority of knowledge is acquired during pre-training, attributing the predictions of fine-tuned LLMs to their pre-training data may provide valuable insights. Influence functions have been proposed as a means to explain model predictions based on training data. However, existing approaches fail to compute ``multi-stage'' influence and lack scalability to billion-scale LLMs. In this paper, we propose the multi-stage influence function to attribute the downstream predictions of fine-tuned LLMs to pre-training data under the full-parameter fine-tuning paradigm. To enhance the efficiency and practicality of our multi-stage influence function, we leverage Eigenvalue-corrected Kronecker-Factored (EK-FAC) parameterization for efficient approximation. Empirical results validate the superior scalability of EK-FAC approximation and the effectiveness of our multi-stage influence function. Additionally, case studies on a real-world LLM, dolly-v2-3b, demonstrate its interpretive power, with exemplars illustrating insights provided by multi-stage influence estimates. Our code is public at https://github.com/colored-dye/multi_stage_influence_function.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the challenge of attributing predictions made by fine-tuned large language models (LLMs) back to their original pre-training data. It proposes a multi-stage influence function framework that traces the influence of pre-training data on the downstream predictions of fine-tuned LLMs, even when the fine-tuning process significantly modifies the model (e.g., changes the output layer). To improve scalability, the authors employ Eigenvalue-corrected Kronecker-Factored (EK-FAC) parameterization to approximate inverse Hessian-Vector Products (iHVPs) and use semantic similarity heuristics to narrow down the candidate training samples. They conduct experiments to demonstrate the scalability of the EK-FAC approximation, the effectiveness of the multi-stage influence function, and its applicability to a publicly available instruction-tuned LLM, showing insights into the LLM's generation based on pre-training data.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:** The key novelty lies in combining multi-stage influence functions with EK-FAC parameterization for scaling to large language models under full-parameter fine-tuning scenarios.  While multi-stage influence functions and EK-FAC have been used previously, their combination in this specific context and scale represents a significant improvement. Specifically, it extends the original IF framework and addresses limitations of prior approaches like frozen encoders stacked with linear classifiers by addressing the issue of output domain mismatch and accommodating the full-parameter tuning paradigm prevalent in LLMs.
* **Significance:** Understanding the influence of pre-training data is crucial for building trustworthy AI systems and providing interpretability for LLMs. The ability to trace predictions back to the original data enables users to better understand the model's behavior, identify potential biases, and verify the grounding of the model's outputs.  The paper offers a practical approach for achieving this, addressing a significant bottleneck in existing influence function methods by improving scalability to billion-scale models. The paper presents a qualitative use case using the developed multi-stage influence function on the Dolly-v2-3b LLM to qualitatively explain its generations based on pre-training data.
* **Strengths:**
    * **Scalability:** The EK-FAC approximation significantly reduces the computational cost of iHVP calculations, enabling the analysis of much larger models than previously possible.
    * **Practicality:**  The combination of EK-FAC and semantic-similarity-based candidate selection provides a practical way to apply influence functions to real-world LLMs.
    * **Empirical Validation:** The experiments provide strong evidence of the scalability of the EK-FAC approximation and the effectiveness of the multi-stage influence function, showing it outperforms simpler methods.
    * **Case Study:** The qualitative case study with Dolly-v2-3b demonstrates the interpretive power of the method, providing concrete examples of how the multi-stage influence function can be used to understand model behavior.
* **Weaknesses:**
    * **Approximations:** The use of EK-FAC and semantic similarity introduces approximations that may reduce the accuracy of the influence estimates.  While the experiments show that EK-FAC provides a good trade-off between accuracy and efficiency, the impact of these approximations on the interpretability of the results should be considered. There is an observed performance gap between the multi-stage IF and simpler methods like GDP with large damping terms. This underscores the necessity for careful selection of the damping term in order to attain the best performance from the IFs.
    * **Decoder-Only Architecture:** The current implementation is limited to decoder-only transformer architectures, which limits its applicability to other types of models (e.g., encoder-decoder models or diffusion models).
    * **Limited Component Analysis**: Analysis is limited to backbones only (MLP and MHA). Excluding other components could potentially limit improvement of influence estimates,
* **Potential Influence:** The paper is likely to have a significant influence on the field of LLM interpretability and trustworthiness.  It provides a practical and scalable method for understanding the behavior of these models, enabling users to better understand their biases and verify the grounding of their outputs.  The approach could be used to develop more robust and reliable LLMs, and could also be used to identify and mitigate potential risks associated with these models.

**Justification of Score:**

I assign a score of **8**. The paper provides a significant contribution to the field by addressing a crucial problem in LLM interpretability and trustworthiness: tracing predictions back to pre-training data. It introduces a novel combination of techniques that significantly improves the scalability and practicality of influence function methods.  The paper is well-written, the experiments are thorough, and the results are compelling. While the method relies on approximations and is currently limited to decoder-only models, these limitations do not detract from the paper's overall contribution. Furthermore, it highlights an area ripe for subsequent exploration and refinement.

Score: 8

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 8/10

### SOAP: Style-Omniscient Animatable Portraits
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.05022v1)
- **Authors**: Tingting Liao, Yujian Zheng, Adilbek Karmanov, Liwen Hu, Leyang Jin, Yuliang Xiu, Hao Li
- **Abstract**: Creating animatable 3D avatars from a single image remains challenging due to style limitations (realistic, cartoon, anime) and difficulties in handling accessories or hairstyles. While 3D diffusion models advance single-view reconstruction for general objects, outputs often lack animation controls or suffer from artifacts because of the domain gap. We propose SOAP, a style-omniscient framework to generate rigged, topology-consistent avatars from any portrait. Our method leverages a multiview diffusion model trained on 24K 3D heads with multiple styles and an adaptive optimization pipeline to deform the FLAME mesh while maintaining topology and rigging via differentiable rendering. The resulting textured avatars support FACS-based animation, integrate with eyeballs and teeth, and preserve details like braided hair or accessories. Extensive experiments demonstrate the superiority of our method over state-of-the-art techniques for both single-view head modeling and diffusion-based generation of Image-to-3D. Our code and data are publicly available for research purposes at https://github.com/TingtingLiao/soap.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the SOAP paper:

**Summary:**

The paper "SOAP: Style-Omniscient Animatable Portraits" introduces a novel pipeline for generating animatable 3D head avatars from a single 2D portrait image, regardless of the style (realistic, cartoon, anime, etc.). The method combines a style-omniscient multi-view diffusion model, trained on a large dataset of 3D heads, with a differentiable rendering-based optimization technique that adapts a FLAME mesh to the target appearance while preserving animation rigging and topology. The resulting avatars are rigged for FACS-based animation, include eyeballs and teeth, and can reproduce complex hairstyles and accessories.

**Critical Evaluation:**

The paper addresses an important and challenging problem: creating animatable 3D avatars from single images across a wide range of styles.  The core strength of SOAP lies in its demonstrated ability to handle diverse artistic styles (style-omniscient), which has been a limitation of many previous single-view 3D reconstruction methods.  The combination of a diffusion model for multi-view generation and a differentiable rendering-based fitting process is a sound approach.

Here's a breakdown of the strengths and weaknesses:

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Style-agnostic approach:** The demonstrated ability to generalize to a wide range of styles (realistic, cartoon, anime) is a significant contribution. This is enabled by the carefully curated 3D head dataset and the design of the multi-view diffusion model.
    *   **Animatability:** Unlike many image-to-3D methods, SOAP produces fully rigged avatars with FACS controls, making them immediately useful for animation. The inclusion of eyeballs and teeth further enhances the realism.
    *   **Handling complexity:**  The method effectively captures complex hairstyles and accessories, which are often problematic for single-view reconstruction techniques.
    *   **Differentiable rendering:** The use of differentiable rendering is crucial for optimizing the mesh topology and skinning weights, allowing for fine-grained control over the avatar's shape and animation.
    *   **Clear presentation and evaluation:** The paper is well-written and includes thorough qualitative and quantitative comparisons against state-of-the-art methods. The ablation studies provide valuable insights into the importance of different components of the pipeline.
*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Dependency on Pre-trained Models:** The method relies on several pre-trained models including FLAME estimation, face parsing, and landmark detection. While this is common, performance will be directly limited by these components. The authors acknowledge this limitation and note that it is most apparent with stylized inputs where landmark and parsing detection may be less accurate.
    *   **Complexity:** The method involves multiple steps (diffusion, optimization, remeshing, etc.), increasing the potential for failure and the computational cost. While inference time is reported, it may be a concern for real-time applications, especially on lower-end hardware.
    *   **Limited resolution:** The limited resolution of the output, while sufficient, could be a further improvement, potentially aided by increased GPU resources.
    *   **Potential Dataset Bias:** While the dataset is claimed to be style omniscient, one must acknowledge biases present and the need for further investigation of the method's performance on truly novel/rare styles.
*   **Novelty:**

    *   The style-omniscient approach to single-view animatable avatar creation is relatively novel. Previous methods often focused on specific styles or lacked animatability. The combination of a multiview diffusion model and a differentiable mesh deformation pipeline is also innovative.

*   **Significance:**

    *   The paper has the potential to significantly impact the field of avatar creation and virtual reality. The ability to generate high-quality, animatable avatars from single images could streamline character production in games, films, and other interactive applications. The style-agnostic nature of the method makes it particularly valuable for creating personalized avatars across a wide range of artistic styles.

**Justification for the Score:**

While SOAP has weaknesses, its strengths outweigh them and its contributions are significant. The ability to create animatable avatars across a diverse range of styles, and the innovative combination of diffusion models and differentiable rendering-based optimization, make this paper a valuable contribution to the field. The limitations regarding the pre-trained models, complexity, and resolution are acknowledged and represent avenues for future work.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10

### LSRP: A Leader-Subordinate Retrieval Framework for Privacy-Preserving Cloud-Device Collaboration
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.05031v1)
- **Authors**: Yingyi Zhang, Pengyue Jia, Xianneng Li, Derong Xu, Maolin Wang, Yichao Wang, Zhaocheng Du, Huifeng Guo, Yong Liu, Ruiming Tang, Xiangyu Zhao
- **Abstract**: Cloud-device collaboration leverages on-cloud Large Language Models (LLMs) for handling public user queries and on-device Small Language Models (SLMs) for processing private user data, collectively forming a powerful and privacy-preserving solution. However, existing approaches often fail to fully leverage the scalable problem-solving capabilities of on-cloud LLMs while underutilizing the advantage of on-device SLMs in accessing and processing personalized data. This leads to two interconnected issues: 1) Limited utilization of the problem-solving capabilities of on-cloud LLMs, which fail to align with personalized user-task needs, and 2) Inadequate integration of user data into on-device SLM responses, resulting in mismatches in contextual user information. In this paper, we propose a Leader-Subordinate Retrieval framework for Privacy-preserving cloud-device collaboration (LSRP), a novel solution that bridges these gaps by: 1) enhancing on-cloud LLM guidance to on-device SLM through a dynamic selection of task-specific leader strategies named as user-to-user retrieval-augmented generation (U-U-RAG), and 2) integrating the data advantages of on-device SLMs through small model feedback Direct Preference Optimization (SMFB-DPO) for aligning the on-cloud LLM with the on-device SLM. Experiments on two datasets demonstrate that LSRP consistently outperforms state-of-the-art baselines, significantly improving question-answer relevance and personalization, while preserving user privacy through efficient on-device retrieval. Our code is available at: https://github.com/Zhang-Yingyi/LSRP.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces LSRP (Leader-Subordinate Retrieval Framework), a novel framework for privacy-preserving cloud-device collaboration in scenarios involving Large Language Models (LLMs) and Small Language Models (SLMs). LSRP aims to address limitations in existing cloud-device frameworks that often underutilize both the problem-solving capabilities of on-cloud LLMs and the data-access advantages of on-device SLMs. The framework enhances on-cloud LLM guidance to on-device SLMs through a dynamic selection of task-specific leader strategies (U-U-RAG, User-to-User Retrieval-Augmented Generation) and integrates on-device SLM data advantages through small model feedback (SMFB-DPO) to align the on-cloud LLM. Experiments on two datasets demonstrate that LSRP consistently outperforms state-of-the-art baselines, improving question-answer relevance and personalization while preserving user privacy through efficient on-device retrieval.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies in its combined approach of dynamic leader strategy selection (U-U-RAG) and feedback-driven optimization (SMFB-DPO) within a cloud-device collaboration framework.  While individual components like RAG and DPO are well-established, their integration in this specific context, designed to address the unique challenges of privacy-preserving cloud-device LLMs, provides a degree of originality.  The conceptualization of the cloud LLM as a "leader" providing different styles of guidance to the "subordinate" on-device SLM is a helpful framing. The novelty is also derived from tailoring these components to the specific challenges present within device-cloud collaborative framework where user data remains on device for privacy reasons.

*   **Significance:** The significance of the work stems from the increasing importance of privacy-preserving AI solutions. The framework effectively addresses the tradeoff between model performance and data privacy in collaborative LLM scenarios.  The reported improvements in relevance and personalization compared to baseline methods suggest practical value. Also, the ablation studies provide insights into the contribution of each component and offer potential directions for future work.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Clear Problem Definition:** The paper clearly articulates the limitations of existing cloud-device collaboration frameworks.
    *   **Well-Defined Solution:**  LSRP is presented as a well-structured framework with clear components (U-U-RAG and SMFB-DPO).
    *   **Empirical Validation:** The experiments on multiple datasets provide evidence supporting the effectiveness of LSRP.
    *   **Ablation Studies:**  The ablation studies offer a detailed understanding of each component's contribution.
    *   **Comprehensive Evaluation Metrics:** The evaluation metrics consider both relevance and personalization.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Dependency on external GPT-4 for Evaluation:** Relying on an external LLM, albeit a strong one, to evaluate the framework brings some amount of bias, and could create evaluation instability if GPT-4 undergoes changes in the future. Using other objective measure beside LLM would strengthen the evaluation.
    *   **Synthetic Data:** Reliance on synthetic data for one dataset, while acceptable, limits the real-world applicability of that experimental analysis.
    *   **Complexity:** The framework, while well-defined, is relatively complex to implement. The components require careful tuning to maximize performance.
    *   **Latency analysis**: While the inference-stage latency is acknowledged, the analysis can benefit from being more comprehensive. Include more detailed breakdown of latency by component could highlight potential bottlenecks for optimization.

*   **Potential Influence:** This work is likely to influence future research in privacy-preserving cloud-device collaboration. It provides a solid foundation for developing more sophisticated and efficient frameworks that can leverage the power of LLMs while respecting user privacy. The U-U-RAG and SMFB-DPO techniques could be adopted or adapted in other collaborative AI systems.  However, the real-world adoption depends on simplifying the implementation and demonstrating scalability in various use cases.

*   **Justification for the Score:** The paper presents a reasonably novel framework with a clear problem statement, a well-defined solution, and solid empirical validation. However, dependencies on GPT-4 for evaluation introduce bias, complex implementation and synthetic user data limit the generalizability, and lack of a more thorough latency analysis are significant limitations.  The theoretical and conceptual innovations are incremental rather than groundbreaking.

- **Classification**: cs.IR
- **Score**: 4/10

### Divide-and-Conquer: Cold-Start Bundle Recommendation via Mixture of Diffusion Experts
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.05035v1)
- **Authors**: Ming Li, Lin Li, Xiaohui Tao, Dong Zhang, Jimmy Xiangji Huang
- **Abstract**: Cold-start bundle recommendation focuses on modeling new bundles with insufficient information to provide recommendations. Advanced bundle recommendation models usually learn bundle representations from multiple views (e.g., interaction view) at both the bundle and item levels. Consequently, the cold-start problem for bundles is more challenging than that for traditional items due to the dual-level multi-view complexity. In this paper, we propose a novel Mixture of Diffusion Experts (MoDiffE) framework, which employs a divide-and-conquer strategy for cold-start bundle recommendation and follows three steps:(1) Divide: The bundle cold-start problem is divided into independent but similar sub-problems sequentially by level and view, which can be summarized as the poor representation of feature-missing bundles in prior-embedding models. (2) Conquer: Beyond prior-embedding models that fundamentally provide the embedded representations, we introduce a diffusion-based method to solve all sub-problems in a unified way, which directly generates diffusion representations using diffusion models without depending on specific features. (3) Combine: A cold-aware hierarchical Mixture of Experts (MoE) is employed to combine results of the sub-problems for final recommendations, where the two models for each view serve as experts and are adaptively fused for different bundles in a multi-layer manner. Additionally, MoDiffE adopts a multi-stage decoupled training pipeline and introduces a cold-start gating augmentation method to enable the training of gating for cold bundles. Through extensive experiments on three real-world datasets, we demonstrate that MoDiffE significantly outperforms existing solutions in handling cold-start bundle recommendation. It achieves up to a 0.1027 absolute gain in Recall@20 in cold-start scenarios and up to a 47.43\% relative improvement in all-bundle scenarios.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper "Divide-and-Conquer: Cold-Start Bundle Recommendation via Mixture of Diffusion Experts" addresses the challenging problem of cold-start bundle recommendation, where new bundles lack sufficient interaction data. The authors propose a novel framework called MoDiffE (Mixture of Diffusion Experts). It leverages a divide-and-conquer strategy: first, it divides the problem into smaller, manageable sub-problems by considering the bundle-level and item-level interactions separately. Second, it conquers each sub-problem using diffusion models to generate representations for cold-start bundles and items, effectively learning to denoise representations without relying on existing embeddings. Finally, it combines the results using a cold-aware hierarchical Mixture of Experts (MoE) architecture, adaptively weighting the contributions of different views (bundle-level and item-level) and experts. The framework uses a multi-stage training pipeline and incorporates a cold-start gating augmentation method to improve training the MoE. The authors empirically validate MoDiffE on three real-world datasets, demonstrating significant performance gains over existing cold-start bundle recommendation methods.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:**

The paper has several novel aspects:

1.  **Formalizing Dual-Level Multi-View Complexity:** The paper explicitly acknowledges and formalizes the dual-level (bundle/item) multi-view interaction problem within cold-start bundle recommendation.  This is a significant contribution. Previous approaches often implicitly addressed these complexities without making them explicit.

2.  **Diffusion Models for Cold Start:** The use of diffusion models to generate representations directly, bypassing the need for feature embeddings for cold bundles and items, is a compelling idea.  It addresses the inherent limitation of feature-based methods, which struggle with missing data.

3.  **Cold-Aware Hierarchical MoE:** The MoE architecture, particularly with the cold-start gating augmentation, aims to adaptively handle bundles with varying degrees of cold start. This architecture is key for stable aggregation.

4.  **Divide-and-Conquer Approach:** The divide-and-conquer strategy simplifies what could be an overwhelmingly complex problem. It contributes by providing a structured solution process.

**Significance:**

*   **Improved Performance:** The empirical results clearly demonstrate that MoDiffE outperforms existing state-of-the-art methods in cold-start and all-bundle scenarios.
*   **Practical Applicability:**  The improved performance in the cold-start setting is crucial for real-world applications, as it allows newly introduced bundles to gain visibility and provide relevant recommendations early on.
*   **Framework Adaptability:**  The modularity of MoDiffE allows for the framework to be tailored with different implementations (e.g. different prior-embedding models or different diffusion solver techniques).

**Strengths:**

*   **Well-Defined Problem:** The paper clearly defines the cold-start bundle recommendation problem, emphasizing its dual-level, multi-view complexity.
*   **Novel and Well-Justified Approach:** The proposed MoDiffE framework is innovative and well-motivated. The use of diffusion models, MoE, and the divide-and-conquer strategy are all thoughtfully explained and justified.
*   **Comprehensive Experiments:** The experimental evaluation is extensive, using multiple real-world datasets and comparing against a range of baselines.
*   **Detailed Ablation Study:** The ablation study effectively demonstrates the contribution of each component of MoDiffE.
*   **Insightful Analysis:** The visualizations and case studies provide valuable insights into how MoDiffE works.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Computational Complexity:** The use of diffusion models can be computationally expensive, which might be a concern for large-scale recommendation systems. Although they use a fast inference solver, the complexity may still be high compared to traditional methods. This is a potential limitation.
*   **Parameter Sensitivity:** Diffusion models have many hyperparameters to be tuned. The process of tuning hyperparameters can be costly.
*   **Black Box Nature:**  While the case study is helpful, diffusion models can be perceived as black boxes.  Further investigation into *why* specific bundled are handled particularly well and others are less so would strengthen the paper. This would help understand model failure modes, which are particularly important with limited data.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper makes a significant contribution to the field of bundle recommendation, particularly in the challenging area of cold start. The explicit acknowledgement of the dual-level multi-view complexity and the innovative use of diffusion models sets this paper apart from prior work.  The experimental results are convincing, and the ablation studies provide valuable insights.  While the computational complexity of diffusion models is a potential limitation, the demonstrated performance gains and the well-defined problem warrant a high score.

**Score: 8.5**

The formalization of the problem and the innovative solution are substantial contributions, and the results are well-supported. The computational concerns are a valid consideration for real-world scaling, but do not detract from the paper's overall significance and impact. A slightly higher score could be warranted if the black box nature of the diffusion model was addressed with explanation methodologies for individual bundle recommendations.

- **Classification**: cs.IR
- **Score**: 8/10

### Towards Mitigating API Hallucination in Code Generated by LLMs with Hierarchical Dependency Aware
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.05057v1)
- **Authors**: Yujia Chen, Mingyu Chen, Cuiyun Gao, Zhihan Jiang, Zhongqi Li, Yuchi Ma
- **Abstract**: Application Programming Interfaces (APIs) are crucial in modern software development. Large Language Models (LLMs) assist in automated code generation but often struggle with API hallucination, including invoking non-existent APIs and misusing existing ones in practical development scenarios. Existing studies resort to Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) methods for mitigating the hallucination issue, but tend to fail since they generally ignore the structural dependencies in practical projects and do not indeed validate whether the generated APIs are available or not. To address these limitations, we propose MARIN, a framework for mitigating API hallucination in code generated by LLMs with hierarchical dependency aware. MARIN consists of two phases: Hierarchical Dependency Mining, which analyzes local and global dependencies of the current function, aiming to supplement comprehensive project context in LLMs input, and Dependency Constrained Decoding, which utilizes mined dependencies to adaptively constrain the generation process, aiming to ensure the generated APIs align with the projects specifications. To facilitate the evaluation of the degree of API hallucination, we introduce a new benchmark APIHulBench and two new metrics including Micro Hallucination Number (MiHN) and Macro Hallucination Rate (MaHR). Experiments on six state-of-the-art LLMs demonstrate that MARIN effectively reduces API hallucinations, achieving an average decrease of 67.52% in MiHN and 73.56% in MaHR compared to the RAG approach. Applied to Huaweis internal projects and two proprietary LLMs, MARIN achieves average decreases of 57.33% in MiHN and 59.41% in MaHR.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "Towards Mitigating API Hallucination in Code Generated by LLMs with Hierarchical Dependency Aware":

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the problem of API hallucination in code generated by Large Language Models (LLMs). The authors propose MARIN, a framework that mitigates this issue by incorporating hierarchical dependency awareness. MARIN consists of two phases: 1) Hierarchical Dependency Mining, which analyzes local and global dependencies to enrich the LLM's input, and 2) Dependency Constrained Decoding, which uses these dependencies to constrain token generation and ensure alignment with project specifications.  The paper introduces a new benchmark, APIHulBench, and two metrics, MiHN and MaHR, to evaluate API hallucination.  Experiments on several state-of-the-art LLMs demonstrate that MARIN reduces API hallucinations compared to Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) and other baselines.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:** The paper presents a novel framework, MARIN, which distinguishes itself from existing RAG-based approaches by explicitly modelling and leveraging hierarchical dependencies in software projects. The Hierarchical Dependency Mining phase allows for more comprehensive context beyond simple code snippet retrieval. The Dependency Constrained Decoding ensures that generated APIs conform to the project's specifications, a notable improvement over unconstrained generation. The creation of a new benchmark, APIHulBench, tailored for evaluating API hallucination with up-to-date code samples, is another contribution.

**Significance:** API hallucination is a critical problem in LLM-based code generation, directly impacting code correctness and developer productivity. By effectively reducing this issue, MARIN has the potential to enhance the reliability of code generated by LLMs. The new benchmark and metrics provide valuable tools for evaluating and comparing different mitigation techniques. The experiments, performed on both open-source and proprietary models, demonstrate the practicality and generalizability of MARIN. The results showing substantial reductions in hallucination rates and improvements in accuracy are significant.

**Strengths:**

*   **Clear problem definition:** The paper clearly defines the problem of API hallucination and its limitations.
*   **Novel approach:** MARIN introduces a novel approach that overcomes the limitations of RAG by considering the structural dependencies in software projects.
*   **Rigorous evaluation:** The paper presents a thorough experimental evaluation with appropriate benchmarks, metrics, and baselines.
*   **Practical validation:** Evaluating MARIN on both open-source LLMs and internal models/projects significantly strengthens the practical relevance of the findings.
*   **Well-written and organized:** The paper is well-structured and easy to follow.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Scope:** The evaluation is primarily focused on Java projects. While the authors claim the framework is language-agnostic, further investigation is needed for other languages.
*   **Scalability of dependency mining:**  The hierarchical dependency mining may face scalability challenges in very large projects. The paper should discuss potential optimization strategies.
*   **Limitations of static analysis:** The paper relies on static analysis for dependency mining.  Dynamic analysis or hybrid approaches could potentially uncover additional dependencies that are missed by static analysis.
*   **Greedy decoding:** While simple, greedy decoding as a constrained decoding strategy can be suboptimal. A more sophisticated decoding strategy like beam search could be considered, albeit at increased computational cost.

**Justification for Score:**

I am assigning a score of 7.5 to this paper. While MARIN shows a very strong approach, it presents incremental advancements. The novelty lies in its hierarchical dependency-aware structure, but the underlying static analysis and decoding techniques, while functional, are well-established. The significance is high, as the paper tackles a practically important problem, demonstrated by the evaluation on proprietary models. But the overall impact could be greater with future research involving more diverse programming languages, larger projects, and more advanced dependency extraction and decoding methods.

Score: 7.5

- **Classification**: cs.SE
- **Score**: 7/10

### CodeMixBench: Evaluating Large Language Models on Code Generation with Code-Mixed Prompts
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.05063v1)
- **Authors**: Manik Sheokand, Parth Sawant
- **Abstract**: Large Language Models (LLMs) have achieved remarkable success in code generation tasks, powering various applications like code completion, debugging, and programming assistance. However, existing benchmarks such as HumanEval, MBPP, and BigCodeBench primarily evaluate LLMs on English-only prompts, overlooking the real-world scenario where multilingual developers often use code-mixed language while interacting with LLMs. To address this gap, we introduce CodeMixBench, a novel benchmark designed to evaluate the robustness of LLMs on code generation from code-mixed prompts. Built upon BigCodeBench, CodeMixBench introduces controlled code-mixing (CMD) into the natural language parts of prompts across three language pairs: Hinglish (Hindi-English), Spanish-English, and Chinese Pinyin-English. We comprehensively evaluate a diverse set of open-source code generation models ranging from 1.5B to 15B parameters. Our results show that code-mixed prompts consistently degrade Pass@1 performance compared to their English-only counterparts, with performance drops increasing under higher CMD levels for smaller models. CodeMixBench provides a realistic evaluation framework for studying multilingual code generation and highlights new challenges and directions for building robust code generation models that generalize well across diverse linguistic settings.
- **Summary**: This paper introduces CodeMixBench, a novel benchmark for evaluating the robustness of Large Language Models (LLMs) on code generation tasks when prompted with code-mixed instructions. Building on the existing BigCodeBench benchmark, CodeMixBench introduces controlled code-mixing (CMD) in the prompts, blending English with Hinglish, Spanish-English, and Chinese Pinyin-English. The authors evaluate several open-source code generation models (1.5B-15B parameters) across varying CMD levels, demonstrating that code-mixing consistently degrades performance, particularly for smaller models and higher CMD values. The paper highlights the importance of evaluating model robustness in multilingual settings, reflecting the real-world scenario of global software development.

**Critical Evaluation:**

The paper addresses a crucial gap in the existing code generation benchmarks, which predominantly focus on English-only prompts, thereby neglecting the linguistic diversity present in real-world programming environments. The introduction of CodeMixBench is valuable as it provides a more realistic evaluation framework for LLMs in code generation.

**Novelty:** The main novelty lies in the creation of a benchmark dataset specifically designed to evaluate LLMs on code generation with code-mixed prompts. While code-mixing has been explored in other NLP tasks, its application to code generation benchmarks is relatively new. The use of controlled code-mixing (CMD) allows for a more systematic analysis of the impact of code-mixing on model performance.

**Significance:** The findings of the paper are significant because they reveal the limitations of existing LLMs in handling code-mixed prompts. The performance degradation observed with code-mixing highlights the need for further research into developing robust code generation models that can generalize well across diverse linguistic settings. The benchmark provides a valuable tool for researchers to study and improve the multilingual capabilities of code generation models. The paper also reveals that simply scaling up model sizes or instruction tuning does not guarantee code-mixing robustness, as evidenced by some smaller models exhibiting greater resilience under these conditions due to diverse and large training datasets. This nuance further enhances the significance of their findings.

**Strengths:**
*   Clearly identifies a gap in existing code generation benchmarks.
*   Introduces a novel benchmark dataset with controlled code-mixing.
*   Provides a comprehensive evaluation of several LLMs on the proposed benchmark.
*   Analyzes the impact of code-mixing on model performance and identifies potential reasons for performance degradation.
*   Highlights the importance of multilingual and noisy pretraining.

**Weaknesses:**
*   The code-mixing is currently limited to instructions and docstrings. Real-world code-mixing can occur in variable names and comments within the code itself.
*   The study focuses on only three language pairs. Expanding the benchmark to other language pairs would increase its generalizability.
*   The evaluation is primarily based on functional correctness (Pass@1). Evaluating code quality, readability, and adherence to stylistic conventions would provide a more comprehensive assessment.
*   The GAME score is only used to verify the back-translation quality of code-mixed prompts and not the actual generation quality.

**Justification for Score:**

While the paper introduces a novel and valuable benchmark, there is room for improvement in its scope and evaluation metrics. The current version provides a solid foundation for future research in multilingual code generation, but the limitations mentioned above prevent it from being considered an exceptional contribution at this stage. The impact on the field hinges on how extensively the benchmark is adopted and how it catalyzes the development of more robust multilingual code generation models. It will likely drive future research by pointing out the existing deficiency. The paper demonstrates a solid, well-reasoned approach to addressing an important area of research. Therefore, the score reflects these strengths and weaknesses.

### WaterDrum: Watermarking for Data-centric Unlearning Metric
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.05064v1)
- **Authors**: Xinyang Lu, Xinyuan Niu, Gregory Kang Ruey Lau, Bui Thi Cam Nhung, Rachael Hwee Ling Sim, Fanyu Wen, Chuan-Sheng Foo, See-Kiong Ng, Bryan Kian Hsiang Low
- **Abstract**: Large language model (LLM) unlearning is critical in real-world applications where it is necessary to efficiently remove the influence of private, copyrighted, or harmful data from some users. However, existing utility-centric unlearning metrics (based on model utility) may fail to accurately evaluate the extent of unlearning in realistic settings such as when (a) the forget and retain set have semantically similar content, (b) retraining the model from scratch on the retain set is impractical, and/or (c) the model owner can improve the unlearning metric without directly performing unlearning on the LLM. This paper presents the first data-centric unlearning metric for LLMs called WaterDrum that exploits robust text watermarking for overcoming these limitations. We also introduce new benchmark datasets for LLM unlearning that contain varying levels of similar data points and can be used to rigorously evaluate unlearning algorithms using WaterDrum. Our code is available at https://github.com/lululu008/WaterDrum and our new benchmark datasets are released at https://huggingface.co/datasets/Glow-AI/WaterDrum-Ax.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces WaterDrum, a novel data-centric metric for evaluating LLM unlearning based on robust text watermarking. It addresses the limitations of existing utility-centric metrics, which often fail in real-world scenarios where the forget and retain sets have semantically similar content, retraining is impractical, or model owners can manipulate the metric without actual unlearning. The paper also presents WaterDrum-Ax, a new benchmark dataset designed to evaluate unlearning algorithms under these challenging conditions, including data from multiple parties and varying degrees of data similarity. Through experiments, the authors demonstrate that WaterDrum outperforms existing metrics in satisfying several important desiderata for an effective, practical, and resilient unlearning metric.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's primary novelty lies in proposing a data-centric unlearning metric that overcomes limitations of utility-centric approaches. Shifting focus from model performance to directly tracking the presence of data through watermarking is a valuable contribution. The WaterDrum-Ax dataset is also a novel contribution, directly designed to address the limitations in existing datasets for the problem of LLM unlearning. However, building upon the Waterfall framework of Lau et al. (2024) does decrease the innovation slightly.

*   **Significance:** The paper's significance stems from addressing critical issues in LLM unlearning evaluation. By providing a metric and dataset that are robust to real-world challenges (semantic similarity, lack of retraining access, and potential for manipulation), the authors enable more accurate and reliable assessment of unlearning algorithms. This contribution is particularly relevant given the increasing concerns about privacy, copyright, and the ethical implications of LLMs. The focus on query-access, which is practically relevant in many modern LLM applications, is another strength.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   Clear and well-defined desiderata for unlearning metrics.
    *   A practical and resilient data-centric approach based on watermarking.
    *   New benchmark dataset specifically designed to address existing limitations.
    *   Empirical validation demonstrating WaterDrum's superiority.
    *   Demonstration of potential weaknesses of commonly used utility-centric metrics.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The watermarking approach adds complexity to the training data preparation.
    *   The effectiveness of WaterDrum relies on the robustness of the underlying watermarking scheme. Future work could test this more rigorously.
    *   While the authors demonstrate resilience to one specific threat model, further analysis of other potential attacks and defenses is warranted.
    *   While WaterDrum provides a more data-centric perspective, it is important to note that watermarking, by its very nature, adds additional complexity to both the model training and evaluation pipeline.

*   **Impact:** The paper has the potential to significantly influence the field of LLM unlearning. The data-centric perspective and the benchmark dataset could facilitate the development of more effective and practical unlearning algorithms.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper makes a strong contribution to the field of LLM unlearning by identifying limitations in current unlearning metrics and presenting a new approach and dataset to address them. WaterDrum offers a more realistic and practical evaluation framework, which is crucial for advancing the development of effective unlearning techniques. However, there are some weaknesses due to the fact that it builds on previous works.

### Performance Evaluation of Large Language Models in Bangla Consumer Health Query Summarization
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.05070v1)
- **Authors**: Ajwad Abrar, Farzana Tabassum, Sabbir Ahmed
- **Abstract**: Consumer Health Queries (CHQs) in Bengali (Bangla), a low-resource language, often contain extraneous details, complicating efficient medical responses. This study investigates the zero-shot performance of nine advanced large language models (LLMs): GPT-3.5-Turbo, GPT-4, Claude-3.5-Sonnet, Llama3-70b-Instruct, Mixtral-8x22b-Instruct, Gemini-1.5-Pro, Qwen2-72b-Instruct, Gemma-2-27b, and Athene-70B, in summarizing Bangla CHQs. Using the BanglaCHQ-Summ dataset comprising 2,350 annotated query-summary pairs, we benchmarked these LLMs using ROUGE metrics against Bangla T5, a fine-tuned state-of-the-art model. Mixtral-8x22b-Instruct emerged as the top performing model in ROUGE-1 and ROUGE-L, while Bangla T5 excelled in ROUGE-2. The results demonstrate that zero-shot LLMs can rival fine-tuned models, achieving high-quality summaries even without task-specific training. This work underscores the potential of LLMs in addressing challenges in low-resource languages, providing scalable solutions for healthcare query summarization.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided research paper:

**Summary:**

The paper investigates the zero-shot performance of nine large language models (LLMs) for summarizing Bengali consumer health queries (CHQs). These queries often contain extraneous information, making it difficult for medical professionals to extract the most important details. The study uses the BanglaCHQ-Summ dataset, which consists of 2,350 query-summary pairs, to benchmark the LLMs using ROUGE metrics. The performance is compared against BanglaT5, a fine-tuned, state-of-the-art model. The results indicate that some zero-shot LLMs, particularly Mixtral-8x22b-Instruct, can rival the performance of fine-tuned models like BanglaT5, highlighting the potential of LLMs in addressing challenges in low-resource languages like Bengali, and improving healthcare query summarization.  The paper also analyzes summary length and quality, as well as the impact of pronunciation variations on the results.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:** The paper's primary novelty lies in being the first comprehensive study to evaluate several state-of-the-art LLMs specifically for Bengali CHQ summarization in a zero-shot setting. Previous work has explored LLMs for general medical summarization or in other languages, but this paper focuses on a specific low-resource language and a relevant domain.  While the methodology (using ROUGE) is standard, the application to this specific scenario is novel. The prompt engineering aspect, specifically for Bengali, contributes incremental value.

**Significance:** The work is significant for several reasons:

*   **Low-resource language focus:** It addresses the critical need for NLP solutions in under-represented languages like Bengali, where data and resources are limited.
*   **Healthcare application:**  CHQ summarization directly impacts healthcare accessibility and efficiency, especially in densely populated areas like Bangladesh where medical professionals face heavy workloads.
*   **Zero-shot learning:** Demonstrating that zero-shot LLMs can perform competitively with fine-tuned models is a major advantage, as it reduces the reliance on large, task-specific annotated datasets.
*   **Practical Impact:** The insights could be used to deploy LLM-based CHQ summarization tools for Bengali speakers, potentially improving the quality and speed of healthcare information access.

**Strengths:**

*   **Comprehensive Evaluation:** The paper evaluates a range of LLMs, including both proprietary and open-source models.
*   **Clear Methodology:** The methodology is clearly described, making the results reproducible.
*   **Relevant Dataset:** The use of the BanglaCHQ-Summ dataset ensures the study is grounded in real-world consumer health queries.
*   **Analysis of Errors:** It identifies an interesting issue regarding pronunciation variations and its impact on performance which is critical for Bengali specifically.
*   **Comparison to Baseline:**  The use of a fine-tuned BanglaT5 model provides a solid baseline for comparison.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Limited error analysis:**  While the study touches on pronunciation variability, a more detailed error analysis, including examples of common summarization errors and their causes, would strengthen the paper.  Analyzing failure cases, such as instances where the zero-shot LLMs significantly underperform compared to the fine-tuned model would be valuable.
*   **Dataset Limitations:** While the use of BanglaCHQ-Summ is good, the data comes from a health forum. It is likely that the complexity of the queries is limited. Summarizing CHQs that include more complex medical language would challenge the models further.
*   **Prompt engineering limitations:** While prompt engineering is discussed, it could be further elaborated with a more structured approach to finding the optimized prompt.
*   **Lack of Qualitative Evaluation:** The study relies primarily on ROUGE metrics. A qualitative evaluation, involving human assessment of the summaries' accuracy, relevance, and coherence, would provide a more comprehensive understanding of the LLMs' performance.
*   **Limited Generalizability Discussion:** The paper should consider the broader implications of these findings for other low-resource languages and healthcare domains.

**Justification for Score:**

I am assigning a score of **7**.

*   The study is well-executed, clearly written, and addresses a relevant problem.
*   The focus on Bengali and CHQ summarization is novel.
*   The demonstration of competitive performance from zero-shot LLMs is promising.
*   However, the limitations regarding error analysis, dataset complexity, and the absence of a qualitative evaluation somewhat limit the impact. The study does not explore the potential biases within the training data and their impact on the generated responses which could be important within the healthcare context. The paper could be strengthened through more advanced evaluation, error analysis and broader generalizability discussion.

### PIDiff: Image Customization for Personalized Identities with Diffusion Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.05081v1)
- **Authors**: Jinyu Gu, Haipeng Liu, Meng Wang, Yang Wang
- **Abstract**: Text-to-image generation for personalized identities aims at incorporating the specific identity into images using a text prompt and an identity image. Based on the powerful generative capabilities of DDPMs, many previous works adopt additional prompts, such as text embeddings and CLIP image embeddings, to represent the identity information, while they fail to disentangle the identity information and background information. As a result, the generated images not only lose key identity characteristics but also suffer from significantly reduced diversity. To address this issue, previous works have combined the W+ space from StyleGAN with diffusion models, leveraging this space to provide a more accurate and comprehensive representation of identity features through multi-level feature extraction. However, the entanglement of identity and background information in in-the-wild images during training prevents accurate identity localization, resulting in severe semantic interference between identity and background. In this paper, we propose a novel fine-tuning-based diffusion model for personalized identities text-to-image generation, named PIDiff, which leverages the W+ space and an identity-tailored fine-tuning strategy to avoid semantic entanglement and achieves accurate feature extraction and localization. Style editing can also be achieved by PIDiff through preserving the characteristics of identity features in the W+ space, which vary from coarse to fine. Through the combination of the proposed cross-attention block and parameter optimization strategy, PIDiff preserves the identity information and maintains the generation capability for in-the-wild images of the pre-trained model during inference. Our experimental results validate the effectiveness of our method in this task.
- **Summary**: Okay, I will provide a summary, critical evaluation, and score for the paper based on the provided text.

**Summary:**

The paper "PIDiff: Image Customization for Personalized Identities with Diffusion Models" proposes a novel fine-tuning-based diffusion model designed to generate personalized images from text prompts and a reference identity image. PIDiff aims to address the limitations of existing methods that struggle with disentangling identity information from background and text prompts, leading to loss of identity characteristics and reduced diversity. The key innovations include: (1) utilizing the W+ space of StyleGAN for more accurate and comprehensive identity feature extraction; (2) a Visual Guidance Module (VGM) to process reference images and provide visual prompts to the diffusion model; (3) Style Cross-Attention (SCA) to integrate visual prompts effectively; and (4) a customized fine-tuning strategy to avoid semantic entanglement. The authors also construct a small-scale dataset tailored for personalized identity customization. Experiments are conducted to demonstrate that PIDiff outperforms state-of-the-art methods in preserving identity, maintaining semantic consistency, and achieving higher image quality and diversity.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Addresses a relevant problem:** Personalized image generation is a rapidly growing area with significant practical applications.  The paper clearly identifies the limitations of current methods in disentangling identity and background information.
    *   **Technical novelty:** The proposed architecture, combining W+ space, VGM, SCA, and a customized fine-tuning strategy, represents a well-reasoned and potentially effective approach to address the identified limitations. The SCA module, in particular, seems like a clever way to incorporate visual prompts without disrupting text-image coherence.
    *   **Dataset:** The creation of a custom dataset specifically tailored for this task fills a gap in available resources and ensures more controlled experimentation.
    *   **Experimental validation:** The quantitative and qualitative results appear to support the claim that PIDiff outperforms existing methods, showing improvements in identity preservation, semantic consistency, and image quality. The ablation studies further validate the effectiveness of the individual components.
    *   **Style editing capability:** The ability to achieve style editing by manipulating the W+ vector is a valuable feature, adding to the versatility of the model.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited Dataset Size:** Although the paper mentions addressing high-quality face images, it needs to have more details on the dataset for identity image generation.
    *   **Incremental Contribution:** While the paper combines several existing techniques (StyleGAN's W+ space, diffusion models, attention mechanisms), the overall contribution could be seen as incremental. The novelty lies more in the specific combination and tailored training strategy than in revolutionary new components.
    *   **Dependency on StyleGAN:** The reliance on StyleGAN's W+ space might limit the applicability of the method to face images or domains where a StyleGAN representation is readily available or easily learned.
    *   **Lack of detail in implementation:** The paper states that the faces are aligned and the backgrounds removed, but it lacks further detail on it.
    *   **Small Parameter set:** The parameter count seems quite small in that only the mappings and SCAs are trainable. Further analysis is needed to investigate if the pre-trained SD parameters were sufficient.

*   **Significance:**

    *   The paper presents a practical and effective approach to personalized image generation, offering a potential improvement over existing methods.
    *   The insights into the challenges of disentangling identity and background information are valuable for the research community.
    *   The custom dataset could serve as a useful resource for future research in this area.
    *   The style editing capabilities broaden the potential applications of the model.

**Justification of Score:**

I am assigning a score of 7. This reflects a paper that has significant strengths in addressing a relevant problem with a technically sound and well-validated solution. The construction of the dataset is another positive attribute. However, the incremental nature of the contribution, potential limitations due to dependency on StyleGAN, and certain concerns surrounding the lack of implementation details slightly diminish the overall impact. Nevertheless, the paper presents a valuable contribution to the field of personalized image generation. The empirical validation, which clearly demonstrates the method’s advantages over SOTA methods, carries significant weight in the final score.

### ItDPDM: Information-Theoretic Discrete Poisson Diffusion Model
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.05082v1)
- **Authors**: Sagnik Bhattacharya, Abhiram R. Gorle, Ahmed Mohsin, Ahsan Bilal, Connor Ding, Amit Kumar Singh Yadav, Tsachy Weissman
- **Abstract**: Existing methods for generative modeling of discrete data, such as symbolic music tokens, face two primary challenges: (1) they either embed discrete inputs into continuous state-spaces or (2) rely on variational losses that only approximate the true negative log-likelihood. Previous efforts have individually targeted these limitations. While information-theoretic Gaussian diffusion models alleviate the suboptimality of variational losses, they still perform modeling in continuous domains. In this work, we introduce the Information-Theoretic Discrete Poisson Diffusion Model (ItDPDM), which simultaneously addresses both limitations by directly operating in a discrete state-space via a Poisson diffusion process inspired by photon arrival processes in camera sensors. We introduce a novel Poisson Reconstruction Loss (PRL) and derive an exact relationship between PRL and the true negative log-likelihood, thereby eliminating the need for approximate evidence lower bounds. Experiments conducted on the Lakh MIDI symbolic music dataset and the CIFAR-10 image benchmark demonstrate that ItDPDM delivers significant improvements, reducing test NLL by up to 80% compared to prior baselines, while also achieving faster convergence.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "ItDPDM: Information-Theoretic Discrete Poisson Diffusion Model":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces ItDPDM, a novel diffusion model for discrete data that operates directly in the discrete state-space using a Poisson diffusion process. Unlike many existing discrete diffusion models, ItDPDM avoids embedding discrete data into continuous spaces. It proposes a novel Poisson Reconstruction Loss (PRL) objective and shows an exact relationship between PRL and the true negative log-likelihood, eliminating the need for approximate evidence lower bounds (ELBOs).  Experimental results on the Lakh MIDI dataset and CIFAR-10 demonstrate significant improvements in negative log-likelihood compared to baselines, along with faster convergence.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies in the combination of a discrete-space diffusion model *with* an information-theoretic approach using a Poisson process.  Existing discrete diffusion models often rely on variational losses (approximations of the likelihood) or embedding into continuous spaces. ItDPDM tackles both these limitations simultaneously.  The PRL and its theoretical justification linking it to the true likelihood are also novel.  The application of Poisson noise, inspired by photon arrival processes, to discrete data generation is an interesting conceptual shift.

**Significance:** The demonstrated improvements in NLL and convergence speed suggest that ItDPDM could be a promising approach for generative modeling of inherently discrete data like symbolic music or images with discrete pixel values. The avoidance of ELBOs and the direct optimization of the likelihood are significant because they potentially lead to more accurate density estimation. The framework also offers a potential new way to view and model inherently discrete data compared to traditional gaussian-based diffusion models. The theoretical justifications, particularly regarding the I-MPRL relationship, provide a solid foundation for the proposed method.

**Strengths:**

*   **Strong Theoretical Foundation:** The paper provides a clear and rigorous mathematical derivation of the PRL and its relationship to the true negative log-likelihood.  The information-theoretic framework is well-articulated.
*   **Empirical Validation:**  The experimental results on two different datasets (music and images) provide evidence for the effectiveness of ItDPDM.  The comparison with baselines, including Gaussian diffusion models and variants, highlights the advantages of the proposed approach.
*   **Addresses Key Limitations:** ItDPDM effectively addresses the two primary limitations of existing methods for discrete data: suboptimal variational losses and continuous state space embedding.
*   **Faster convergence speed:** It offers faster convergence relative to other methods.
*   **Soundness of core argument:** The paper identifies several limitations in existing methods and successfully resolves these.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Computational Cost:** The paper mentions the computational cost of integral estimation, even with importance sampling. Addressing these practical complexities will be crucial for wider adoption. Details in "H. Theoretical Runtime Analysis of ItDPDM Architecture" imply a high computational complexity.
*   **Architecture-Specific Results:** The performance gains may be partly dependent on the specific architectures (U-Net, NCSN++) used in the experiments. Generalization to other architectures needs to be explored.
*   **Limited Scope:** The paper focuses on relatively simple datasets (CIFAR-10, Lakh MIDI).  Demonstrating its scalability and effectiveness on more complex datasets (e.g., ImageNet, larger music datasets with more intricate structure) would strengthen the impact.
*   **Incremental Contribution to pre-trained methods:** Compared to "pre-trained models like DDPM (Ho et al., 2020) and an Improved DDPM (IDDPM) (Nichol & Dhariwal, 2021)" ItDPDM appears to be weaker than these when not training from scratch.

**Potential Influence:**

ItDPDM has the potential to influence research in generative modeling of discrete data. The ideas of using Poisson diffusion and direct likelihood optimization could inspire new approaches in areas like:

*   Symbolic music generation
*   Discrete image generation
*   Natural language processing
*   Molecular modeling
*   Other structured data generation tasks

The theoretical framework and the PRL loss function could be valuable tools for developing more efficient and accurate discrete generative models.

**Justification of Score:**

Given the novelty of the Poisson diffusion approach in the discrete domain, the strong theoretical justifications, and the promising empirical results, but considering the computational complexities, limitations in scope and weaker results compared to training from scratch using existing algorithms, this is a significant, although not groundbreaking contribution. The addressed limitations are very relevant.

### Reliably Bounding False Positives: A Zero-Shot Machine-Generated Text Detection Framework via Multiscaled Conformal Prediction
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.05084v1)
- **Authors**: Xiaowei Zhu, Yubing Ren, Yanan Cao, Xixun Lin, Fang Fang, Yangxi Li
- **Abstract**: The rapid advancement of large language models has raised significant concerns regarding their potential misuse by malicious actors. As a result, developing effective detectors to mitigate these risks has become a critical priority. However, most existing detection methods focus excessively on detection accuracy, often neglecting the societal risks posed by high false positive rates (FPRs). This paper addresses this issue by leveraging Conformal Prediction (CP), which effectively constrains the upper bound of FPRs. While directly applying CP constrains FPRs, it also leads to a significant reduction in detection performance. To overcome this trade-off, this paper proposes a Zero-Shot Machine-Generated Text Detection Framework via Multiscaled Conformal Prediction (MCP), which both enforces the FPR constraint and improves detection performance. This paper also introduces RealDet, a high-quality dataset that spans a wide range of domains, ensuring realistic calibration and enabling superior detection performance when combined with MCP. Empirical evaluations demonstrate that MCP effectively constrains FPRs, significantly enhances detection performance, and increases robustness against adversarial attacks across multiple detectors and datasets.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "Reliably Bounding False Positives: A Zero-Shot Machine-Generated Text Detection Framework via Multiscaled Conformal Prediction":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces a novel zero-shot machine-generated text (MGT) detection framework based on multiscaled conformal prediction (MCP). It aims to address the problem of high false positive rates (FPRs) in existing MGT detection methods, which can lead to societal harm. The MCP framework leverages conformal prediction (CP) to provide a provable upper bound on the FPR while simultaneously improving detection performance.  The core idea is to derive multiscaled quantiles from the nonconformity scores of a basic detector on a calibration set, using these quantiles as thresholds for detection. The paper also presents a new large-scale bilingual benchmark dataset called RealDet, designed to better represent real-world MGT detection scenarios. Empirical results demonstrate that MCP effectively constrains FPRs, enhances detection performance, and improves robustness against adversarial attacks.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:** The paper presents a few notable aspects of novelty. Firstly, the application of conformal prediction to the task of MGT detection is itself a novel contribution, offering a statistically sound approach to controlling FPRs. Secondly, the multiscaled quantile calculation method, which incorporates text length information to improve detection performance is a good idea. Finally, the creation of the RealDet dataset is a substantial contribution. Existing datasets are often domain-specific and/or limited by outdated LLMs. RealDet, with its broad domain coverage and bilingual texts, offers a more realistic benchmark for MGT detection.

**Significance:** The paper addresses a critical challenge in the field of MGT detection: the trade-off between detection accuracy and FPR. High FPRs can have severe consequences, including unjustly accusing humans of generating machine-written text, which is an important consideration in deploying MGT detection systems in real-world settings. By offering a framework that provides a statistically guaranteed upper bound on FPR, the paper contributes to making MGT detection systems more trustworthy and reliable.

**Strengths:**

*   **Strong Theoretical Foundation:** The use of conformal prediction provides a solid theoretical basis for controlling FPRs.
*   **Practical Applicability:** The zero-shot nature of the MCP framework is advantageous, as it eliminates the need for retraining the model on new datasets.
*   **Improved Performance:** Empirical results demonstrate that MCP effectively constrains FPRs while simultaneously improving detection performance compared to using vanilla detectors.
*   **Comprehensive Evaluation:** The experiments cover a range of datasets, detectors, and adversarial attacks, providing a thorough evaluation of the MCP framework.
*   **High-Quality Dataset:** The RealDet dataset is a valuable resource for the MGT detection research community.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Binning Strategy:** The fixed-width binning strategy used for multiscaled quantile calculation may not be optimal. The authors acknowledge this limitation and suggest exploring more flexible binning strategies in future work.
*   **Performance with Weak Detectors:** The paper notes that MCP may not improve the performance of detectors with poor baseline performance.  This suggests that MCP is more effective as a refinement technique for already capable detectors.
*   **Limited Analysis of Calibration Methods:** While the paper compares MCP to other calibration methods, the analysis is not very thorough. More in-depth comparisons would be beneficial.

**Potential Influence:** The MCP framework has the potential to influence the development of more reliable and trustworthy MGT detection systems. The RealDet dataset will likely become a widely used benchmark for evaluating future MGT detection methods. The paper could spur further research into combining statistical guarantees with machine learning techniques for controlling FPRs in other natural language processing tasks.

**Justification for Score:**

While the paper presents solid contributions, a score of 7 reflects certain limitations. The idea of using conformal prediction for MGT detection is novel and impactful, and the RealDet dataset addresses an important need in the field. However, the specific implementation, particularly the fixed-width binning strategy, leaves room for improvement. The performance of MCP is also dependent on the quality of the underlying detector. These weaknesses prevent the paper from achieving a higher score, but the core ideas and contributions are valuable.
