### QualBench: Benchmarking Chinese LLMs with Localized Professional Qualifications for Vertical Domain Evaluation
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.05225v1)
- **Authors**: Mengze Hong, Wailing Ng, Di Jiang, Chen Jason Zhang
- **Abstract**: The rapid advancement of Chinese large language models (LLMs) underscores the need for domain-specific evaluations to ensure reliable applications. However, existing benchmarks often lack coverage in vertical domains and offer limited insights into the Chinese working context. Leveraging qualification exams as a unified framework for human expertise evaluation, we introduce QualBench, the first multi-domain Chinese QA benchmark dedicated to localized assessment of Chinese LLMs. The dataset includes over 17,000 questions across six vertical domains, with data selections grounded in 24 Chinese qualifications to closely align with national policies and working standards. Through comprehensive evaluation, the Qwen2.5 model outperformed the more advanced GPT-4o, with Chinese LLMs consistently surpassing non-Chinese models, highlighting the importance of localized domain knowledge in meeting qualification requirements. The best performance of 75.26% reveals the current gaps in domain coverage within model capabilities. Furthermore, we present the failure of LLM collaboration with crowdsourcing mechanisms and suggest the opportunities for multi-domain RAG knowledge enhancement and vertical domain LLM training with Federated Learning.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "QualBench: Benchmarking Chinese LLMs with Localized Professional Qualifications for Vertical Domain Evaluation":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces QualBench, a new multi-domain Chinese QA benchmark designed for evaluating Chinese Large Language Models (LLMs) in vertical domains.  QualBench uses data from 24 professional qualification exams across six different domains in mainland China. The authors argue that existing benchmarks lack coverage of vertical domains and provide limited insights into the Chinese working context.  Through comprehensive evaluation, they find that Chinese LLMs generally outperform non-Chinese LLMs, and the Qwen2.5 model specifically surpasses even GPT-4o in this context. They also analyze the failure of simple LLM crowdsourcing and suggest opportunities for future work in RAG and Federated Learning.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's primary novelty lies in the *creation of a new benchmark dataset* tailored to the Chinese context and leveraging professional qualification exams across multiple vertical domains.  This is a valuable contribution, as it directly addresses a identified gap in existing evaluation resources. The use of real-world qualification exams to assess LLMs makes the benchmark very grounded in the actual requirements of specific professions in China. A weakness of this dataset is, however, the emphasis on multiple choice, single choice and True/False answers only. Other existing benchmarks focused also on question generation tasks and open ended answering which are not evaluated in this paper.

*   **Significance:** The benchmark fills a crucial need for better evaluating LLMs' ability to meet specific domain requirements in the Chinese context.  The finding that Chinese LLMs outperform general-purpose models (including GPT-4o) when evaluated with localized knowledge highlights the importance of domain adaptation. The study also provides practical insights for deploying LLMs in real-world applications and offers directions for improving LLMs' domain knowledge and collaborative problem-solving capabilities. The paper also identifies that multi-LLM aggregations via basic voting may not be better than individual state-of-the-art models which shows a need for further improvement for LLM collaboration.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Dataset Design:** QualBench is well-motivated and systematically constructed, considering localization and domain coverage.
    *   **Evaluation:** The evaluation methodology is comprehensive and includes a comparison of Chinese and non-Chinese LLMs, as well as an attempt at LLM crowdsourcing.
    *   **Practical Implications:** The paper offers valuable insights for developing and deploying LLMs in China, highlighting the importance of domain-specific knowledge and localization.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Dataset Imbalance:** The dataset has a known class imbalance, which could skew the results.
    *   **Question Types Limited:** The dataset only incorporates multiple-choice and true/false questions, omitting open-ended question-answering and reasoning tasks, which could provide more nuanced evaluation of LLMs' capabilities.
    *   **LLM Crowdsourcing:** The method for LLM Crowdsourcing is too simple. More complex aggregation methods exist (e.g., learning to rank) which can be adopted in this paper.
    *   **Limited exploration of RAG/Fine-tuning:** The discussion of RAG and Federated Learning is very superficial and more of a future work proposal rather than an in-depth analysis. Experiments with a basic retrieval model would give some further insight to the reader. Fine-tuning the models on some of the qualification datasets is also an option.

*   **Potential Influence:** QualBench has the potential to become a widely used benchmark for evaluating Chinese LLMs, similar to other established benchmarks in the NLP field. It can also stimulate further research in domain adaptation, knowledge integration, and collaborative problem-solving for LLMs in specific cultural and regulatory contexts.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper presents a valuable and timely contribution by introducing QualBench. The benchmark addresses a real need for domain-specific and localized evaluation of Chinese LLMs. However, the limitations in dataset balance, question types, and limited exploration of RAG/Federated Learning prevent it from being a truly outstanding contribution. Despite these drawbacks, the paper's novel approach to benchmark creation and valuable insights warrant a positive evaluation. The benchmark's emphasis on professional qualification exams is very valuable in the assessment of human-aligned expertise.

### ChemRxivQuest: A Curated Chemistry Question-Answer Database Extracted from ChemRxiv Preprints
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.05232v1)
- **Authors**: Mahmoud Amiri, Thomas Bocklitz
- **Abstract**: The rapid expansion of chemistry literature poses significant challenges for researchers seeking to efficiently access domain-specific knowledge. To support advancements in chemistry-focused natural language processing (NLP), we present ChemRxivQuest, a curated dataset of 970 high-quality question-answer (QA) pairs derived from 155 ChemRxiv preprints across 17 subfields of chemistry. Each QA pair is explicitly linked to its source text segment to ensure traceability and contextual accuracy. ChemRxivQuest was constructed using an automated pipeline that combines optical character recognition (OCR), GPT-4o-based QA generation, and a fuzzy matching technique for answer verification. The dataset emphasizes conceptual, mechanistic, applied, and experimental questions, enabling applications in retrieval-based QA systems, search engine development, and fine-tuning of domain-adapted large language models. We analyze the dataset's structure, coverage, and limitations, and outline future directions for expansion and expert validation. ChemRxivQuest provides a foundational resource for chemistry NLP research, education, and tool development.
- **Summary**: Okay, I've analyzed the document and I'm ready to provide a summary, followed by a critical evaluation and score.

**Summary:**

The paper introduces ChemRxivQuest, a curated question-answer (QA) dataset specifically designed for chemistry-related natural language processing (NLP). The dataset comprises 970 high-quality QA pairs extracted from 155 ChemRxiv preprints, covering 17 subfields of chemistry. Each QA pair is explicitly linked to its source text within the preprints, ensuring traceability and contextual accuracy. The dataset creation process involved optical character recognition (OCR), GPT-4o-based QA generation, and a fuzzy matching technique for answer verification. The authors emphasize that the dataset emphasizes conceptual, mechanistic, applied, and experimental questions to enable applications like retrieval-based QA systems, search engine development, and fine-tuning of domain-adapted large language models (LLMs). The paper analyzes the dataset's structure, coverage, and limitations, and discusses future directions, including expert validation and expansion to peer-reviewed literature. The authors posit ChemRxivQuest as a foundational resource for chemistry NLP research, education, and tool development.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Strengths:**

*   **Novelty:** The key strength of the paper lies in the creation of a domain-specific QA dataset for chemistry, ChemRxivQuest. While scientific QA datasets exist (PubMedQA, SciQ, BioASQ, S2ORC), the authors convincingly argue that a dedicated dataset tailored to the specific demands of chemical language is currently lacking. This represents a *genuine* gap in the NLP resources available for chemistry.
*   **Quality:** The authors outline a systematic and reproducible pipeline for dataset construction, which instills a level of trust in the generated QA pairs. The integration of OCR, GPT-4o, and fuzzy matching, combined with manual validation of a subset, makes a reasonable attempt at quality control. The inclusion of contextual links back to source preprints is also a valuable asset for traceability and further validation.
*   **Coverage:** The dataset's coverage of 17 subfields of chemistry is commendable and aims to provide breadth within the dataset. Moreover, the deliberate emphasis on conceptual, mechanistic, applied, and experimental question types enhances the utility of the dataset across various application areas.
*   **Potential Applications:** The paper clearly articulates potential applications for ChemRxivQuest, including retrieval-based search engines, fine-tuning LLMs, benchmarking NLP models, educational resources, and analyzing knowledge drift in LLMs. This demonstrates a strong understanding of how the dataset can be leveraged to advance the field.
*   **Transparency:** The authors are transparent about the dataset's limitations, including potential model hallucination, biases within the source data, and the use of preprints as the primary source material. They explicitly outline avenues for future work to mitigate these limitations, such as expert validation and integration of peer-reviewed literature.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Reliance on LLMs for QA Generation:** While GPT-4o is a powerful tool, relying on it to generate QA pairs introduces the potential for errors, inconsistencies, and biases inherent in the model. The fuzzy matching verification strategy, while helpful, may not be sufficient to catch all instances of incorrect or contextually inappropriate answers, particularly concerning subtle semantic inaccuracies.
*   **Preprint Data Source:** The decision to use ChemRxiv preprints is a double-edged sword. While it provides access to recent research, preprints have not undergone peer review. This raises concerns about the reliability and validity of the information contained in the QA pairs. The dataset's utility will always be limited by the quality of the preprints it's based on.
*   **Lack of Expert Validation:** The manual validation was only performed on a subset of the retained QA pairs. Without more widespread expert validation from the chemistry community, the true accuracy and reliability of the dataset remains uncertain.
*   **Limited Dataset Size:** While 970 QA pairs are a good start, the size of the dataset is relatively small compared to other NLP datasets, even in specialized domains. This may limit its effectiveness for training and fine-tuning large language models.
*   **Evaluation Metrics:** The discussion around evaluation metrics, while acknowledging standard approaches, doesn't demonstrate how the dataset *itself* has been rigorously evaluated *beyond* the post-filtering accuracy estimation. Were standard QA evaluation pipelines implemented to assess its generalizability? Or to identify QA pairs that are particularly challenging?

**Significance:**

Despite its limitations, ChemRxivQuest represents a *significant step* towards addressing the lack of specialized NLP resources for chemistry. By providing a structured, validated QA dataset, the authors have created a valuable asset for researchers and practitioners in the field. The dataset has the potential to facilitate the development of more effective chemistry-specific NLP tools and applications. However, to maximize its impact, the dataset must be continuously improved through expert validation, expansion to peer-reviewed literature, and the implementation of more robust quality control measures.

**Overall Assessment and Score:**

ChemRxivQuest is a valuable contribution to the field of chemistry NLP, but its limitations prevent it from being a truly groundbreaking work. The reliance on preprints, the limited size, and the reliance on fuzzy matching (instead of semantic or expert-led validation) hold it back. Given these considerations,

**Score: 6.5**

- **Classification**: cs.AI
- **Score**: 6/10

### Latte: Transfering LLMs` Latent-level Knowledge for Few-shot Tabular Learning
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.05237v1)
- **Authors**: Ruxue Shi, Hengrui Gu, Hangting Ye, Yiwei Dai, Xu Shen, Xin Wang
- **Abstract**: Few-shot tabular learning, in which machine learning models are trained with a limited amount of labeled data, provides a cost-effective approach to addressing real-world challenges. The advent of Large Language Models (LLMs) has sparked interest in leveraging their pre-trained knowledge for few-shot tabular learning. Despite promising results, existing approaches either rely on test-time knowledge extraction, which introduces undesirable latency, or text-level knowledge, which leads to unreliable feature engineering. To overcome these limitations, we propose Latte, a training-time knowledge extraction framework that transfers the latent prior knowledge within LLMs to optimize a more generalized downstream model. Latte enables general knowledge-guided downstream tabular learning, facilitating the weighted fusion of information across different feature values while reducing the risk of overfitting to limited labeled data. Furthermore, Latte is compatible with existing unsupervised pre-training paradigms and effectively utilizes available unlabeled samples to overcome the performance limitations imposed by an extremely small labeled dataset. Extensive experiments on various few-shot tabular learning benchmarks demonstrate the superior performance of Latte, establishing it as a state-of-the-art approach in this domain
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "Latte: Transferring LLMs' Latent-level Knowledge for Few-shot Tabular Learning":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces Latte, a novel framework designed to improve few-shot tabular learning by transferring latent-level knowledge from large language models (LLMs). Latte addresses the limitations of existing approaches that either suffer from test-time latency or unreliable feature engineering due to text-level knowledge hallucination. The framework involves extracting task-relevant knowledge from LLMs using metadata, encoding tabular data with a semantic-aware encoder, and employing a knowledge adapter to fuse information from different feature values guided by the LLM's latent knowledge. Latte also incorporates an unsupervised pre-training stage to leverage unlabeled data, further enhancing its performance in extremely low-data scenarios. Experimental results on various benchmarks demonstrate Latte's superior performance compared to existing methods.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:**  The paper presents a relatively novel approach to few-shot tabular learning. While using LLMs for tabular data is not entirely new, Latte's key contribution lies in its training-time knowledge extraction, latent-level knowledge transfer, and the design of semantic-aware tabular encoder coupled with the knowledge adapter. The unsupervised pre-training stage to leverage unlabeled data is also a valuable addition. The core idea of transferring *latent* knowledge rather than relying on generated text is a crucial distinction and addresses a key weakness in some existing LLM-based methods.

*   **Significance:** Latte addresses a significant challenge in machine learning: effectively learning from tabular data with limited labeled samples. This is highly relevant in many real-world applications where annotation costs are high. The results demonstrate that Latte consistently outperforms other approaches on multiple benchmarks, which indicates its potential for practical applications. The efficiency advantage (low LLM invocation costs) is also a significant benefit. The ablation studies are well-conducted and provide insights into the importance of each component in the framework. The visualization of learned representations offers further evidence of Latte's effectiveness.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   Well-defined problem and clear motivation.
    *   Technically sound approach with a detailed description of the framework.
    *   Comprehensive experimental evaluation with multiple datasets and baselines.
    *   Strong ablation studies that validate the effectiveness of individual components.
    *   Demonstrated efficiency in terms of LLM invocation costs.
    *   Addresses a critical limitation of text-level knowledge reliance in LLM-based tabular learning (i.e., hallucinations).

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The specific choice of GTransformer in the knowledge adapter could be investigated further. Are there other architectures or design choices that would work even better? This isn't a major weakness, but it's a potential area for future research.
    *   The scale of LLMs explored could be broader. While the study includes 7B and 13B models, exploring larger models might reveal further performance gains or limitations.
    *   The paper could benefit from a more in-depth analysis of the types of tabular datasets and tasks for which Latte is most suitable. Are there specific characteristics of the data or task that make Latte particularly effective or ineffective?
    *   The novelty, while there, is partly an engineering one. It takes ideas that exist and combines them in a clever and useful way. This isn't inherently bad, but limits the fundamental novelty.

*   **Potential Influence:** Latte has the potential to influence future research in few-shot tabular learning, particularly in the development of more efficient and robust LLM-based methods. The idea of latent knowledge transfer is a valuable contribution and could inspire new approaches in other domains. The framework can be extended by incorporating other advanced techniques, such as contrastive learning or meta-learning algorithms.

**Justification for the Score:**

I'm assigning a score of 7.  While the paper demonstrates a clear improvement over existing methods and provides a well-engineered solution for a significant problem, there are limitations.  Latte builds upon existing techniques and the core novelty is the combination and refinement of those techniques, rather than introducing entirely new fundamental concepts.  The scale of LLMs investigated is also somewhat limited, and a deeper analysis of dataset characteristics would strengthen the paper. However, the well-defined approach, rigorous evaluation, and the focus on latent knowledge transfer make it a valuable contribution to the field.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 7/10

### T-T: Table Transformer for Tagging-based Aspect Sentiment Triplet Extraction
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.05271v1)
- **Authors**: Kun Peng, Chaodong Tong, Cong Cao, Hao Peng, Qian Li, Guanlin Wu, Lei Jiang, Yanbing Liu, Philip S. Yu
- **Abstract**: Aspect sentiment triplet extraction (ASTE) aims to extract triplets composed of aspect terms, opinion terms, and sentiment polarities from given sentences. The table tagging method is a popular approach to addressing this task, which encodes a sentence into a 2-dimensional table, allowing for the tagging of relations between any two words. Previous efforts have focused on designing various downstream relation learning modules to better capture interactions between tokens in the table, revealing that a stronger capability to capture relations can lead to greater improvements in the model. Motivated by this, we attempt to directly utilize transformer layers as downstream relation learning modules. Due to the powerful semantic modeling capability of transformers, it is foreseeable that this will lead to excellent improvement. However, owing to the quadratic relation between the length of the table and the length of the input sentence sequence, using transformers directly faces two challenges: overly long table sequences and unfair local attention interaction. To address these challenges, we propose a novel Table-Transformer (T-T) for the tagging-based ASTE method. Specifically, we introduce a stripe attention mechanism with a loop-shift strategy to tackle these challenges. The former modifies the global attention mechanism to only attend to a 2-dimensional local attention window, while the latter facilitates interaction between different attention windows. Extensive and comprehensive experiments demonstrate that the T-T, as a downstream relation learning module, achieves state-of-the-art performance with lower computational costs.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper "T-T: Table Transformer for Tagging-based Aspect Sentiment Triplet Extraction" proposes a novel Table-Transformer (T-T) architecture for the task of aspect sentiment triplet extraction (ASTE). ASTE aims to extract triplets of (aspect term, opinion term, sentiment polarity) from a sentence. The paper builds upon the table tagging approach, which represents a sentence as a 2D table, allowing for the modeling of relations between any two words.  T-T uses transformer layers as downstream relation learning modules but addresses the challenges of overly long table sequences (quadratic in the sentence length) and unfair local attention interaction. This is achieved via a "stripe attention mechanism" that restricts attention to a local window and a "loop-shift strategy" that facilitates interaction between different attention windows.  Experimental results demonstrate state-of-the-art performance with lower computational costs compared to existing methods.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The main novelty lies in the specific combination of stripe attention and loop-shift strategies within a Transformer-based architecture designed explicitly for table tagging in ASTE. While components like stripe attention (in some form) and windowed attention are not entirely new in the broader transformer literature, their adaptation and integration in this context, especially with the loop-shift mechanism, is the innovative aspect. The core idea is to address the computational burden of processing the large table created in table tagging methods and enhance the local interaction problem.
*   **Significance:**  ASTE is an important task in NLP for fine-grained sentiment analysis, as the paper claims. While the task itself has been the subject of research for some time, improvement in performance (state-of-the-art as is shown here) are valuable for real-world applications. However, the significance is somewhat limited by the focus on specific modules tailored to table-based approach, rather than a more generalizable sentiment analysis method.
*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Performance:**  The experimental results convincingly demonstrate the T-T module's superior performance compared to existing state-of-the-art methods in ASTE, showing improved F1 scores across several datasets.
    *   **Efficiency:** The paper effectively addresses the computational challenges associated with using transformers on large table sequences, demonstrating that T-T achieves competitive performance with lower computational costs.  The ablation studies validate the contribution of the proposed mechanisms.
    *   **Clarity:** The paper is well-written and clearly explains the proposed architecture and the motivation behind the design choices. The diagrams help clarify the functionality of the stripe attention and loop-shift strategies.
*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited Generalizability:**  The T-T architecture is specifically tailored to table tagging.  While effective, its direct applicability to other NLP tasks using standard sequence representations is limited.
    *   **Incremental Improvement:** While the improvements over the baselines are measurable, it's an incremental improvement on existing SOTA methods rather than a disruptive breakthrough. The contribution is a well-engineered solution to a particular architectural choice.
    *   **Parameter Explosion:** Transformer-based models generally suffer from high parameter counts. Although this paper mitigates computational costs, it doesn't address the underlying issue of model size.

*   **Justification of Score:**

The paper provides a valuable contribution to the specific area of table-based ASTE. Its main strength is the clever engineering of a Transformer-based architecture to address computational challenges and improve performance on this specific task. However, the method is quite specialized and its generalizability is questionable.  There is significant engineering work to balance performance and cost. The performance gains, while SOTA, are incremental. I assign it a score of 7 because it addresses a real problem, presents a well-engineered solution with demonstrated performance improvement. However, I penalize because of the limited generalizability and incremental nature of the advance.

**Score: 7**

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Software Development Life Cycle Perspective: A Survey of Benchmarks for CodeLLMs and Agents
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.05283v1)
- **Authors**: Kaixin Wang, Tianlin Li, Xiaoyu Zhang, Chong Wang, Weisong Sun, Yang Liu, Bin Shi
- **Abstract**: Code large language models (CodeLLMs) and agents have shown great promise in tackling complex software engineering tasks.Compared to traditional software engineering methods, CodeLLMs and agents offer stronger abilities, and can flexibly process inputs and outputs in both natural and code. Benchmarking plays a crucial role in evaluating the capabilities of CodeLLMs and agents, guiding their development and deployment. However, despite their growing significance, there remains a lack of comprehensive reviews of benchmarks for CodeLLMs and agents. To bridge this gap, this paper provides a comprehensive review of existing benchmarks for CodeLLMs and agents, studying and analyzing 181 benchmarks from 461 relevant papers, covering the different phases of the software development life cycle (SDLC). Our findings reveal a notable imbalance in the coverage of current benchmarks, with approximately 60% focused on the software development phase in SDLC, while requirements engineering and software design phases receive minimal attention at only 5% and 3%, respectively. Additionally, Python emerges as the dominant programming language across the reviewed benchmarks. Finally, this paper highlights the challenges of current research and proposes future directions, aiming to narrow the gap between the theoretical capabilities of CodeLLMs and agents and their application in real-world scenarios.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper provides a comprehensive survey of benchmarks for CodeLLMs and agents, viewed from the perspective of the Software Development Life Cycle (SDLC). It analyzes 181 benchmarks from 461 papers, categorizing them according to the different phases of the SDLC (Requirements Engineering, Design, Software Development, Testing, and Maintenance). The authors find an imbalance in coverage, with a significant focus on the software development phase and limited attention to the earlier phases like Requirements Engineering and Design.  They also analyze benchmark usage, programming languages, and the evolution of benchmarks towards more realistic scenarios. Finally, they highlight the limitations of existing benchmarks and propose future directions for research, including standardized benchmarks, cross-phase evaluations, non-functional requirements, multi-modal benchmarks, multi-agent systems, and code-centric evaluation frameworks.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Strengths:**

*   **Comprehensive Scope:** The paper provides a valuable service to the community by systematically organizing and categorizing a large number of benchmarks.  The SDLC lens is a useful framework for understanding the strengths and weaknesses of the current evaluation landscape.
*   **Clear Identification of Gaps:** The paper convincingly demonstrates the imbalance in benchmark coverage across different SDLC phases. Highlighting the neglect of Requirements Engineering and Design phases is a crucial observation.
*   **Forward-Looking Recommendations:** The proposed future directions are well-reasoned and address the identified limitations. Suggestions like focusing on non-functional requirements, multi-modal inputs, and human-model collaboration are particularly relevant.
*   **Well-Structured and Readable:** The paper is logically organized and clearly written, making it accessible to a broad audience.
*   **Reproducibility is well articulated:** The inclusion of all papers and search terms makes it easy to reproduce and validate.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Limited Depth of Analysis for Individual Benchmarks:** While the paper covers a large number of benchmarks, the individual descriptions of each benchmark are relatively brief. A more in-depth analysis of the individual tasks and metrics within each benchmark could have been helpful.
*   **Potential for Subjectivity in Categorization:** Classifying benchmarks into specific SDLC phases can be subjective, as some benchmarks may touch upon multiple phases. More detailed explanations or examples for the categorization decisions would strengthen the argument.

**Novelty and Significance:**

The novelty lies in the systematic application of the SDLC framework to analyze the landscape of CodeLLM benchmarks. This approach is significant because it highlights the practical implications of evaluation practices and offers a structured roadmap for future benchmark development. The paper successfully identifies the areas where research is lacking and suggests directions that align with the evolving needs of real-world software engineering. The comprehensive analysis and forward-looking recommendations make it a valuable resource for researchers and practitioners in the field.
The novelty and contribution are high, but some points lack specific detail (e.g. categorization decisions).
Overall a well constructed and valuable survey with a new take on existing benchmarks.

Score: 8

- **Classification**: cs.SE
- **Score**: 8/10

### HEXGEN-TEXT2SQL: Optimizing LLM Inference Request Scheduling for Agentic Text-to-SQL Workflow
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.05286v1)
- **Authors**: You Peng, Youhe Jiang, Chen Wang, Binhang Yuan
- **Abstract**: Recent advances in leveraging the agentic paradigm of large language models (LLMs) utilization have significantly enhanced Text-to-SQL capabilities, enabling users without specialized database expertise to query data intuitively. However, deploying these agentic LLM-based Text-to-SQL systems in production poses substantial challenges due to their inherently multi-stage workflows, stringent latency constraints, and potentially heterogeneous GPU infrastructure in enterprise environments. Current LLM serving frameworks lack effective mechanisms for handling interdependent inference tasks, dynamic latency variability, and resource heterogeneity, leading to suboptimal performance and frequent service-level objective (SLO) violations. In this paper, we introduce HEXGEN-TEXT2SQL, a novel framework designed explicitly to schedule and execute agentic multi-stage LLM-based Text-to-SQL workflows on heterogeneous GPU clusters that handle multi-tenant end-to-end queries. HEXGEN-TEXT2SQL introduce a hierarchical scheduling approach combining global workload-balanced task dispatching and local adaptive urgency-guided prioritization, guided by a systematic analysis of agentic Text-to-SQL workflows. Additionally, we propose a lightweight simulation-based method for tuning critical scheduling hyperparameters, further enhancing robustness and adaptability. Our extensive evaluation on realistic Text-to-SQL benchmarks demonstrates that HEXGEN-TEXT2SQL significantly outperforms state-of-the-art LLM serving frameworks. Specifically, HEXGEN-TEXT2SQL reduces latency deadlines by up to 1.67$\times$ (average: 1.41$\times$) and improves system throughput by up to 1.75$\times$ (average: 1.65$\times$) compared to vLLM under diverse, realistic workload conditions. Our code is available at https://github.com/Relaxed-System-Lab/Hexgen-Flow.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces HEXGEN-TEXT2SQL, a novel framework for optimizing the scheduling of large language model (LLM) inference requests within agentic Text-to-SQL workflows in heterogeneous GPU clusters. The system addresses challenges like multi-stage dependencies, latency variability, and resource heterogeneity. HEXGEN-TEXT2SQL employs a hierarchical scheduling approach combining global workload-balanced task dispatching and local adaptive urgency-guided prioritization.  A simulation-based method tunes scheduling hyperparameters.  The paper presents experimental results showing improved latency and throughput compared to vLLM in realistic Text-to-SQL benchmarks.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:**

The paper's primary novelty lies in its holistic approach to scheduling agentic LLM-based Text-to-SQL workflows. While individual components (workload balancing, priority queuing) are not entirely new, their integration within a hierarchical framework tailored to the specific characteristics of Text-to-SQL pipelines is a valuable contribution. The detailed analysis of Text-to-SQL workflows and the identification of key challenges related to dependency management, heterogeneity, and SLO guarantees are important and well-argued. The simulator-driven hyperparameter tuning adds a practical aspect for adapting to diverse workloads.

*   **Strengths:** The integration of different scheduling techniques specifically to address the needs of the TEXT2SQL agentic pipeline is a novel and impactful aspect of this work. Also, the experimental setup seems realistic and makes the results of the experiment more reliable.

*   **Weaknesses:**  The reliance on known techniques (workload balancing, priority queues) somewhat limits the groundbreaking nature of the work. While the paper claims novelty in the "holistic approach", a thorough comparison to alternative scheduling strategies customized to the Text-to-SQL scenario but utilizing different combinations would strengthen the claims. The paper also does not describe the training process of its performance model that provides estimates on the compute requirements.

**Significance:**

The paper addresses a crucial problem in deploying LLM-based Text-to-SQL systems in production.  Efficient scheduling is essential for achieving acceptable latency and throughput, particularly in resource-constrained or heterogeneous environments.  The reported improvements in latency deadlines and throughput demonstrate the practical significance of HEXGEN-TEXT2SQL.

*   **Strengths:** The paper has clear practical implications for enterprises adopting agentic LLM-based Text-to-SQL systems. The performance gains are substantial and demonstrate the value of the proposed framework. The framework's adaptability to heterogeneous GPU clusters is a significant advantage.
*   **Weaknesses:** While the paper claims the benefit in multitenant environment, this is not rigorously evaluated experimentally. The experimental comparisons are limited to vLLM. Comparisons to other state-of-the-art LLM serving systems (TGI, TensorRT-LLM) would strengthen the evidence of superiority. The paper does not discuss failure modes of the framework and the limitations of applying a heuristic driven model.

**Potential Influence:**

The paper has the potential to influence the design and implementation of future LLM serving systems, especially those targeting complex, multi-stage workflows.  The insights gained from analyzing Text-to-SQL pipelines can be generalized to other agentic LLM applications.  The hierarchical scheduling approach and the simulator-driven hyperparameter tuning may serve as valuable blueprints for researchers and practitioners.

**Justification for the Score:**

The paper presents a valuable engineering solution to a real-world problem. While not revolutionary in its individual components, the integrated system is well-motivated, rigorously evaluated, and demonstrates significant performance improvements. The paper fills a gap in current LLM serving systems by focusing on complex, multi-stage workflows with dependencies. The experimental setup seems realistic and shows tangible improvements on the performance. Also, the work is accessible as they released the source code as relaxed-system-lab/hexgen-flow. However, the lack of the comparison to other existing works and the limited multitenant experimental evaluation limit the strength of the claims. It is difficult to say whether this proposed system is better in production environment, considering the multitenant setup.
I assess the paper as:

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.DB
- **Score**: 7/10

### Benchmarking Ophthalmology Foundation Models for Clinically Significant Age Macular Degeneration Detection
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.05291v1)
- **Authors**: Benjamin A. Cohen, Jonathan Fhima, Meishar Meisel, Baskin Meital, Luis Filipe Nakayama, Eran Berkowitz, Joachim A. Behar
- **Abstract**: Self-supervised learning (SSL) has enabled Vision Transformers (ViTs) to learn robust representations from large-scale natural image datasets, enhancing their generalization across domains. In retinal imaging, foundation models pretrained on either natural or ophthalmic data have shown promise, but the benefits of in-domain pretraining remain uncertain. To investigate this, we benchmark six SSL-pretrained ViTs on seven digital fundus image (DFI) datasets totaling 70,000 expert-annotated images for the task of moderate-to-late age-related macular degeneration (AMD) identification. Our results show that iBOT pretrained on natural images achieves the highest out-of-distribution generalization, with AUROCs of 0.80-0.97, outperforming domain-specific models, which achieved AUROCs of 0.78-0.96 and a baseline ViT-L with no pretraining, which achieved AUROCs of 0.68-0.91. These findings highlight the value of foundation models in improving AMD identification and challenge the assumption that in-domain pretraining is necessary. Furthermore, we release BRAMD, an open-access dataset (n=587) of DFIs with AMD labels from Brazil.
- **Summary**: Okay, I will provide a summary, a critical evaluation, and a score for the paper "Benchmarking Ophthalmology Foundation Models for Clinically Significant Age Macular Degeneration Detection."

**Summary:**

This paper benchmarks several self-supervised learning (SSL) pre-trained Vision Transformers (ViTs) for identifying intermediate-to-late Age-related Macular Degeneration (AMD) using digital fundus images (DFIs).  The authors evaluated models pre-trained on natural images and ophthalmic images across multiple datasets.  They found that iBOT, pre-trained on natural images, achieved superior out-of-distribution (OOD) generalization performance compared to domain-specific pre-trained models.  The best performing model was fine-tuned using a multi-source domain training approach resulting in their model called AMDNet.  They also released a new open-access DFI dataset with AMD labels from Brazil called BRAMD. The authors compared AMDNet to a SOTA open-source model and found AMDNet had superior performance.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper makes several contributions, but the degree of novelty is debatable.

    *   The comprehensive benchmarking is valuable.  While benchmarking *per se* isn't novel, the specific models and datasets used, combined with the focus on OOD generalization within the AMD detection context, provide a useful resource for researchers.  It directly addresses a crucial question: does in-domain pre-training always improve performance in medical imaging? The finding that a natural image pre-trained model (iBOT) outperforms ophthalmic-specific models is noteworthy, and challenges current assumptions.

    *   The introduction of the BRAMD dataset is a positive contribution.  Increasing the availability of labeled medical imaging data is always beneficial, particularly when it comes from diverse populations or imaging conditions, and can drive downstream research. However, the number of images in BRAMD (n=587) is relatively small compared to other datasets used in the study (e.g., AREDS).

    *   The AMDNet model, while demonstrating good performance, isn't a radical departure from existing architectures or training methodologies. The core novelty lies in identifying the iBOT backbone and using multi-source training, rather than introducing a completely new architecture.

*   **Significance:** The paper has several aspects contributing to its significance.

    *   Highlighting the importance of OOD generalization:  The paper underscores the need to evaluate models beyond in-domain performance, which is crucial for real-world clinical applicability where variations in patient populations, imaging protocols, and equipment are inevitable.

    *   Challenging in-domain pre-training: The finding that a natural image pre-trained model outperforms ophthalmic-specific models is significant, because it suggests that simply scaling up pre-training data on natural images could potentially yield benefits, without the need for labor-intensive ophthalmic data annotation.

    *   Resource for future research: The benchmark results provide a baseline for future AMD detection models and serve as a reference for the community.  The release of BRAMD is also helpful.
    *   Error Analysis: The error analysis, while basic, provides valuable insights into the model's performance limitations, which is important for guiding future improvements.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   The size of the BRAMD dataset is limited, potentially reducing its impact.
    *   The error analysis could be more in-depth, examining specific image characteristics that lead to misclassifications.
    *   The study mainly looks at the binary AMD/non-AMD classification. A more granular classification into AMD severity stages would have more clinical relevance.
    *   The choice of datasets while broad in number, lack external validation which is a key factor in measuring how the model will perform when used on new unseeen datasets.

*   **Potential Impact:**

    *   The paper's findings could influence the design and evaluation of future AMD detection models, encouraging researchers to prioritize OOD generalization and consider non-domain-specific pre-training strategies.
    *   The BRAMD dataset could facilitate further research on AMD detection, particularly for models trained on diverse populations.
    *   The AMDNet model serves as a strong baseline for future comparison and potential clinical deployment.

*   **Rigour:** The experimental setup appears sound. Datasets, models, and training procedure are clearly described. Bootstrapping and statistical testing are utilized for result validation.

**Overall:** The paper presents a comprehensive benchmark study with a valuable OOD generalization focus, challenging conventional wisdom regarding the importance of in-domain pre-training. While the individual components (benchmarking, dataset release, model development) may not be groundbreaking, their combination provides a useful contribution to the field of ophthalmic image analysis and the study of AMD.

**Score: 7**

*Justification:*

The paper provides useful and actionable information by debunking the myth that domain-specific pretraining is always better when using foundation models. The release of a new dataset is a plus, but the value is diminished by its size. Although AMDNet performs well it still lags behind human-level detection which limits the significance. This combined with only looking at OOD generalization for binary classification and a limited error analysis makes me believe a score of 7 to be justified.

- **Classification**: eess.IV
- **Score**: 7/10

### Toward Reasonable Parrots: Why Large Language Models Should Argue with Us by Design
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.05298v1)
- **Authors**: Elena Musi, Nadin Kokciyan, Khalid Al-Khatib, Davide Ceolin, Emmanuelle Dietz, Klara Gutekunst, Annette Hautli-Janisz, Cristian Manuel Santibañez Yañez, Jodi Schneider, Jonas Scholz, Cor Steging, Jacky Visser, Henning Wachsmuth
- **Abstract**: In this position paper, we advocate for the development of conversational technology that is inherently designed to support and facilitate argumentative processes. We argue that, at present, large language models (LLMs) are inadequate for this purpose, and we propose an ideal technology design aimed at enhancing argumentative skills. This involves re-framing LLMs as tools to exercise our critical thinking rather than replacing them. We introduce the concept of 'reasonable parrots' that embody the fundamental principles of relevance, responsibility, and freedom, and that interact through argumentative dialogical moves. These principles and moves arise out of millennia of work in argumentation theory and should serve as the starting point for LLM-based technology that incorporates basic principles of argumentation.
- **Summary**: Okay, here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

The paper "Toward Reasonable Parrots: Why Large Language Models Should Argue with Us by Design" advocates for a shift in the design of large language models (LLMs). Instead of simply mimicking language patterns (being "stochastic parrots"), LLMs should be designed as "reasonable parrots" that actively engage users in argumentative processes. This involves incorporating principles of relevance, responsibility, and freedom into their interactions. The authors argue that current LLMs fail to facilitate critical thinking and deliberation, and they propose a design where LLMs embody different argumentative roles (Socratic, Cynical, Eclectic, Aristotelian parrots) to foster more comprehensive and critical user reflection.  The authors illustrate their concept through an example dialogue and contrast it with the output of existing LLMs like ChatGPT. They argue this allows LLMs to better foster deliberation and critical thought by engaging users in productive, dialogical argument.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's primary contribution lies in its framing of LLM design within the context of argumentation theory and its emphasis on critical thinking as a core design principle. While the idea of AI-assisted deliberation isn't entirely new, the explicit integration of well-established argumentation principles (relevance, responsibility, freedom) and distinct argumentative personas ("reasonable parrots") is a relatively novel approach. Existing work often focuses on factuality, reasoning accuracy, or mitigating biases, but this paper emphasizes the *process* of deliberation itself and how LLMs can facilitate it.

*   **Significance:** If implemented, the ideas presented could have significant implications.
    *   **Improved Critical Thinking:** By actively engaging users in argument, LLMs could promote better understanding of complex issues and reduce susceptibility to misinformation.
    *   **Enhanced Decision-Making:**  AI assistants designed around argumentation could lead to more informed and reasoned choices in various domains, from personal decisions to policy-making.
    *   **Ethical Considerations:** The focus on responsible argument and diverse perspectives is crucial in addressing potential biases and harms associated with LLMs.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Clear Problem Definition:** The paper effectively articulates the limitations of current LLMs in promoting critical thinking.
    *   **Well-Grounded in Theory:** The proposal is rooted in established argumentation theory, providing a solid conceptual foundation.
    *   **Concrete Examples:** The example dialogue helps to illustrate the proposed design in practice.
    *   **Thought-Provoking:** The paper raises important questions about the role of AI in human reasoning and decision-making.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Limited Technical Detail:** The paper is primarily conceptual. It lacks a detailed technical specification of how the "reasonable parrot" design would be implemented. More specific implementation suggestions are needed.
    *   **Evaluation Methodology:** There's no formal evaluation presented. The paper would be strengthened by a proposed methodology for assessing the effectiveness of the "reasonable parrot" design in fostering critical thinking and deliberation. A thorough evaluation is needed to ensure it does not produce the reverse effect by overwhelming, confusing or disengaging users, or by increasing polarization.
    *   **Oversimplification:** The proposed personas, while illustrative, may be too simplistic to capture the full complexity of human argumentation. How they would react to unexpected, conflicting, or malicious user input is also undefined.
    *   **Feasibility Concerns:** Implementing a truly "reasonable" parrot that can engage in nuanced argument and avoid fallacies poses significant technical challenges, especially in dealing with open-ended, real-world topics. The inherent "stochastic parrot" nature of LLMs can make this challenging.

*   **Potential Impact:** The paper has the potential to influence the direction of LLM research, shifting the focus from pure generation to active facilitation of critical thinking. It could inspire the development of new AI tools that promote more reasoned and informed discourse. However, realizing this impact will depend on addressing the technical and evaluative challenges outlined above.

**Score: 6.5**

**Rationale:**

The paper presents a valuable and novel perspective on LLM design, grounded in argumentation theory. Its conceptual clarity and thought-provoking arguments warrant a solid score. However, the lack of technical details and concrete evaluation significantly limits its immediate impact. The proposed solution, while promising, remains at the conceptual stage and faces considerable implementation challenges. Therefore, the score reflects a promising idea with significant potential, but which still requires substantial development and validation to realize its full impact.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 6/10

### ICon: In-Context Contribution for Automatic Data Selection
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.05327v1)
- **Authors**: Yixin Yang, Qingxiu Dong, Linli Yao, Fangwei Zhu, Zhifang Sui
- **Abstract**: Data selection for instruction tuning is essential for improving the performance of Large Language Models (LLMs) and reducing training cost. However, existing automated selection methods either depend on computationally expensive gradient-based measures or manually designed heuristics, which may fail to fully exploit the intrinsic attributes of data. In this paper, we propose In-context Learning for Contribution Measurement (ICon), a novel gradient-free method that takes advantage of the implicit fine-tuning nature of in-context learning (ICL) to measure sample contribution without gradient computation or manual indicators engineering. ICon offers a computationally efficient alternative to gradient-based methods and reduces human inductive bias inherent in heuristic-based approaches. ICon comprises three components and identifies high-contribution data by assessing performance shifts under implicit learning through ICL. Extensive experiments on three LLMs across 12 benchmarks and 5 pairwise evaluation sets demonstrate the effectiveness of ICon. Remarkably, on LLaMA3.1-8B, models trained on 15% of ICon-selected data outperform full datasets by 5.42% points and exceed the best performance of widely used selection methods by 2.06% points. We further analyze high-contribution samples selected by ICon, which show both diverse tasks and appropriate difficulty levels, rather than just the hardest ones.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces ICON (In-Context Contribution for Measurement), a novel data selection method for instruction tuning of large language models (LLMs). ICON leverages the implicit fine-tuning nature of in-context learning (ICL) to measure the contribution of each training sample without relying on gradient-based measures or manually designed heuristics. ICON comprises three components: constructing an assessment set, quantifying contribution via ICON scores, and training a selection paradigm. The paper presents extensive experiments on three LLMs across 12 benchmarks and 5 pairwise evaluation sets, demonstrating that models trained on a small fraction of ICON-selected data outperform those trained on the full dataset. The paper also analyzes the properties of high-contribution samples selected by ICON, showing a preference for diverse tasks and appropriate difficulty levels.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:**

The core novelty lies in the application of in-context learning principles to data selection. While ICL is a well-established concept, using it to *measure the contribution of individual training samples* for automated data selection is a unique approach.  The paper moves beyond handcrafted features and computationally expensive gradient calculations.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Gradient-free & Bias-reduced:** ICON presents a practical gradient-free data selection method. This is important for scenarios with limited computational resources or those where gradient calculations are unstable. ICON leverages ICL to capture intrinsic data attributes, circumventing reliance on predefined heuristics.
    *   **ICL Analogy:** ICL is a strong and well-reasoned justification for the sample scoring method.
    *   **Comprehensive Experiments:** The experimental setup is extensive, covering multiple LLMs, benchmarks, and evaluation metrics. This provides a solid foundation for demonstrating the effectiveness of the method.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Assessment Set Dependency:** The method's effectiveness hinges on the quality and representativeness of the assessment set. The method relies on samples from ChatGPT, GPT-4, and a human dataset. While the paper mentions these sources to ensure diversity, further work is needed to characterize how sensitive ICON is to the composition of this assessment set and provide robustness against variations.
    *   **LoRA Dependency:** ICON trains the ICON-guided selection with LoRA, while reducing training cost and complexity, it introduces another hyperparameter into the mix.
    *   **Metric Choice:** The perplexity-based metric to quantify contribution can be sensitive to various factors. Though there is a baseline perplexity adjustment, it introduces additional complexity.

**Significance:**

Data selection is crucial for efficient LLM training. ICON offers a valuable alternative to existing methods by reducing computational cost and human inductive bias. The performance improvements demonstrated in the experiments, particularly the significant outperformance on LLaMA3.1-8B, showcase the practical potential of ICON. The analyses of high-contribution samples provide valuable insights into the characteristics of effective instruction tuning data.
**Detailed Rationale**
The contribution of the paper is novel because it uses ICL in data selection. Gradient-free and bias-reduced properties are desirable in the data selection process. Also, the experiments are comprehensive. However, the assessment set and LoRA dependencies, and the reliance on perplexity-based scoring makes the method vulnerable to hyperparameters and introduces unnecessary complexities. Considering the pros and cons, the contribution does provide a good direction in terms of data selection and the comprehensive experiment. The significance is meaningful in this task, and the results are practical.

**Score: 7**

**Justification:** The paper presents a novel and well-executed approach to data selection for instruction tuning. The gradient-free nature and demonstrated performance improvements are significant advantages. However, the dependency on assessment set quality, the metric complexity, and the LoRA implementation (which requires hyperparameter tuning), slightly detracts from the overall novelty and robustness. A higher score would be warranted with a more thorough exploration of the assessment set's sensitivity and more robust implementations.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Denoising Diffusion Probabilistic Models for Coastal Inundation Forecasting
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.05381v1)
- **Authors**: Kazi Ashik Islam, Zakaria Mehrab, Mahantesh Halappanavar, Henning Mortveit, Sridhar Katragadda, Jon Derek Loftis, Madhav Marathe
- **Abstract**: Coastal flooding poses significant risks to communities, necessitating fast and accurate forecasting methods to mitigate potential damage. To approach this problem, we present DIFF-FLOOD, a probabilistic spatiotemporal forecasting method designed based on denoising diffusion models. DIFF-FLOOD predicts inundation level at a location by taking both spatial and temporal context into account. It utilizes inundation levels at neighboring locations and digital elevation data as spatial context. Inundation history from a context time window, together with additional co-variates are used as temporal context. Convolutional neural networks and cross-attention mechanism are then employed to capture the spatiotemporal dynamics in the data. We trained and tested DIFF-FLOOD on coastal inundation data from the Eastern Shore of Virginia, a region highly impacted by coastal flooding. Our results show that, DIFF-FLOOD outperforms existing forecasting methods in terms of prediction performance (6% to 64% improvement in terms of two performance metrics) and scalability.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "Denoising Diffusion Probabilistic Models for Coastal Inundation Forecasting":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces DIFF-FLOOD, a novel probabilistic spatiotemporal forecasting method for coastal inundation based on denoising diffusion models. DIFF-FLOOD predicts inundation levels by considering both spatial context (neighboring inundation levels and digital elevation data) and temporal context (historical inundation data and other relevant covariates like tidal cycles). The model uses convolutional neural networks and a cross-attention mechanism to capture the complex spatiotemporal dynamics in the data.  The authors trained and tested DIFF-FLOOD on data from the Eastern Shore of Virginia and showed that it outperforms existing forecasting methods in terms of prediction performance and scalability. The paper also demonstrates the model's utility in answering scenario-based queries of interest to policymakers.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:**  The application of diffusion models to coastal inundation forecasting is a novel approach.  While diffusion models have been used for time-series forecasting and spatiotemporal prediction, the specific adaptation and optimization for high-resolution coastal inundation with the integration of both spatial and temporal context appears to be a significant contribution. The proposed model and its demonstrated ability to provide probabilistic forecasts at a fine spatial scale, suitable for decision making are strengths.

*   **Significance:** Coastal inundation forecasting is a practically important problem. Improved forecasting methods can lead to better preparedness and mitigation strategies, reducing potential damage to communities.  The demonstrated performance of DIFF-FLOOD in comparison to existing methods highlights its potential to improve real-world forecasting capabilities. The ability of the model to address questions such as "What is the probability that the flooding level in an area A will be above d units within the next T hours?" shows the real application of the model.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Strong Performance:** The experimental results convincingly demonstrate the superiority of DIFF-FLOOD over several baseline methods, including state-of-the-art diffusion-based models for spatiotemporal forecasting (DiffSTG).
    *   **Scalability:** The paper demonstrates that DIFF-FLOOD scales better to large, high-resolution datasets than competing methods, which is crucial for practical applications.
    *   **Ablation Study:** The ablation study effectively isolates the contributions of different components (elevation data, temporal covariates) to the overall performance of the model.
    *   **Practical Applicability:**  The discussion of inundation scenario queries and their relevance to policymakers demonstrates the potential for real-world impact.
    *   **Clarity and Completeness:** The paper is well-written and provides sufficient details about the model architecture, training procedure, and experimental setup.
*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Dataset Limitations:**  While the Tidewatch dataset is a good choice, it has its own limitations. Further evaluation on diverse datasets from different coastal regions with varying environmental conditions would strengthen the generalizability of the findings.
    *   **Complexity:** Diffusion models are computationally expensive to train. The paper could benefit from a more in-depth discussion of the computational resources required and strategies for optimizing training time.
    *   **Comparison Details**: It could be helpful to know more details of parameters of comparison of different model training details.

*   **Potential Influence:** This paper has the potential to influence the field by demonstrating the effectiveness of diffusion models for coastal inundation forecasting. It could spur further research into applying these models to other environmental forecasting problems and developing more efficient diffusion model architectures for spatiotemporal data. The model and its approach could be used for several real world decision-making.

**Justification of Score:**

The paper presents a novel and significant contribution to the field of coastal inundation forecasting. The DIFF-FLOOD model demonstrably outperforms existing methods in both prediction accuracy and scalability, addressing a crucial practical challenge. The inclusion of the ability to answer scenario based queries adds to the usefulness of the model. While there are limitations regarding dataset diversity and computational cost, the paper is well-executed and the results are compelling.

### PillarMamba: Learning Local-Global Context for Roadside Point Cloud via Hybrid State Space Model
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.05397v1)
- **Authors**: Zhang Zhang, Chao Sun, Chao Yue, Da Wen, Tianze Wang, Jianghao Leng
- **Abstract**: Serving the Intelligent Transport System (ITS) and Vehicle-to-Everything (V2X) tasks, roadside perception has received increasing attention in recent years, as it can extend the perception range of connected vehicles and improve traffic safety. However, roadside point cloud oriented 3D object detection has not been effectively explored. To some extent, the key to the performance of a point cloud detector lies in the receptive field of the network and the ability to effectively utilize the scene context. The recent emergence of Mamba, based on State Space Model (SSM), has shaken up the traditional convolution and transformers that have long been the foundational building blocks, due to its efficient global receptive field. In this work, we introduce Mamba to pillar-based roadside point cloud perception and propose a framework based on Cross-stage State-space Group (CSG), called PillarMamba. It enhances the expressiveness of the network and achieves efficient computation through cross-stage feature fusion. However, due to the limitations of scan directions, state space model faces local connection disrupted and historical relationship forgotten. To address this, we propose the Hybrid State-space Block (HSB) to obtain the local-global context of roadside point cloud. Specifically, it enhances neighborhood connections through local convolution and preserves historical memory through residual attention. The proposed method outperforms the state-of-the-art methods on the popular large scale roadside benchmark: DAIR-V2X-I. The code will be released soon.
- **Summary**: Okay, I will provide a summary, critical evaluation, and novelty assessment, including a score for the paper.

**Summary:**

The paper "PillarMamba: Learning Local-Global Context for Roadside Point Cloud via Hybrid State Space Model" introduces a new architecture for 3D object detection using roadside point cloud data. The core idea is to leverage the Mamba (State Space Model) architecture known for its efficient global receptive field, addressing challenges unique to roadside point cloud data like dense context and different sensor perspective relative to vehicle-side detectors. The paper proposes a Cross-stage State-space Group (CSG) to reduce computational burden and a Hybrid State-space Block (HSB) to maintain local context and historical information, which could be lost when using SSM. Experiments on the DAIR-V2X-I dataset show improved performance compared to state-of-the-art methods.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Addressing a relevant problem:** Roadside point cloud perception is an increasingly important area for ITS and V2X, and the paper tackles the specific challenges it presents.
    *   **Novel Architectural Integration:** The paper's innovative use of Mamba architecture (specifically addressing its weaknesses in the context of point clouds) is well-motivated and forms the central technical contribution. Addressing the limitations of simply adapting vehicle-side methods to roadside scenarios is also a plus.
    *   **Technically Sound:** The proposed CSG and HSB modules seem well-designed to tackle the computational cost and local context preservation issues, respectively.
    *   **Solid Experimental Results:** Improvement demonstrated on DAIR-V2X-I.
    *   **Code Release:** Mentioning code release fosters reproducibility and adoption by the community.
*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Incremental Improvement:** The reported performance improvement may be incremental, and it's important to consider if it justifies the complexity of the proposed architecture. A more thorough ablation study is required to clearly showcase the individual contributions of HSB and CSG.
    *   **Limited Ablation:** The paper could have benefited from a more detailed component-wise ablation study showing the contribution of each element in HSB to overall performance to show the importance of each module.
    *   **Lack of Deeper Analysis:** Deeper insights could be provided that are specific to roadside point clouds. It should explain *why* Mamba, CSG and HSB components are better suited for roadside datasets.
    *   **Limited Latency/FPS discussion:** Latency discussion has to provide more context on practical real-world impact, especially considering the real-time nature of ITS tasks.

*   **Novelty:** The use of Mamba in a point cloud 3D object detection context is novel.  The specific adaptations (CSG and HSB) to tailor Mamba for roadside point clouds adds to this novelty.

*   **Significance:** The paper's significance lies in its potential to advance the field of roadside perception, which is crucial for future intelligent transportation systems. However, the actual impact hinges on whether the performance improvements justify the added complexity and on whether the released code is easily adopted and reproduced by other researchers.

**Justification for the Score:**

I am assigning a score of 7.

The integration of Mamba with point cloud data processing is a good starting point, addressing a critical issue in ITS. The adaptations to make Mamba suitable for this specific sensor setup (CSG and HSB) also reflect a good technical contribution. The experimental results, while positive, are not earth-shattering, and a deeper analysis that shows how each module specifically solves the issues is needed. Also, more discussion on computational complexity vs. improvement is also needed.

### Frame In, Frame Out: Do LLMs Generate More Biased News Headlines than Humans?
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.05406v1)
- **Authors**: Valeria Pastorino, Nafise Sadat Moosavi
- **Abstract**: Framing in media critically shapes public perception by selectively emphasizing some details while downplaying others. With the rise of large language models in automated news and content creation, there is growing concern that these systems may introduce or even amplify framing biases compared to human authors. In this paper, we explore how framing manifests in both out-of-the-box and fine-tuned LLM-generated news content. Our analysis reveals that, particularly in politically and socially sensitive contexts, LLMs tend to exhibit more pronounced framing than their human counterparts. In addition, we observe significant variation in framing tendencies across different model architectures, with some models displaying notably higher biases. These findings point to the need for effective post-training mitigation strategies and tighter evaluation frameworks to ensure that automated news content upholds the standards of balanced reporting.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

The paper investigates whether Large Language Models (LLMs) generate more biased news headlines (framed content) compared to humans. The authors perform a comparative analysis across various LLM families (both out-of-the-box and fine-tuned) using the XSUM dataset, detecting framing through the same method in Pastorino et al. (2024). The key findings are: LLMs generally frame information more than humans, particularly in politically and socially sensitive contexts; Fine-tuning helps reduce framing tendencies; Larger models demonstrate a higher propensity for framing; and framing rates vary depending on the topic and model architecture. They also find a weak correlation between the framing tendency and length of the text.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper addresses a crucial and timely question: the potential for LLMs to amplify or introduce framing biases in news generation. While framing analysis itself is not new, applying it to a diverse set of modern LLMs and comparing them directly to human baselines is a valuable contribution. The finding that LLMs are consistently more prone to framing than humans, even in supposedly neutral topics, is a novel and concerning result. The paper also presents novelty in considering different models and fine-tuning techniques, in addition to exploring framing patterns for various topics.

*   **Significance:** The findings have significant implications for the responsible development and deployment of LLMs in news and content creation. If LLMs inherently tend to frame information more than humans, it raises serious concerns about the potential for these models to shape public opinion in unintended or even harmful ways. This has direct relevance to AI ethics, journalism, and media studies. The research provides empirical evidence to support the growing concern about bias in AI-generated content, encouraging further investigation into mitigation strategies. It also pushes the evaluation metrics of LLMs beyond just accuracy and fluency and brings attention to fairness and bias.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Comprehensive Analysis:** The paper analyzes a wide range of LLM architectures, including both out-of-the-box and fine-tuned models.
    *   **Clear Methodology:** The methodology is well-defined, building upon existing techniques for framing detection (Pastorino et al., 2024). It makes the choice of prompting scheme very clear.
    *   **Empirical Evidence:** The paper provides empirical evidence to support its claims, using statistical tests (t-tests, Pearson correlation) to validate the significance of its findings.
    *   **Timeliness:** The topic is highly relevant given the increasing adoption of LLMs for content creation.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Dataset Limitations:** The XSUM dataset, while a good benchmark for summarization, focuses on BBC news articles and may not fully represent the breadth of news content or the nuances of different framing techniques used across various media outlets.
    *   **Framing Detection Method:** The study depends on the jury-based framing detection method, in Pastorino et al. (2024). While this is the best performing approach currently, framing detection itself is still an evolving area of research, and improvements in detection methods could impact the results.
    *   **Limited Mitigation Exploration:** While the paper identifies the problem, it does not delve deeply into specific mitigation strategies or techniques to reduce framing bias in LLMs, though the results on fine-tuning offer a starting point.
    *   **Simple Keyword-based Topic Analysis:** More sophisticated topic modeling methods could provide a better understanding of the relationship between topics and framing tendencies.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper is likely to influence the field by raising awareness of the issue of framing bias in LLMs and prompting further research into:
    *   Developing better evaluation metrics that incorporate fairness and bias assessments.
    *   Exploring mitigation techniques to reduce framing bias in LLMs.
    *   Investigating the impact of different training datasets and model architectures on framing tendencies.
    *   Creating more comprehensive and diverse datasets for framing analysis.

**Overall:**

This is a significant contribution to the field. The paper provides compelling evidence that LLMs are more prone to framing than humans, highlighting a critical issue that needs to be addressed. The findings will likely stimulate further research and development in this area, driving the creation of more responsible and unbiased AI systems for content generation. While the analysis could be strengthened with more advanced topic modeling, framing detection, and dataset diversification, the paper's novel insights and clear presentation merit a high score.
