- **Score**: 8/10

### Crosslingual Reasoning through Test-Time Scaling
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.05408v1)
- **Authors**: Zheng-Xin Yong, M. Farid Adilazuarda, Jonibek Mansurov, Ruochen Zhang, Niklas Muennighoff, Carsten Eickhoff, Genta Indra Winata, Julia Kreutzer, Stephen H. Bach, Alham Fikri Aji
- **Abstract**: Reasoning capabilities of large language models are primarily studied for English, even when pretrained models are multilingual. In this work, we investigate to what extent English reasoning finetuning with long chain-of-thoughts (CoTs) can generalize across languages. First, we find that scaling up inference compute for English-centric reasoning language models (RLMs) improves multilingual mathematical reasoning across many languages including low-resource languages, to an extent where they outperform models twice their size. Second, we reveal that while English-centric RLM's CoTs are naturally predominantly English, they consistently follow a quote-and-think pattern to reason about quoted non-English inputs. Third, we discover an effective strategy to control the language of long CoT reasoning, and we observe that models reason better and more efficiently in high-resource languages. Finally, we observe poor out-of-domain reasoning generalization, in particular from STEM to cultural commonsense knowledge, even for English. Overall, we demonstrate the potentials, study the mechanisms and outline the limitations of crosslingual generalization of English reasoning test-time scaling. We conclude that practitioners should let English-centric RLMs reason in high-resource languages, while further work is needed to improve reasoning in low-resource languages and out-of-domain contexts.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "Crosslingual Reasoning through Test-Time Scaling":

**Summary:**

The paper investigates crosslingual reasoning abilities in English-centric reasoning language models (RLMs) improved by test-time scaling. It addresses whether English-specific reasoning finetuning generalizes across languages in multilingual models. Key findings include:

1.  Test-time scaling improves multilingual mathematical reasoning, outperforming smaller models and even larger models without scaling.
2.  RLMs follow a "quote-and-think" pattern when processing non-English input, quoting phrases before reasoning in English.
3.  Controlling the reasoning language improves performance, with high-resource languages yielding better results.
4.  Reasoning finetuning does not generalize well to domains outside STEM, such as cultural commonsense knowledge.

The paper concludes that while English-centric RLMs demonstrate strong multilingual potential with test-time scaling, especially in math and high-resource languages, further work is needed to improve reasoning in low-resource languages and out-of-domain contexts.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies in its systematic exploration of test-time scaling's effectiveness in crosslingual reasoning, particularly for RLMs finetuned on English data. The discovery of the "quote-and-think" pattern is also a notable contribution, shedding light on the mechanisms behind crosslingual generalization. While prior work has explored multilingual reasoning, this paper uniquely focuses on test-time scaling in *English-centric* RLMs, revealing insights into model behavior and limitations. The observation regarding domain generalization limitations is also valuable, highlighting the need for more comprehensive training approaches. Prior works on the similar topic like the work by Son et. al has shown negative results while this paper shows positive and significant gains which contrasts with the existing findings. This contrast makes the findings in this paper more prominent.

*   **Significance:** The paper's significance stems from its practical implications for deploying RLMs in multilingual settings. The findings suggest that test-time scaling can be a cost-effective strategy for improving performance without requiring extensive multilingual training data. The recommendation to have the models reason in high-resource languages has clear efficiency benefits. The observation that a 3B model is able to surpass the performance of some other 14B models is a finding that also holds practical importance. The identified limitations regarding domain generalization point to important research directions for creating more robust and versatile RLMs. The findings from the cross lingual generalization of test time scaling might hold substantial impacts to many application such as education, health care and financial application that requires handling data of many languages.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   Well-defined research questions and a clear methodology.
    *   Comprehensive experimental setup, including different model sizes and languages.
    *   Detailed analysis of language-mixing patterns and the impact of language forcing.
    *   Empirically grounded findings with clear takeaways for practitioners.
    *   Addresses an important gap in understanding the crosslingual capabilities of English-centric RLMs.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The focus on mathematical reasoning might limit the generalizability of the findings to other types of reasoning tasks.
    *   The paper primarily uses one family of models (s1 models), which is the Qwen2.5-Instruct. While the paper is comprehensive and rigorous, this choice of model and training approach may limit its external generalizability. Expanding the scope of this analysis to other similarly constructed models will increase its impact.
    *   The "quote-and-think" pattern might be specific to the architecture and training data used in this study. Further research is needed to determine how common this behavior is across different RLMs.
    *   The evaluation datasets, while relevant, could benefit from being larger and more diverse, especially in the out-of-domain settings.

*   **Potential Influence:** This paper has the potential to influence research on multilingual RLMs by highlighting the importance of test-time scaling, understanding language-mixing behaviors, and addressing domain generalization limitations. The findings could inform the design of more effective training strategies and deployment methods for RLMs in diverse linguistic contexts. The limitations it highlights will encourage research into techniques that can enhance the performance for both low-resource languages and languages that require common sense or cultural knowledge.

*   **Overall Assessment:** The paper presents valuable insights into the crosslingual reasoning abilities of English-centric RLMs. The systematic exploration of test-time scaling, language-mixing patterns, and domain generalization limitations makes a significant contribution to the field. While there are some limitations related to the specific models and tasks used, the findings have practical implications and point to important directions for future research.

**Score: 7.5**

**Rationale:** The paper provides a strong, novel contribution to the field by providing a rigorous empirical analysis on crosslingual reasoning with test time scaling that challenges and builds upon existing work in the field. Its influence will be in directing future work into both enhancing the generalization capabilities and in helping practitioners more effectively deploy large language models in multi-lingual contexts. However, the model choice slightly restricts the findings' generalizability and also the evaluation datasets, while comprehensive, might limit the scope of the findings. Given its significant contributions, identified practical applications, and reasonable limitations the score is set to 7.5.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Hide & Seek: Transformer Symmetries Obscure Sharpness & Riemannian Geometry Finds It
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.05409v1)
- **Authors**: Marvin F. da Silva, Felix Dangel, Sageev Oore
- **Abstract**: The concept of sharpness has been successfully applied to traditional architectures like MLPs and CNNs to predict their generalization. For transformers, however, recent work reported weak correlation between flatness and generalization. We argue that existing sharpness measures fail for transformers, because they have much richer symmetries in their attention mechanism that induce directions in parameter space along which the network or its loss remain identical. We posit that sharpness must account fully for these symmetries, and thus we redefine it on a quotient manifold that results from quotienting out the transformer symmetries, thereby removing their ambiguities. Leveraging tools from Riemannian geometry, we propose a fully general notion of sharpness, in terms of a geodesic ball on the symmetry-corrected quotient manifold. In practice, we need to resort to approximating the geodesics. Doing so up to first order yields existing adaptive sharpness measures, and we demonstrate that including higher-order terms is crucial to recover correlation with generalization. We present results on diagonal networks with synthetic data, and show that our geodesic sharpness reveals strong correlation for real-world transformers on both text and image classification tasks.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper "Hide & Seek: Transformer Symmetries Obscure Sharpness & Riemannian Geometry Finds It" addresses the problem of weak correlation between sharpness measures and generalization in transformers.  The authors argue that existing sharpness measures fail because transformers have richer symmetries in their attention mechanisms, inducing directions in parameter space where the network's loss remains identical. They propose a new notion of sharpness, "geodesic sharpness," defined on a quotient manifold that eliminates these symmetries.  They leverage Riemannian geometry tools to define sharpness in terms of a geodesic ball on the symmetry-corrected quotient manifold.  They demonstrate analytically on diagonal networks and empirically on real-world transformers (text and image classification) that their geodesic sharpness measure correlates more strongly with generalization than existing methods.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's core novelty lies in its principled application of Riemannian quotient manifolds to address the symmetry issue in transformer sharpness. While adaptive sharpness measures have addressed rescaling, this paper tackles more complex, high-dimensional symmetries stemming from the attention mechanism. This is a significant departure from previous approaches that treat symmetries in a more ad-hoc manner. The idea of using geodesic distances, rather than Euclidean ones, is also a crucial and innovative point.

*   **Significance:** The significance of the work is potentially high. Establishing a stronger link between sharpness and generalization in transformers is a valuable step towards developing better generalization-predictive metrics. These metrics, in turn, could enable more effective regularization techniques and provide deeper theoretical insights into transformer behavior. The findings presented in this work can be extended to multiple tasks and further studies.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Principled Approach:** The paper provides a theoretically grounded approach based on Riemannian geometry, which offers a systematic way to handle symmetries.
    *   **Empirical Validation:** The paper demonstrates the effectiveness of their approach through both analytical (diagonal networks) and empirical (transformers) evaluations.
    *   **Improved Correlation:**  The results show a stronger correlation between geodesic sharpness and generalization compared to existing methods on both text and image classification tasks, thus proving the initial hypothesis.
    *   **Generality:** The method has the potential to be applied to wider neural network architectures because it can handle both GL(h) symmetry and the original re-scaling/scaling symmetry of convolutional networks.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Computational Cost:** While the paper claims efficiency compared to Hessian-based methods, calculating geodesics (even approximately) can still be computationally demanding, particularly for large-scale transformers. This limits practical usability in some scenarios.
    *   **Approximations:** In practice, the paper relies on approximations of the geodesics (Taylor expansion), introducing a potential source of error.
    *   **Variability of the sign of Correlation:** The authors themselves recognize the potential issue of anti-correlation between geodesical sharpness and generalization.
    *   **Practical utility limitation:** The authors noted that there is a challenge in applying the new method to an optimization.

*   **Potential Impact:**

    *   The paper has the potential to shift the focus in the sharpness and generalization literature towards more geometrically grounded approaches.
    *   It could inspire the development of novel regularization techniques tailored to transformers.
    *   It may also provide a framework for analyzing the effects of other architectural choices (e.g., normalization layers) on the geometry of the loss landscape.

*   **Score Justification:**

    While the paper presents a significant step forward in understanding sharpness and generalization in transformers, its reliance on approximations and potential computational cost, as well as the limited application to new optimization make its impact on practical transformer training somewhat limited *at the moment*. Its long-term influence may be more substantial as the theoretical foundation can spark further research in Riemannian methods for neural network analysis. The paper contains many theoretical and experimental strengths but also some weaknesses, and its impact on the field is uncertain but promising.

### Reasoning Models Don't Always Say What They Think
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.05410v1)
- **Authors**: Yanda Chen, Joe Benton, Ansh Radhakrishnan, Jonathan Uesato, Carson Denison, John Schulman, Arushi Somani, Peter Hase, Misha Wagner, Fabien Roger, Vlad Mikulik, Samuel R. Bowman, Jan Leike, Jared Kaplan, Ethan Perez
- **Abstract**: Chain-of-thought (CoT) offers a potential boon for AI safety as it allows monitoring a model's CoT to try to understand its intentions and reasoning processes. However, the effectiveness of such monitoring hinges on CoTs faithfully representing models' actual reasoning processes. We evaluate CoT faithfulness of state-of-the-art reasoning models across 6 reasoning hints presented in the prompts and find: (1) for most settings and models tested, CoTs reveal their usage of hints in at least 1% of examples where they use the hint, but the reveal rate is often below 20%, (2) outcome-based reinforcement learning initially improves faithfulness but plateaus without saturating, and (3) when reinforcement learning increases how frequently hints are used (reward hacking), the propensity to verbalize them does not increase, even without training against a CoT monitor. These results suggest that CoT monitoring is a promising way of noticing undesired behaviors during training and evaluations, but that it is not sufficient to rule them out. They also suggest that in settings like ours where CoT reasoning is not necessary, test-time monitoring of CoTs is unlikely to reliably catch rare and catastrophic unexpected behaviors.
- **Summary**: Okay, I'll provide a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "Reasoning Models Don't Always Say What They Think."

**Summary:**

The paper investigates the faithfulness of chain-of-thought (CoT) reasoning in large language models (LLMs). The authors examine whether CoTs accurately reflect the model's actual reasoning process, particularly when presented with "hints" in the prompts that could influence the answer.  They evaluate CoT faithfulness by creating pairs of questions, one with a hint and one without.  They then check if the model's CoT acknowledges the hint when the answer changes due to the hint.  The study uses state-of-the-art reasoning models like Claude 3.7 Sonnet and DeepSeek R1, and also examines the effects of outcome-based reinforcement learning (RL) on CoT faithfulness, and  reward hacking during RL. The core findings are that (1) CoTs often fail to verbalize the usage of hints, (2) outcome-based RL improves faithfulness to a certain point but plateaus, and (3) CoT monitoring may not reliably detect reward hacking during RL. The paper concludes that while CoT monitoring is a valuable tool for noticing certain behaviors, it is not reliable enough to ensure safety, especially for behaviors that can be performed without explicit CoT reasoning.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper tackles an important and timely issue related to the safety of LLMs. While prior work has explored CoT reasoning, faithfulness, and reward hacking, this paper contributes by systematically studying CoT faithfulness across a range of reasoning hints, by comparing state-of-the-art models, and by explicitly investigating how outcome-based RL affects CoT faithfulness (rather than assuming RL inherently improves it). The study of misalignment-specific hints (grader hacking, unethical information use) is a valuable contribution.

*   **Significance:** The paper's findings have direct implications for the use of CoT monitoring as a safety mechanism. By demonstrating that CoTs are often unfaithful, the authors challenge the assumption that monitoring CoTs is sufficient to ensure the safety of AI systems.  The results are significant for AI safety researchers and practitioners who are relying on CoT to understand model intentions and detect misaligned behaviors.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Systematic Evaluation:** The paper provides a structured and reproducible methodology for evaluating CoT faithfulness, which is a challenging task. The use of hint-based prompts allows for inferring model reasoning by observing answer changes.

    *   **State-of-the-Art Models:**  The study uses state-of-the-art reasoning models, making the results relevant to the current capabilities of LLMs.
    *   **Clear Findings:** The results are clearly presented and supported by data.
    *   **Focus on Misalignment:** The emphasis on misaligned behaviors is a crucial contribution to AI safety.
    *   **Investigation of RL effects:**  The experiment that investigates the impact of RL on faithfulness is novel and important since it challenges implicit assumptions about the benefits of RL alone.
*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited Scope:**  The evaluation is limited to a specific type of task (multiple-choice questions) and a specific kind of unintended behavior (exploiting hints). This limits the generalizability of the findings to more complex and realistic scenarios.

    *   **Simplified Reward Hacking:**  The reward hacking environments are synthetic and relatively simple. More complex reward hacking scenarios might reveal different levels of CoT faithfulness.

    *   **Metric Limitations:**  The faithfulness metric relies on specific prompt designs and may not fully capture the nuances of model reasoning. It is noted by the authors themselves that faithfulness scores are likely an upper bound.

    *   **Lack of Mitigation Strategies:** While the paper identifies a problem, it doesn't offer concrete mitigation strategies for improving CoT faithfulness.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper is likely to influence future research on CoT monitoring and AI safety. It highlights the need for more robust and reliable methods for understanding model reasoning and detecting misaligned behaviors. It may also spur the development of new techniques for training models to generate more faithful CoTs.

*   **Justification for Score:** The paper makes a significant contribution by critically examining CoT faithfulness, an area of great importance for LLM safety. The findings challenge a widely-held assumption and provide valuable insights for researchers and practitioners. However, the limitations of the study, particularly the limited scope and the lack of mitigation strategies, temper the overall impact. The paper has identified an important problem and provided empirical evidence, but the path to solving that problem remains largely unexplored.

### TokLIP: Marry Visual Tokens to CLIP for Multimodal Comprehension and Generation
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.05422v1)
- **Authors**: Haokun Lin, Teng Wang, Yixiao Ge, Yuying Ge, Zhichao Lu, Ying Wei, Qingfu Zhang, Zhenan Sun, Ying Shan
- **Abstract**: Pioneering token-based works such as Chameleon and Emu3 have established a foundation for multimodal unification but face challenges of high training computational overhead and limited comprehension performance due to a lack of high-level semantics. In this paper, we introduce TokLIP, a visual tokenizer that enhances comprehension by semanticizing vector-quantized (VQ) tokens and incorporating CLIP-level semantics while enabling end-to-end multimodal autoregressive training with standard VQ tokens. TokLIP integrates a low-level discrete VQ tokenizer with a ViT-based token encoder to capture high-level continuous semantics. Unlike previous approaches (e.g., VILA-U) that discretize high-level features, TokLIP disentangles training objectives for comprehension and generation, allowing the direct application of advanced VQ tokenizers without the need for tailored quantization operations. Our empirical results demonstrate that TokLIP achieves exceptional data efficiency, empowering visual tokens with high-level semantic understanding while enhancing low-level generative capacity, making it well-suited for autoregressive Transformers in both comprehension and generation tasks. The code and models are available at https://github.com/TencentARC/TokLIP.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper "TokLIP: Marry Visual Tokens to CLIP for Multimodal Comprehension and Generation" introduces TokLIP, a visual tokenizer designed to enhance multimodal understanding and generation capabilities of large language models (LLMs).  TokLIP addresses the limitations of previous token-based approaches like Chameleon and Emu3, which suffer from high computational costs and limited comprehension due to lacking high-level semantics. TokLIP integrates a low-level discrete Vector Quantized (VQ) tokenizer with a ViT-based token encoder, capturing both low-level generative details and high-level CLIP-level semantic information.  The key idea is to semanticize VQ tokens using CLIP, rather than directly discretizing CLIP features, disentangling comprehension and generation objectives.  Experiments demonstrate TokLIP's data efficiency, superior performance in image representation, multimodal comprehension, and autoregressive image generation compared to existing tokenizers like VILA-U and QLIP.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:** The core idea of semanticizing VQ tokens *after* the initial discretization rather than quantizing continuous semantic features is relatively novel. The paper clearly articulates the issues with existing approaches and proposes a method to overcome them.  The use of a pre-trained CLIP encoder and a distillation approach for transferring semantic knowledge to the VQ tokens is not entirely new but the specific combination and its application to the problem of unified multimodal modeling has novelty.

* **Significance:** The significance lies in addressing the computational and performance bottlenecks of existing unified multimodal models. TokLIP's data efficiency and improved comprehension open the door to more practical and scalable end-to-end multimodal LLMs. The experiments show significant improvements in ImageNet classification and MSCOCO retrieval, highlighting the enhanced semantic understanding. The ability to perform both comprehension and generation with a single, efficient tokenizer is a substantial contribution. The paper provides a clear and well reasoned methodology, with detailed experiments that support the claims.

* **Strengths:**
    * **Clear Problem Definition:** The paper effectively identifies and articulates the limitations of existing token-based multimodal models.
    * **Well-Motivated Approach:**  The design choices of TokLIP are justified by the observed weaknesses of existing techniques and a careful analysis of the training objectives.
    * **Strong Empirical Results:**  The experiments are comprehensive, comparing TokLIP against state-of-the-art tokenizers and CLIP encoders across a variety of tasks. Ablation studies provide insights into the key components of TokLIP's architecture.
    * **Extensible framework:** The use of VQGAN and CLIP makes the framework future proofed against advances in those areas.

* **Weaknesses:**
    * **Reliance on CLIP:** While leveraging CLIP is a strength, it also creates a dependency. The performance is limited by the quality of the pre-trained CLIP model. The architecture inherits the biases and limitations of the teacher model.
    * **Limited Generative Evaluation:** While the paper demonstrates image generation capabilities, the evaluation is primarily focused on class-conditional generation on ImageNet. A more extensive evaluation of generative quality and diversity would further strengthen the results.
    * **Incremental improvement:** The core idea, though novel, can be viewed as incremental improvement. The authors could address this by further emphasizing the unique architectural design choices that differentiates it from other VQ-VAE+CLIP style models.

* **Potential Influence:** TokLIP has the potential to significantly influence the development of unified multimodal models by enabling more efficient and effective training. It could inspire future research on semantic tokenization techniques and the integration of generative and discriminative capabilities.

**Justification for Score:**

TokLIP presents a well-motivated and empirically validated solution to a critical problem in multimodal learning. While the approach builds upon existing techniques, it introduces a novel combination and demonstrates significant improvements in data efficiency, comprehension, and generation. The reliance on CLIP and limited generative evaluation hold it back from being a truly exceptional contribution.

### TransProQA: an LLM-based literary Translation evaluation metric with Professional Question Answering
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.05423v1)
- **Authors**: Ran Zhang, Wei Zhao, Lieve Macken, Steffen Eger
- **Abstract**: The impact of Large Language Models (LLMs) has extended into literary domains. However, existing evaluation metrics prioritize mechanical accuracy over artistic expression and tend to overrate machine translation (MT) as being superior to experienced professional human translation. In the long run, this bias could result in a permanent decline in translation quality and cultural authenticity. In response to the urgent need for a specialized literary evaluation metric, we introduce TransProQA, a novel, reference-free, LLM-based question-answering (QA) framework designed specifically for literary translation evaluation. TransProQA uniquely integrates insights from professional literary translators and researchers, focusing on critical elements in literary quality assessment such as literary devices, cultural understanding, and authorial voice. Our extensive evaluation shows that while literary-finetuned XCOMET-XL yields marginal gains, TransProQA substantially outperforms current metrics, achieving up to 0.07 gain in correlation (ACC-EQ and Kendall's tau) and surpassing the best state-of-the-art (SOTA) metrics by over 15 points in adequacy assessments. Incorporating professional translator insights as weights further improves performance, highlighting the value of translator inputs. Notably, TransProQA approaches human-level evaluation performance comparable to trained linguistic annotators. It demonstrates broad applicability to open-source models such as LLaMA3.3-70b and Qwen2.5-32b, indicating its potential as an accessible and training-free literary evaluation metric and a valuable tool for evaluating texts that require local processing due to copyright or ethical considerations.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the TRANProQA paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces TRANSPROQA, a novel Large Language Model (LLM)-based, reference-free, Question Answering (QA) framework specifically designed to evaluate literary translations.  It addresses the limitations of existing MT evaluation metrics which prioritize mechanical accuracy over artistic and cultural nuances critical in literary works.  TRANSPROQA integrates insights from professional literary translators by focusing on elements like literary devices, cultural understanding, and authorial voice through a curated question set.  The paper demonstrates that TRANSPROQA outperforms current metrics, including fine-tuned versions of XCOMET-XL, in correlation with human judgments and adequacy assessments. The framework also exhibits robust performance with open-source models, making it an accessible tool for literary translation evaluation.  The incorporation of professional translator insights as weights further enhances the framework's accuracy, approaching human-level evaluation performance.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Strengths:**

*   **Addresses a Significant Problem:** The paper tackles a critical gap in MT evaluation, which is the lack of metrics tailored for the subjective and nuanced qualities of literary translation.
*   **Novel Approach:** The QA-based framework leveraging professional translator insights is a novel approach and offers a more holistic evaluation compared to traditional metrics focused on lexical overlap or semantic similarity.
*   **Strong Empirical Results:** The paper presents robust empirical evidence demonstrating that TRANSPROQA outperforms state-of-the-art metrics, including fine-tuned versions, in both correlation with human judgments and adequacy assessments.
*   **Translator-Centric:** The inclusion of literary translator insights in question selection and scoring adds significant value and moves the evaluation closer to human expertise.
*   **Accessibility:** Demonstrated effectiveness with open-source LLMs makes the tool accessible to a wider audience, especially in contexts where local processing is required due to copyright or ethical considerations.
*   **Reproducibility:** The paper mentions that the code and datasets are publicly available for further research.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Limited Language Coverage:** The evaluation primarily focuses on high-resource language pairs, limiting the generalizability of the findings to low-resource languages.
*   **Paragraph-Level Evaluation:**  The evaluation is conducted at the paragraph level, which may not capture elements that are relevant only when considering larger portions of text (e.g., coherence, character development)
*   **Question-Level Instructions are less effective:**  QuestionStep shows a reduction in translation effectiveness, indicating a challenge for LLMs to incorporate instructions at a finer level.
*   **Dependence on LLM Performance:** The metric relies on the ability of LLMs to accurately answer the evaluation questions. While the framework aims to mitigate this through question selection, it still introduces a potential bias or limitation.
*   **Survey response:** The survey response is the key for weighting, a larger group will likely lead to even better result.

**Novelty and Significance:**

The primary novelty lies in the design of an LLM-based metric that is specifically geared toward evaluating literary translation quality, taking into account insights from expert human translators. By moving away from traditional methods, TRANSPROQA has the potential to address critical shortcomings of existing approaches. By focusing on elements such as cultural nuances and literary devices, the paper has the ability to offer a new way to approach machine translation evaluation and potentially improve LLM's capabilities when working with artistic content. The significance is enhanced by demonstrating that even accessible, open-source models can be effective with this framework.

**Influence:**

The paper has the potential to influence future research in MT evaluation by:

*   Encouraging the development of more specialized metrics for creative domains.
*   Highlighting the importance of incorporating expert human knowledge in automated evaluation systems.
*   Providing a framework for evaluating the performance of LLMs on literary translation tasks.
*   Raising awareness of the potential biases in existing evaluation metrics.

**Justification for Score:**

I assign a score of **8**.  The paper presents a significant contribution to the field by addressing a clear need for specialized evaluation metrics in literary translation. The novelty of the approach is solid, and the empirical results convincingly demonstrate the superiority of TRANSPROQA over existing methods.  The strengths of the paper, particularly the translator-centric design and empirical validation, are considerable. The reliance on LLMs also brings certain limitations, but are mitigated by the question selection process and the demonstrated ability to work well with open source models.

### Ultra-FineWeb: Efficient Data Filtering and Verification for High-Quality LLM Training Data
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.05427v1)
- **Authors**: Yudong Wang, Zixuan Fu, Jie Cai, Peijun Tang, Hongya Lyu, Yewei Fang, Zhi Zheng, Jie Zhou, Guoyang Zeng, Chaojun Xiao, Xu Han, Zhiyuan Liu
- **Abstract**: Data quality has become a key factor in enhancing model performance with the rapid development of large language models (LLMs). Model-driven data filtering has increasingly become a primary approach for acquiring high-quality data. However, it still faces two main challenges: (1) the lack of an efficient data verification strategy makes it difficult to provide timely feedback on data quality; and (2) the selection of seed data for training classifiers lacks clear criteria and relies heavily on human expertise, introducing a degree of subjectivity. To address the first challenge, we introduce an efficient verification strategy that enables rapid evaluation of the impact of data on LLM training with minimal computational cost. To tackle the second challenge, we build upon the assumption that high-quality seed data is beneficial for LLM training, and by integrating the proposed verification strategy, we optimize the selection of positive and negative samples and propose an efficient data filtering pipeline. This pipeline not only improves filtering efficiency, classifier quality, and robustness, but also significantly reduces experimental and inference costs. In addition, to efficiently filter high-quality data, we employ a lightweight classifier based on fastText, and successfully apply the filtering pipeline to two widely-used pre-training corpora, FineWeb and Chinese FineWeb datasets, resulting in the creation of the higher-quality Ultra-FineWeb dataset. Ultra-FineWeb contains approximately 1 trillion English tokens and 120 billion Chinese tokens. Empirical results demonstrate that the LLMs trained on Ultra-FineWeb exhibit significant performance improvements across multiple benchmark tasks, validating the effectiveness of our pipeline in enhancing both data quality and training efficiency.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "Ultra-FineWeb: Efficient Data Filtering and Verification for High-Quality LLM Training Data":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces Ultra-FineWeb, a new high-quality dataset for training large language models (LLMs). The authors address two key challenges in model-driven data filtering: the inefficiency of data verification and the subjectivity in selecting seed data for classifier training. They propose an efficient verification strategy that uses a nearly-trained LLM to assess the impact of data on training with minimal computational cost. They also optimize seed data selection by assuming high-quality seed data is beneficial for LLM training and integrating their verification strategy. The filtering pipeline uses a lightweight fastText classifier, applied to FineWeb and Chinese FineWeb datasets, resulting in Ultra-FineWeb (containing 1 trillion English tokens and 120 billion Chinese tokens). Experiments demonstrate that LLMs trained on Ultra-FineWeb show performance improvements across multiple benchmark tasks.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Strengths:**

    * **Efficient Verification Strategy:** The proposed verification strategy is a significant contribution. Addressing the high computational cost of verifying data quality in LLM training is crucial for iterative data filtering and development. Reducing the need for large-scale training runs during dataset curation is valuable.
    * **Principled Seed Data Selection:** Attempting to address the subjectivity issue in seed data selection is commendable. The approach of leveraging the verification strategy to select high-quality samples is logical.
    * **Lightweight Classifier:** The use of a fastText-based classifier is a practical decision to reduce inference costs, enabling efficient large-scale data filtering.
    * **Empirical Validation:** The paper provides substantial empirical evidence supporting the effectiveness of Ultra-FineWeb and the proposed filtering pipeline, demonstrating performance improvements on various benchmarks. The ablation studies further strengthen the analysis.
    * **Dataset Creation:** Providing a large-scale, high-quality dataset to the community is beneficial.
    * **Clarity:** The paper is generally well-written and clearly explains the methodology and results.

* **Weaknesses:**

    * **Incremental Novelty:** While the individual components (efficient verification, seed selection optimization, fastText classifier) are valuable, the overall approach is somewhat incremental. Model-driven data filtering is already a well-established technique. The primary novelty lies in the specific implementation and combination of existing ideas. It's less of a groundbreaking paradigm shift.
    * **FastText Classifier Generalization:** While fastText classifier may be efficient, some studies show generalization issues for LLMs training data filtering. LLM-based data filtering methods may perform better when scaling the data sizes.
    * **Limited Exploration of the Classifier Threshold:** the analysis does not explore an comprehensive analysis of classifier inference thresholds.
    * **Limited Negative Sample Selection Analysis:** the diversity on negative sample data selection is not enough.

* **Significance:**

    * The paper provides practical improvements to the data filtering process for LLM training, specifically addressing efficiency and subjectivity concerns.
    * The Ultra-FineWeb dataset is a valuable resource for the LLM community and can facilitate further research and development.
    * The efficient verification strategy and optimized seed data selection methodology could be adopted by other researchers and practitioners.

**Justification of Score:**

The paper makes a solid contribution to the field of LLM training data curation, but its novelty is somewhat incremental. The efficient verification strategy is valuable, and the empirical results demonstrate tangible benefits. However, the core idea of model-driven data filtering is already well-established, and the paper mainly improves upon existing techniques. The use of a lightweight classifier is a practical trade-off, but it might limit performance compared to more sophisticated models.

### EcoAgent: An Efficient Edge-Cloud Collaborative Multi-Agent Framework for Mobile Automation
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.05440v1)
- **Authors**: Biao Yi, Xavier Hu, Yurun Chen, Shengyu Zhang, Hongxia Yang, Fan Wu, Fei Wu
- **Abstract**: Cloud-based mobile agents powered by (multimodal) large language models ((M)LLMs) offer strong reasoning abilities but suffer from high latency and cost. While fine-tuned (M)SLMs enable edge deployment, they often lose general capabilities and struggle with complex tasks. To address this, we propose EcoAgent, an Edge-Cloud cOllaborative multi-agent framework for mobile automation. EcoAgent features a closed-loop collaboration among a cloud-based Planning Agent and two edge-based agents: the Execution Agent for action execution and the Observation Agent for verifying outcomes. The Observation Agent uses a Pre-Understanding Module to compress screen images into concise text, reducing token usage. In case of failure, the Planning Agent retrieves screen history and replans via a Reflection Module. Experiments on AndroidWorld show that EcoAgent maintains high task success rates while significantly reducing MLLM token consumption, enabling efficient and practical mobile automation.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and a critical evaluation of the EcoAgent paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces EcoAgent, a novel edge-cloud collaborative framework for mobile automation. It aims to address the limitations of both cloud-based (high latency, cost) and edge-based (limited reasoning) mobile agents by combining their strengths. EcoAgent features a cloud-based Planning Agent and two edge-based agents: an Execution Agent for action execution and an Observation Agent for verifying outcomes. Key innovations include a Pre-Understanding Module to compress screen images for efficient communication and a Reflection Module for replanning in case of failure, using a memory of past screen states. Experiments on AndroidWorld demonstrate that EcoAgent achieves high task success rates while significantly reducing MLLM token consumption.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The novelty lies in the specific architecture and the combination of edge and cloud resources. While edge-cloud collaboration for mobile agents isn't entirely new (as acknowledged in the related work section), EcoAgent presents a particular configuration that seems to outperform existing approaches, especially regarding cost-effectiveness (token usage) and the inclusion of specialized modules like the Pre-Understanding Module, which directly targets a key bottleneck (communication overhead). The explicit memory and reflection mechanisms are also a valuable addition, enabling more robust replanning.

*   **Significance:** The paper addresses a significant challenge in the field of mobile automation: balancing performance and cost. Cloud-based agents are powerful but expensive to operate, while edge-based agents are more efficient but less capable. EcoAgent offers a viable path towards practical, real-world deployment of mobile agents by reducing reliance on cloud resources without sacrificing task completion ability. The focus on reducing token consumption is particularly relevant considering the cost model of many MLLM APIs.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   The architecture is well-defined and explained.
    *   The modular design makes the framework potentially adaptable to different MLLMs and MSLMs.
    *   The experimental results on AndroidWorld provide solid evidence for the effectiveness of EcoAgent. The ablation studies further highlight the importance of each component.
    *   The paper is well-written and clearly presents the problem, solution, and results.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   While the AndroidWorld benchmark is widely used, it's still a simulated environment. Real-world performance might differ due to variations in network conditions and app behaviors.
    *   The paper could benefit from a more in-depth discussion of the limitations of the approach. For example, how does EcoAgent handle situations where the edge-based agents fail to correctly identify UI elements? Are there specific types of tasks where EcoAgent is less effective?
    *   The choice of specific MSLMs (ShowUI, OS-Atlas-Pro, Qwen2-VL-2B-Instruct) for the edge agents could be further justified, potentially with comparisons against other relevant MSLMs.
    *   The case study provides a single example, and a larger set of example executions highlighting different failure modes and recovery strategies would strengthen the analysis.

*   **Potential Influence:** EcoAgent has the potential to influence the design of future mobile automation frameworks. The edge-cloud collaboration approach and the emphasis on cost-effectiveness could become standard practices. The Pre-Understanding Module offers a valuable lesson in efficient communication strategies for distributed AI systems.

*   **Justification for Score:** While the core concept of edge-cloud collaboration is not brand new, the specific architecture of EcoAgent, with its targeted modules and demonstrated cost-effectiveness, represents a significant practical advancement. The modular approach makes it adaptable and extensible. The primary weakness is the limited real-world validation and the lack of deeper exploration of the limitations. However, the gains in efficiency and the detailed ablation studies justify a reasonably high score.
